American artist who made wall drawings and later reliefs, which consisted of painted cut outs arranged in an abstract assemblage on a panel fixed to the wall.
! Lucio Fontana.
Italian artist working in the 1950s and 60s, Fontana’s paintings are abstract, plain flat coloured canvases with slits cut in them and sometimes textural detail added, such as painted gravel. He uses a range of colours from dark reds and blues to bright copper green. Sometimes his paintings are made on egg shaped canvases.
!Heilman Mary.
Abstract painter born in the 1940s, Heilman makes small paintings of carefully considered abstract shapes, lines and forms exploring endless possibilities of form and colour.
!Held, Al.
Twentieth century American painter.
!Hodgkin Howard.
British painter born in the 1940s who makes abstract paintings. Hodgkin uses very bright jewel like colours, applied in large, often round repeated splodges, the paint continuing right off the edge of the canvas onto and round the frame. Hodgkin’s paintings are never larger than two metres high. He sometimes uses round and oval shaped canvases. Hodgkin has made work for public commission including the massively enlarged photomural of brushstrokes that fills the windows of the circular Imax cinema in London.

!Riley Brigit.
Britsih artist born in the 1940s famous for her Op Art black and white paintings made in the 1960s. Riley’s geometric abstract Op Art work endures as one of the best-known images of the 1960s. She made these paintings by drawing and distorting grids and filling them in with black and white. The distortions create illusions of curves, of shimmering and of motion making the paintings hard to look at. Riley used grids of squares, triangles, as well as dots and ellipses and parallel lines. One example, 1m high, has a grid of squares, which gradually decrease in width as they move toward the centre of the painting, creating the illusion of two vertical chequered cylinders. Another painting 1.5 m by 2 m has parallel horizontal wavy lines. Each line is fractionally further to one side of the painting than the next creating an optical shimmer. A large 2m square painting has black elliptical dots placed about 30 cm apart on a grid. The angle of the ellipse changes gradually, giving an impression of motion. A triangle pattern creates waves as each of the triangles on the square canvas is progressively tilted along a curved line.
Riley made a large number of black and white works before beginning to add colour. At first she added one or two colours, for example yellow, red and blue, to the black and white paintings. A horizontal wavy line painting has colour added. 
In the 1980s Riley began to explore the optical colour blending effects she had created, and made the first of a large number of pure colour works. She ceased to use black and white and has never returned to it. She continued to study the work of Cézanne and to refer to his paintings in her geometric abstractions, many of which draw directly on historical painting. Works from this period are mostly a grid of multicoloured parallelograms, the choice of colour influenced by the source image. These images predate but later seemed to anticipate pixellation although they have nothing to do with this.
Riley made many public commissions including applying coloured stripes to the corridors on several floors of a London hospital.
During the late 1990s and early 00s Riley continued to use colour but began to draw much larger shapes. The shapes remain hard edged but she ceases to use a grid. 
The tendencies evident in early twentieth cnetury abstract painting delvoped into two discitnct but complementary movements, abstract expressionism and minimalism.
!!ABSTRACT EXPRESSIONISM.
The critic Clement Greenberg argued for Abtract Expressionism as the only logical way forward for painting. He supported many of its best exponents, including Pollock.
! Jackson Pollock.
The best known of the American abstract expressionist painters and arguably the best-known modernist painter of all time. Pollock was born in the 1920s and died in the early 70s in a car accident. 
Pollock began working in the 1950s and at the end of the decade he began to experiment with pouring paint. Placing the canvas on the floor he began to use the brush to pour house paint from, not touching the canvas at all. This was a radical step, since no one had ever made a painting without touching the canvas and with so little control over the result. Pollock became very skilled in this way of applying paint, using bigger and bigger canvases and more colours.
These works made him a sensation. Nobody had ever seen anything like them. A photographer Hans Namuth made a seminal film of Pollock showing him pouring paint on the canvas, which included a section, filmed from below through glass. It became known as action painting. The paintings are up to 2 metres high and very wide. They are all composed of an all over ‘pattern’ of drips, applied in a dynamic but highly controlled way, so that an overall design is evident. Far from relinquishing control, Pollock became master of the technique. The combinations of colours are subtle, usually involving black and white with one or two other colours. One of the best known is Blue Poles, which has two vertical blue stripes amidst the swirling drips. Another, titled One, is a largely black and white composition.
Pollock made many public commissions and his work was taken up by the American government as being a pinnacle of modernism and representing American freedom. Unwittingly Pollock’s work was used as US propaganda against the Soviet Union, when the CIA covertly funded an international touring exhibition of the work of the abstract expressionists designed to be compared directly and unfavourably with soviet socialist realist art. The abstract expressionists represented freedom of expression at a time when the government wanted to promote American as the land of freedom and thus perpetuate anti-communist sentiment.
Pollock was married to Lee Krasner, also an abstract painter. Krasner saw Pollock through many years of increasing alcoholism and infidelity, taking him to live and work in a house on Long Island to help him stop drinking, until she felt forced to leave him. However she continued to care for him. Krasner subjugated her own career to Pollock’s and it seems reasonable to suppose that he was unlikely to have become successful or even made the action paintings without her support.
!Rothenstein, Susan.
American abstract expressinist painter.

!Kline Franz.
American Kline’s abstract enormous expressionist paintings usually feature large black gestural marks made with a big brush, on a white or even un-primed ground. Much of the ground is left untouched but it becomes an active part of the surface of the painting.
!Krasner Lee.
Krasner was a painter who turned to abstraction. She married Jackson Pollock and found herself nursing him through alcoholism to his death. She established a foundation in their joint names which through its grant making activities contributes to the careers of many artists. 

!Frankenthaler, Helen.
American abstract expressinist painter
!Barnet Newman.
American painter who made abstract paintings from the late 40s to the 60s. Newman’s minimal paintings are known for ‘the zip’, a line of a contrasting colour running vertically down the canvas. Newman invariably used one or two solid colours in blocks or bands, often dark blue, black and maroon, but also golden ochre colours and aquamarine, and the zip (red on a dark brown ground for example) animated these, setting up an optical resonance. He spent his whole career painting these and like Rothko was held up as a spiritual painter. One group of paintings on a single theme fills a whole room; they are white on white paintings with elements of black. 
!Rothko, Mark.
Amerianc painter who is one of the most famour exponents of abstract expressionism. Rothko’s works are simple colourfields, predominatnly one or two shades of the same colour or two contrasting colours, aften arranged as a wide border with a central section and usually portrait format. Althught the colour is solid, it is rouglhy patined with a rough edge so that the colours affect each other optically. Roahtkos paintings are qutie large, up to 3 metres high and he sometimes made four works to shown together. One scuh installation is a dark red group of paintings, rectangles within borders. This installation provokes a spiritaul or contemplative repsonse in many viewers, some of whom say they are moved to tears. These works have come to represent the way in which art can be said to provide a transcendental experience.
Rothko himself reffered to his paintigngs as parts of himself, decribgint them as skins hung on the wall.
Minimalism was an emerging trend in both paiting and sculpture. Minimalist sculputre was gorund breaking in it refusal of any form of modelling, and in its use of prefabircated materials.
!!MINIMALISM.
!Robert Morris.
American Sculptor working from the 1960s to the 1980s who was known for his use of fabricated steel and other metals. Morris made abstract minimal sculptures, which were nearly always cube box shapes, about 1.5 meters high, open at the top. His series of copper plated boxes shown as a group are particularly well known, as well as a similar series made of mirrored glass. The copper boxes emit a glowing pink reflected light from their interiors. These are contradictory works in terms of their representation of a heavy solid for by means of emptiness and light.
Morris also made sculpture with heavy weight felt, including scatter works. He performed in a live work made with Carolee Schneeman in the 1970s title Olympia, in which Schneeman posed as the woman in Manet's eponymous painting, and Morris dressed in a white robe and wearing leather work gloves, carried large plywood boards around, arranging them as a backdrop to Schneeman. Both performers wore white makeup. The piece was about the ‘work’ of art, about the male artist and the female model and about the translation from two to three dimensions, the boards representing ‘planes’. 
!Richard Serra.
American artist who is well known for his monumnetal sculpture in steel, Titled Arc, installed in a public plaze in the 1980s. Tilted Arc was a curviing steel wall which bisected the plaza and was tilted at a step angle along its length. It becmae notorious for the degree of public resistance to it (the suclutpre created a detour makign the walk across the square longer) and for the fact that a worker was killed when a part fo the sculpture fell on him during installation.
Serrra’s work was mostly welded steel sculpture but not always on such a scale. An early video work, Hand Catching Lead, shows the sculptors hand trying repeatedly to catch a piece of falling lead, a difficult task which is rarely successful.
!Donald Judd.
American artist born in the 1940s Donald Judd became one of the most significant minimal sculptors in the world during the 1960s and 70s. He makes steel boxes, shelf life structures, and repeated units, fabricated with a high attention to detail and immaculately spray painted in a variety of colours. Some sculptures are made of polished metal such as brass, so that they are extremely shiny The structures and units are ambiguous, both suggesting function but obviously non-functional. They are either mounted on the wall, in a horizontal line or one above the other, sometimes in ascending sizes, or placed with precision on the floor. He used metal folding machines with virtuosity, and the I Beam is a favourite material.
Judd brought the qualities of steel fabrication and the modular construction of modernism to his sculptures, simple works that often had a monumental quality.
Judd set up a studio and a foundation in Marfa, Texas, where he has a number of assistants working. He is extremely prolific. 
! Carl Andre. 
Born 1948, Andre is a leading artist in a generation of US minimalist sculptors working in the 1970s including Donald Judd and Robert Morris. He is notorious for his ‘pile of bricks’, purchased by the Tate gallery in London in 1980, which was derided in the press for being nothing but a pile of ordinary house bricks. This work, an arrangement of house bricks in a plinth two bricks thick, was similar to his other works, chiefly floor works consisting of metal tiles arranged in rectangles and lines. It was an important part of the work that the viewer was free to walk on the sculpture. These works are both self effacing in their near invisibility and grandiose in their ambition. Andre said that they represented footprints of much larger structures and that he wanted to imply monolithic structures through minimal and economic means. He thereby created a tension between the monolithic and the invisible. 
Andre also made a number of text works, some with ‘x’s and ‘o’s in rows, and others a kind of concrete poetry made by fitting words into some kind of geometric system.
! Brice Marden. 
American painter of minimal abstract works, who made large (at least 2 by 3 often) canvases painted entirely usually muted colours very often grey with one or two contrasting coloured stripes often on the edge of the canvas. Marten's work has similar formal qualities to the work of Barnet Newman, although where Newman's work is painterly, Marden’s is hard edged and painted with flat solid colour. Marden used a lot of grey, black and neutral colours.
! Agnes Martin.
A painter born about 1930 who made the work for which she is best known in the 1950s and 60s. Martin’s minimal abstract paintings are usually based on a grid, mainly white with pencil thin lines of bright colours, which are barely perceptible from a distance. The paintings are cool, calm and full of light, and rigorous in their execution.
! John McCracken
British artist who makes minimal abstract sculptures. Plank and cube shaped objects made to a high gloss lacquered finish in solid colours. The planks (about 3 metres long, 50 cm wide and 12 cm deep) typically lean against the gallery wall. Other sculptures are hung from the wall.
!Rainer, Yvonne.
American choreographer who worked with artists such as Robert Rauschenberg and Jasper Johns as well as musicians such as John Cage on collaborative performance works such as The Mind is A Muscle (1971). These were developed largely at the Black Mountain College summer school and at the Judson Church Centre in New York and were unusual in their success as multidisciplinary projects. 
Rainer choreographed dance works based on the repetition of everyday movements eschewing classical dance form and skills. This emphasis on the everyday had its parallels in both minimalism and Pop Art.
Rainer later went on to make films, and in the 1990s made her first feature film.
!!TOWARDS POP ART.
Artists such as Rauschenberg, Johns and Lichtenstein took the logic of abstraction and minimalism and applied it to everyday objects and images and thier innovations paved the way for pop art.
!Rauschenberg, Robert.
Working in New York Rauschenberg began his career in the late 50s and was part of New York pop art in the 60s. He worked in all media and is particularly known for his paintings with objects attached and his objects with painting attached. In either case the object would be made part of the painting or the painting be made part of the sculpture by extending the paint to cover the object. 
Rauschenberg’s innovation was to use screen printing as a medium, transferring images onto his canvases even before Warhol did, in particular to photographic images from newspapers, or cityscapes and street signs on to canvas before adding paint and even found objects.
One of his best-known paintings features a multi-layered image of a city with a stop sign, a sign that reads ‘one way’ and architectural elements and abstract areas of colour. Another has a photographic transfer of president Kennedy. 
Rauschenberg made his essentially abstract works from found images and objects. One painting features a wooden chair attached to the canvas, for another a single mattress with sheets pillow and blankets becomes the canvas and is hung on the wall and painted on. Rauschenberg's work was a conceptual painterly abstraction that was part of the early development of pop art. He also made many drawings including notably one called ‘Erased De Kooning’, which was simply that, a drawing by de Kooning that Rauschenberg had erased.
Rauschenberg worked with Merce Cunningham the dancer and member of the Judson Church collective of artist and dancers which including choreographer Yvonne Rainer, designing sets, lighting and costumes for dance performances. Often he would paint the set and the costumes himself, for one dance work he made a canvas back painted with spots with bodysuits painted with spots for as costumes. Cunningham was the partner of Jasper Johns. 
!Johns Jasper.
American artist born in the 1930s who was one of the artists who made the move from figuration to abstrctoin in the 1950s, and then in the 1960s began to use recognisable symbols in his paitings, the best known being the Amerifcan flag. Johhs and his contemporary Rauschenberg paved the way for Pop Art. 
John’s target paintings use a circular target motif (the kind used for archery or shooting) – balck and white, or red yellow and blue. The paintings are portrait format so that the cirucvluar target is placed in the piciture plane and there are sometimes other elements in the composititoin. 
Johns’ American flag paintings developed this furhter and were innovative for their use of a preixisting symbol in its entireiyt as the image of the paitnign. The flag paitnigns are just that - painted flags, the stars and stripes covering the cnavas edge to edge. Johns questioned the art object by making it inseparable from the object it depicted. This was the first time anyone had used the flag as an image and johns made it into an abstract painting, the flag as abstraction going right to edge of canvas, rather that a painting of a flag. The flag was ideologically loaded at the time because it was being burnt by protesters against the Vietnam war. 
Johns went on to make a proliferation of flag paintings drawings and lithographs initially red white and blue but then he began changing the colours culminating in a series of black on balck and white on white flags and even an edition of bronze flag table sculptures. 
Johns also made a series of number painitngs, simply painting the numbers zero to nine, drawn using a stencil, on a protrait format canvas to fill the canvas. These were done in many versions includinbg whihte on white. 
Producing an ordinary object that was alos an art object led johns to make some bronze scultpures. The best known of these are the Beer Cans and the can of paint brushes. These life size replicas are patined in true to life colours. They are not trompe l’oile objedcts but they do give the viewer pause for thought, whist remaining resolutuely paitned bronze sculptures.  
!Lichtenstein Roy.
American artist working from the 1960s onwards, Lichtenstein’s work was among the best known of the pop artists. He made paintings using images from printed cartoon magazines. Lichtenstein took single frames or even a detail from a frame and enlarged it, initially to about one metre high but eventually to a much larger size. He then made a painting form the drawing, reproducing exactly what he saw including any text. Initially the paintings were black and white but Lichtenstein soon mover to colour. The painting s nearly always feature a person, a part of a vehicle and a graphic depiction of fire, speed, force, for example. They are part of a larger narrative which we are left to guess at.
Eventually Lichtenstein began to paint the dots made by the printing process, easily visible in the magazine but very obvious when enlarged. These dots are obvious on billboards when they are viewed close up. By making the dots part of the painting Lichtenstein was reproducing a reproduction and the means of its reproduction. He was actually painting the printing process. He used the cyan magenta and yellow process colours on a white ground, with black. From a distance his paintings appeared to be made in flat colours, from close up the dots were the main component. 
Certain of Lichtenstein’s works became iconographic, for example one of a fighter pilot in a cockpit of a plane that has a yellow flash coming from. The text announces the pilots thought at that moment. Another of a glamorous woman crying, again with text revealing her thoughts. His style was and still it copied, plagiarised and borrowed.
!!POP ART.
!Warhol Andy.
An American artist who is one of the most significant of the late twentieth century. Warhol was born in the 1930s and died in the 1980s. He began as a graphic designer in his twenties. He was very accomplished at drawing and became an artist. Warhol is best known for his Campbell’s soup can paintings and his Marilyn paintings, but these are only a small part of his extremely prolific output. 
Warhol’s early graphic work included advertisements for shoe shops, clothes shops, book children’s and adult book illustrations, book jackets, packaging. He used pen and ink, colour and sometimes added gold leaf. He began to make screen prints.
Warhol became an artist and turned his studio into a gathering place and workshop called The Factory. He made his own work at The Factory but by virtue of his eccentric personality and arresting style began to gather around him a coterie of colleagues, assistants and admirers. Warhol’s hair was bleached white and he was never seen without dark glasses and in stylish clothes. He was close to the most inspiring people in the fashion, music and art worlds. It is often said that Warhol himself and The Factory are a part of Warhol’s art. Certainly Warhol’s persona was unique and The Factory was an unprecedented example of collaborative practise. 
At The Factory Warhol worked with photographer Billy Name, who documented everything that happened there, models such as Viva and Edie Sedgwick (among others), who were really his muses, actors and any person usually young and beautiful who interested him. The force of Warhol’s personality and the mystique that he created around himself meant that people were very keen to work with him or to commission him to make a portrait. Warhol was obsessed with fame.
Sedgwick and Viva starred in Chelsea Girls, Warhol’s only feature film. Many other short experimental films were made at the factory including Empire State a single static shot of the Empire State building 24 hours long, that documented the changing light on the building. Warhol said he wanted to make a film that people could watch when they were stoned.
The Screen Tests are a series of head and shoulders shots of just about anyone who passed through the doors of the factory, including many who years later became well known, those who didn’t and those who were well known at the time. Warhol would simply ask the person to sit and look at the camera. 
Warhol published a ground breaking fashion music and art magazine called Interview and made a series of TV chat shows for which he interviewed people. 
Underpinning all this activity was the continuous production of Warhol’s paintings. Warhol took the most ordinary mass produced object and represented it as art, making a photographic silk screen of the label on a Campbell’s soup can. He did this as an anti-art gesture, believing like other pop artists that art did not need to have noble subject matter and that art was a commodity like any other. To this end he used images of coke bottles, Brillo pad boxes and cornflake boxes among others to make his paintings, repeating the colour images in rows to cover the canvas, robbing the image of its original meaning. He produced these in multiples, denying them any exclusivity. He made sculptures of plywood replica Brillo boxes in the same way.
Warhol next made a screen print from a photograph of Marilyn Monroe to which he added paint by hand on the lips and eyes and round her hair. He made as many of these as he felt like, painting each one differently. What made them different was that they were printed on canvas so that they became paintings. Multiples of the Marilyns each one always the same size (one metre high) are often hung in rows and blocks and they have become so famous ads to be almost ubiquitous, exactly what Warhol would have wanted. He was attracted to Monroe because of her fame and glamour. Warhol made a vast number of paintings of Monroe including a pair gold painted round canvases, with her image printed in black on one and the other left blank. 
As well as the Marilyn’s Warhol made a large number of paintings of Chairman Mao in the same way. These two works of Warhol’s are among the most recognisable images of Monroe and Mao.
Warhol also made a number of images of an electric execution Chair, again made in a wide variety of colour schemes. He made a Last Supper, repeated images of Jesus on one canvas and very numerous portrait commissions which were a mainstay of the Factory’s finances. These included Jackie Onassis, Joan Collins, Elvis, and many society and business figures. Warhol also made a number of self portraits, including one with sticking up hair.
Towards the end of his life Warhol made some collaborative paintings with jean Michel Basquiat.

!Oldenburgh Claes.
American artist born in the 1940s who is known for his soft sculptures. Which he began to make in the sixties. Along with Warhol, Oldenburhg was the main pop art sculptor. Oldenburgh worked in a number of different media and also made a large number of working drawings. His soft sculptures are made of fabric, sewn and stuffed in the manner of soft toys, using canvas, which he then painted, or vinyl. The sculptures represent vastly enlarged objects such as a light switch made in white vinyl, a drum kit made in painted canvas and a hamburger, accurately scaled up and constructed but stuffed loosely so that they sagged and collapsed. The light switch (about 1 metre square) hangs drooping on the wall, the drum kit (3 metres across) flops in a heap on the floor. Oldenburgh also made many painted clay sculptures, roughly modelled and painted, some cast in bronze, of which one of a hamburger is the best known. In the 1970s Oldenburgh began to make public sculpture and also made a large number of designs, plans and sketches for monumental works, which were not always realised. These include a 30-metre high lipstick monument, a building based on the head of Mickey Mouse and one based on an electrical plug.
!Ruscha, Ed.
American artist born in the 1940s who became well known in the late 1960s for his paintings of words. Ruscha took the sign and the billboard as his inspiration and made the painted word the subject of his work. He is best known for his painting fo the Hollwood sign. 
Ruscha used a variety of typefaces, three dimensional letters and also made a series of paitings of words as substance, for example the word ‘oil’ painted to look like oil. He also made landscape paintings to which words are added. Sometimes Rushca used a (literal or imaginary) diagonal line in his paintings which he attriburtes to the compostional elements in classical painting
!Rosenquist James.
American artist working from the late 1950s onwards, who has made some of the largest paintings of any contemporary artist. Rosenquist is a pop artist distinguished by his skill at painting of a vast scale acquired during his years as a professional billboard painter, painting images on hoardings above Times Square in New York for example. Rosenquist is known for two paintings in particular, F111 (1961) and another one painted the same year. Both are 4 or 5 metres high and around 25 metres long. F111 shows an American military aircraft, the painted lady on its side, and various other military items, alluding to the contemporary Vietnam War protest movement. The other work shows a collage of items of Americana of varying relative sizes, such as hugely enlarged tinned spaghetti, celebrating the emerging consumer culture in tune with the spirit of the times. Rosenquist’s paintings often include an idealised woman.
Rosenquist was a contemporary of Andy Warhol and shared much of the same subject matter, but the two artists were unaware of each other’s work until later in the 60s. Rosenquist continued to work on the same scale throughout his career refining his technique until with new digital imaging technology to help it became very far removed from his original billboard style. In the late 1999s onwards, he made a return to the way he had worked in the 1960s. 

!Caulfield Patrick.
British artist working from the 1950s onwards who is known for his very distinctive hard edged representative paintings. Caulfield’s subject is architecture and the interior, particularly the domestic or leisure interior. He chose to paint interiors or elements from interiors such as furniture, lighting, ceramics, and developed a way of making his paintings that he never deviated from. Caulfield takes a photograph and copies its most basic structure rendering forms with the minimum of line possible. He treats light and shadow as a solid object and draws it in the same way. Thus the light from a table lamp will be represented as a solid yellow shape, the shadow behind its shade as a black shape. All the objects in a painting are given equal importance, since every element is treated the same. The outlines are painted with a schematic, dramatically simplified line, in black and the objects, walls, floors, light fittings, furniture filled in bright colours. The black lines are vaguely reminiscent of the lead in a stained glass window, yet they also call to mind Matisse’s black outlines. The acrylic paint is applied entirely flat, the edge of every shape outlined in black, the edge of every colour hard and smooth although not made by masking. Colours are every hue and shade, bright, sober, pale and intense.
Caulfield had several favourite items, the lamp and the vase among them. He made many small paintings around 50 cm high of one or two items, as well as many of entire interiors on a larger scale usually two metres high or more. He favoured the portrait format. His paintings can be defined as still lifes and interiors much in the manner of seventeenth century  Dutch artists, and he painted the aspirations of an equivalent social group in his own day. Modern design, the latest in home interiors and fittings featured in his paintings. 
Caulfield’s best known works are some of the simple still lifes, like a single lamp, a goldfish in a bowl, an ashtray with a cigarette, a vase, an oval mirror which somehow conveys the idea that it is reflective even though it is painted in black and white. The schematic, iconographic way these things are painted makes them deceptively simple to look at. 
Of his larger works, perhaps the interiors of a Swiss bar is the best known. This interior is painted mainly in blue (with the usual black outline) which signifies the quality of light in the place rather than the materials of which the furniture is made. In this Caulfield uses another characteristic technique. The photo mural on the wall behind the banquette seating is painted realistically, in that it looks like a view of an actual mountain scenery rather than schematic. This is the only element in the painting that he renders in this way, paying homage to Braque and Picasso, who collaged printed material or imagery onto their canvases as part of a cubist painting. There are also other examples that make this more explicit, for example where Caulfield paints a piece of wood grain realistically (in direct appreciation of the Braque, who stick a piece of wood grain onto a canvas to signify a guitar). He also paints the pattern of textiles in an interior in this way. But only ever one element in the whole composition is painted realistically. Caulfield never painted a person. 
!Hamilton Richard.
British artist who was part of a group of pop artists in the 1960s. Hamilton made paintings and collages. His paintings are figurative and usually have a realistically painted element, perhaps a figure, with an abstract, more painterly element, often blurry and watery, painted on un-primed or thinly primed canvas. Like many of the pop artists, Hamilton used the techniques of graphic art, like brushing and photographic reproduction, in combination with oil painting. For example in the painting of the IRA hunger striker Bobby Sands during the period of his dirty protest made in the 1970s. It shows Sands in his prison cell, long haired and bearded, in front of a wall smeared with his own excrement. Hamilton used an airbrush to paint the excrement as blurry brown squiggles. Hamilton’s collages are made as sketches for paintings but sometimes presented as works in their own right, such as the best known one, and arguably Hamilton’s best known work, A Plan for Everyday Living. This shows a montage of a modern domestic interior, an apartment with cantilevered stairs descending into a double height living area with picture windows, the stairs being vacuumed by a body builder dressed only in a frilly apron. 
!Jim Dine.
A contemporary of Rauschenberg and Johns in New York in the 60s
Dine's best known work features his trademark hearts. Drawn in outline or painted often in red or pink on white.
!Longo Robert.
American artist who made both paintings drawings and sculptures in the 1970s and 80s. The sculptures are usually complementary to or even shown as a part of a painted work. For example Longo made a number of works in tow and half metre high panels arranged in threes and fours, the panels showing a man clothed in a suit apparently falling to the ground (perhaps having been shot) although he could almost be dancing, some blank white panels and some cityscapes. The representational panels would often be drawn in pencil and graphite in a very realistic way. The panels are very well made. In some cases there is a bronze cast of a similar male figure placed in front of the wall panels, the best known being of man ‘falling’, the balance of the sculpture precarious. 
!Robert Indiana.
A screen print and sculpture made by Indiana becaem the motif for a generation. He placed the letters of the word ‘love’ one on top of the other to form a cube shape with a distinctive slanted letter O. 

Concetpual art which had begun in Europe had been brought to America by Duchamp and conceptualism contineud to develop on both continents. 

!Klein Yves.
Although he cannot be classed purely as a conceptual arist, French artist Klien’s subject was the condition of art, and he made several works which gained him notoriety, in particular the Blue Paintings. These were made as a performance in a gallery in Paris. Klein invited female models to act a human brushes. He applied ultramarine blue paint to their naked bodies and then directed them to position themselves and move their bodies in certain ways, making body prints onto paper he had placed on the floor and walls. Klein’s idea was to use the nude to paint the nude. The resulting body prints were exhibited, but the photographic documentation of the event became better known. Later on he made some fire and smoke paintings for which he set fire to canvases and marked them with smoke. 
ultramarine blue was Klein’s trademark colour. All his painted work and sculpture was made using it. He made a large number of paintings in the 1950s and early 60s using natural sponges stuck to canvas and the whole painted blue. He also made simple blue canvases. Similarly he made sculptures with the sponges, often creating tableaux of his flower or seaweed like creations.
Klein worked briefly in ceramics, making small sculptures that drew on porcelain table ornaments; Klein made his own lumpen thrown together and glazed versions that nevertheless made their sources clear. 
The other strand to Klein’s work is more conceptual. He made many works with found objects and works which were events and documentations of actions. He made a work with a light bulb and several works with furniture.  
The best known of these works are the Leap into the Void, a staged photograph in which Klein is seen apparently leaping off a high wall in a street and falling through the air. There is a house on the left and a lamppost behind him. The photograph is about 40 cm high, portrait format. Paris Air was a sealed glass vial containing nothing but air, to which Klein accorded a status as an art work and a monetary value. In a performance work Klein sold gold leaf which he had authenticated to collectors and then invited them to throw the work they had just bought into the river Seine. He also printed his own bonds, and sold these as works of art. The work which summed up Klein’s interests most completely was The Void. This work was a staged event. Klein rented an empty gallery. He sent out invitations to the opening of his exhibition, provided champagnes and hired a commissionaire to stand on the door. No one was allowed to enter the gallery without an invitation. When they did so they found an empty gallery – the void of the title.  
!Broodthaers Marcel.
Like Klein, Marcel Broodthaers cannot be defined as a conceptual aritst but he draws both from this and surrealism. Broodthaers occupies an eccentric personal world which centres around his Musée des Aigles, and the mussel shell. A Belgian artist working in the 50s and 60s Broodthaers was something of a surrealist, with his use of puns and irony, and juxtaposition of unlikely objects. Joseph Beuys work has similar visual qualities to that of Broodthaers, as does his creation of a fake institution and the didactic ( if ironically so) aspirations of his work. He created his own sometimes impenetrable lexicon of signs and symbols, and indeed signs were part of his work. He made several signs displaying diagrammatic objects such as a pipe, with ‘fig.1’, ‘fig 2’ etc. underneath the signs were pressed sheet metal reliefs. He made a number of small sculptures and wall reliefs using mussel shells (possibly as an emblem of Belgian nationality) stuck together. The Musée des Aigles – French for eagle but also a pun on something else like epaulet – contained everything the artist had collected with eagles' wings, such as military crests, logos and such like. All Broodthaers work has a spare, monochrome appearance.

!!CONCEPTUAL ART.
!Haacke Hans.
American artist who began working in the 1960s and became one of the best known of the 70s conceptual artists in America and Europe. His practise was political in the sense that his subject matter was the multinational corporation and advertising, which he believed to be the means by which corporations controlled their customers and the financial markets. 
Haacke made a number of installations consisting of text panels with photographs detailing the activities of multinational companies such as William Morris, often subverting advertising design used by the company.
Haacke's earlier sculptural work includes a piece called Condensation Cube (1965), a Plexiglas cube about 50 cm square with naturally occurring and accumulating droplets of condensation inside it.
!Boetti Alighiero.
Italian artist born early in the century working between the 1960s and 90s, Boetti made quite a lot of wall hangings using embroidery, chiefly maps of the world, but also tapestry panels showing the letters of the alphabet. Naming and writing were his interests and also the time consuming handiwork of embroidery and tapestry along with the very similar method of using the ballpoint pen to colour large areas. He also made a series of drawings in ballpoint pen of invisible words. Reading from key along the top and sides of the drawing it is possible to discern the words in the drawings by referring to inverted commas on the grid of the drawing. The whole paper is coloured by intensive use of the blue or red ballpoint, leaving only the inverted commas white. Boetti’s work seems somehow anti-art, using craft techniques and the tool of the scribble the ballpoint pen.
!On Kawara.
Japanese artist who began working in the 1960s, Kawara known for a number of conceptual projects and endurance works. I Met I Went is a year of postcards written and mailed to friends and acquatinaces by Kawara every day for a year. The work with which Kawara is usually associated is a still accumulating work called the Date Paintigns, one of which he has made every day since the 1960s. Each identical 50cm high painting has the days date in white numerals on a black canvas.

!Craig-Martin Michael.
British American artist born in the 1940s who makes wall paintings depicted much larger than in real life. Ordinary objects – furniture, paperclips, tools, bicycles, lamps, globes, fittings are juxtaposed without reference to relative scale at various angles. The objects are placed on a very brightly coloured ground painted with matt emulsion paint and outlined in black plastic tape, except for a period in the 1980s when Craig-Martin used square sectioned steel rods bent to shape and welded to create the outlines. The wall paintings may fill a whole gallery or museum, or just one wall. 
Craig-Martins earlier work made in the 1960s and 70s is conceptual. His best known work is the Oak Tree, which consists of a glass of water placed on a glass shelf above the viewers head and an accompanying framed text which explains to the viewer why the glass of water is an oak tree, by means of a question and answer between the artist and a fictitious interlocutor. The artist claims that it is by the authority invested in him as an artist that the glass of water is an oak tree, thus this is a work about authorship and subjectivity. He also made a work with a row of clipboards and plumb lines hung on a wall, a series of boxes with hinged lids placed on the floor, the lids taking progressively larger parts of the box until the lid was more box and the box was more lid.
! Sol Lewitt.
American conceptual artist who made installation and drawings. Lewitt is best known for his wall drawings made in pencil directly onto the wall which is painted either black or white. Lewitt devises a complex geometric pattern of lines, parallel, radiating or on a grid, which is drawn in coloured pencil, forming a barely perceptible drawing which nevertheless has a strong effect on the space and adds glowing areas of colour. 
Lewitt also made a number of ssulptures based on the grid. These are three dimensional grids formed from 30cm cubes stacked in rows on the floor the whole sprayed with white lacqquer. Each layer has a different formation and may be up to 40 cubes long. The cubes are not solid, so that the sturcture has a complex appearance.
! Daniel Buren.
Buren painted lozenge shapes on buildings and architectural structures, making site specific works that use the lozenge shapes as a visual intervention to prompt a reconsideration of the scene presented to the viewer, and attempt to alter the reading of the picture plane, treating an environment as if it were no more than a photograph. The lozenges allude to signage and logos are entirely without function. Buren's work is as much to do with reconsidering landscape painting as it is do with installation.
! Victor Burgin.
American artist who made conceptual work from the 1960s onwards with a strong theoretical slant evident both in the work and in his writing about art.
! James Lee Byars.
Byers worked in the 1960s onwards. One sculpture is a clear acrylic case, containing white sand, a white book, and a cream coloured (almost white) snake. the work can be recreated anywhere as long as the artists instructions are followed, he specifies the white sand, the dimensions of case and book, which must be made by a bookbinder and that the snake must be as close to white as is available. 
Early concetpualism implied a form of resistance to subversion of the cultural norm. This gave the work of these artists a political edge until at the end of the twentieth century conceptualism became the norm.
One artist who is identified with a subversive intention is Beuys, who made conceptual and political art between the 50s and the 80s.
 
! Joseph Beuys.
Born in early 1930s. One of the most significant German artists of the second half to the twentieth century. He was a professor at the Dusseldorf academy for many years, where he had a huge influence on several generations of younger artists. Beuys was an eccentric character who always dressed in the same canvas hat and black canvas shooting waistcoat with multiple pockets. He is extremely prolific, working in all media but chiefly known as a sculptor, performance and installation artist who proclaimed that ‘life is art’ making his every action part of his work.
Beuys is notorious for the myth of a near death experience that surrounds him and although long since discredited has proved impossible to ignore. It was said that he had been shot down when flying for the Luftwaffe in the second world war over snowy mountains. His life was saved by local villagers who found him and kept his body temperature up by smearing him in pig fat and wrapping him in felt. 
Whether apocryphal or not, this event would seem to have had a profound influence on most of Beuys’ work. His favoured materials are felt and fat. However Beuys’ personal lexicon is often impenetrable and inaccessible, although s
One of his best known works is Portrait of the Artist Talking to a Dead Hare, which was made for his first solo show in New York in 1979. Beuys sits in the gallery, his head covered in honey and gold leaf (also highly important symbolic substances in his work), cradling a dead hare in his arms talking to it. There is some reference to the city of riches and capitalism in the gold leaf
Politics are a major feature of Beuys work, he is a socialist and an anti-capitalist, (although some claim that he is more of a reactionary). In fact he was sacked from his post at Dusseldorf for inciting student riots, although this to is subject to speculation. In his second New York show he made a work with a live wolf. Beuys sat in the gallery behind a chain link fence separating him and the viewer, with a wolf for company to which he talked. The two stayed there for a week. The wolf symbolised the plundering and appropriation of the American wilderness and by his interaction with its spirit Beuys was trying to heal the wounds caused to the indigenous people. 
Sculptures the best known is his Fat Chair, a wooden chair with lard piled at a slant towards the back of it. He made a number of works from stacks of plate steel and felt which he described as batteries, some kind of life preserving power source. A number of vitrines which contained an array of mysterious but apparently utilitarian metal wood fat and felt objects. 
A series of performance had the format of lectures often conducted in a studio, in which he used blackboards to illustrate his talk, usually about the possibility of art being life and in particular to make his famous claim that ‘Art is a Social Sculpture’. By this he meant that art could not exist with the interaction of the viewer, it was therefore subjective, and thus a social event. In this sense art was by extension political. The blackboards with their drawings diagrams and writing remained as a document of the lecture.
Beuys made himself a felt suit and various other garments and items, these usually had a cross cut from cotton fabric stitched onto them.
In 1895 Beuys showed in London. He installed rolls of felt the height of the gallery all round the walls and across the entrance, leaving only a small 3 foot high gap from the floor up for the viewer to enter. Inside was a grand piano. A total damped silence was implied by felt and experienced by the deadened acoustic.
VW camper van with a number of wooden sledges emerging from its door, linked by a harness.
5000 trees planted in a small town.
Beuys made many works on paper, using charcoal, paint, ink and in particular a specific brand of rust/dried blood red coloured paint the kind used to preserve sail cloth or paint door steps. The name of the paint and the colour was personally symbolic.
Beuys made a cast of an underpass under a road by pouring melted lard into it and then cutting it out.
In the first half of the century other artists were involved more directly in politics.

!!ART AND POLITICS.
Artists have often used their skills in the service of their political beliefs, sometimes serving the state by working as official artists, in which case they were well provided for. The Soviet government used socialist realist art as a propaganda tool, so that artists who wanted to resist found other ways fo working. Chinese state artists were similiary employed, some even worked at altering photographs to suit the govenrments propaganda needs. 
Artists working under totalitarian regimes who had opposting political beliefs may have had no choice but to serve the state or risk punishment. In order to avoid this many defected or worked underground and in theses stiuations art has often been a tool of restistance or subversion. Most of these artists diid not achieve great fame due to the circumstances in which they had to live. Picasso’s Guernica is a well known work of political resistance.
Some artist simply used their skills to record what they found abhorrent.
! Kathe Kollwitz. 
Kollwitz is an Austrian artist who made contemporaneous drawings in the concentration camps during the holocaust mainly in graphite and charcoal often depicting women.
Rivera Diego
Rivera was a Mexican mural artist working in the 1930s and 40s who made political wall paintings celebrating ordinary people and workers in public buildings in the servcie of the socialists. He fell out of favour when the socialist government gave way to a right wing party who found him politically embarrassing and prevented him from finishing his last major public commission. Rivera was the husband of the artist Frida Kahlo and a friend of Leon Trotsky.
The posters used by the demonstrators in the national strike and demonstrations in Paris and other cities in May 1968 borrowed the ideas of the Situationist International group of artists, who set up a poster printing workshop to make them. These bold graphic one colour screen prints had simple images and text.
!! THE SITUATIONIST INTERNATIONAL
Debord Guy
founder and leader of the Situationist International, a Paris based group of international artists who were interested in Debord's revolutionary ideas (and politics). Debord took Baudelaire's idea of the Flaneur, the wanderer through a city and developed it into his personal concept of the ‘dérive’, a directed walk through urban scenery. Debord wanted to make the life of a city his subject and he believed that people should make themselves part of the city. He was responsible through the poster printing initiative he was part of, for many of the slogans familiar from the protests of striking workers in Paris in May 1968, for example ‘Under the pavement is the Beach’ and ‘Beauty is in the streets’. Debord had various ideas including putting light switches on street lamps so that the people could turn them on and off at will. 
Jorn Asger
Dutch artist and member of the Situationists. Jorn made collages with torn posters, for example a sixteenth century portrait of a woman in a blue brocade dress has the face defaced by the additions of some paint and a collaged element. 
There was a strong performative element to what the Situationist proposed. Many artist in the seventies took conceptual concerns with and objectgions to the status fo the art object to their logical extreme and began to make perfomrance art, dispensing with the object and any sense of permanence entiryel and usign their own bodies as both subject and material. Performance art was a focus for many women artist too who saw it as a way to make work about the reclaiming of the female body by women. 
PERFORMANCE ART
Schneeman, Carolee
American artist born around 1950 who made performance art. Schneeman’s best known work is Meatjoy (1975), for a large number of naked performers who were instructed to perform an orgiastic celebration by wallowing in animal entrails and pieces of meat and blood. EyeBody was a solo work by Schneeman in which she sat on a swing, naked, in a space which had white backdrop paper on the walls, holding an umbrella. During the performance she made a drawing by swinging as close as she could to the walls and drawing on them. For another work Interior Scroll Schneeeman read out loud a poem wihc she had written, from a long strip of paper previosuly inseted into her vagina and gradually extracted as she read.
Tseching Hsieh
Chinese artist who made a performance in the early 1980s for which he was tied to another person by a rope 3 metres long for one year.
! Allan Kaprow. 
American artist working between the 1960s and 80s, Kaprow is best known for his Happenings in New York in the late 1960s. These were site specific installations in which a performance would happen witnessed by an invited audience. The Happenings were one of the earliest examples of artists making live art in galleries or gallery like spaces rather than in a theatre or studio space. One happening took place in a loft in which a structure of stud wall frames covered with transparent plastic sheeting created a box like space in which the performers could be seen but were obscured. Kaprow wrote a seminal book about the Happenings which was influential for artists making time based work. 
!Antin Eleanor.
American artist born around 1948. In her best known work Carving: a Traditional Sculpture made in 1971 Antin put herself on a strict diet and had a male doctor weigh and measure her every day for a month. This is one of a number of works in which Antin investigated the effect on women of the ideals forced on them in a patriarchal society. In this work she attempted to literally sculpt her own body, making a comment both on women’s control over their own bodies, on the centuries of depiction of the nude female body by men, and on women’s continuing need to make themselves attractive for men. The performance was documented by one photograph every day which were placed together in a grid and framed, and by a video. During the 1980s Antin began to make more theatrical performances, using cut out puppets. She also developed an alter ego, Eleanora Antinova a trans gender Russian ballet dancer who wore a tutu and a long beard.
!Abramovic, Marina and Ulay. 
Born 1950s, Abramovic first came to public attention when she invited visitors to a gallery in which she appeared naked, stating that the visitors could do anything they wanted to her and she would not resist or retaliate. She provided a number of implements with which to do this including knives and a gun. She narrowly avoided serious injury but her initial premise was correct: like the invisible barrier of etiquette surrounding an art work, there was a barrier of propriety and embarrassment protecting her that was only broached when the beginnings of a mob mentality formed.
Abramovic worked together with her partner Ulay until the early 1980s making performance ‘sculptures’ and endurance works. ‘Breathe’ involved them in a mouth to mouth embrace for as long as they could breathe using their combined exhaled breath. They braided their hair together and stayed sitting back to back for 8 hours. For another work they used a full size bow and arrow and stood facing each other, with Ulay holding the string and Abramovic holding the arrow at the point on the centre of the bow string from which it is released. They maintained this state of equilibrium through tension until the strain of holding on meant they had to either stop or let go of the tension in the bow. This would have resulted in the release of the arrow which was aimed at Abramovic. In Doorway they stood naked either side of the entrance to the gallery in the doorway facing each other. This meant that those wishing to enter were forced to brush past them and had to choose who to face as they turned sideways to enter. 
These partner works focused on endurance through concentration and on the tension in partnerships manifested in physical mutual support. They are notable for the degree of politeness and gentleness combined with a power and implicit violence. The artists described them as sculptures.
After the relationship with Ulay ended Abramovic continued to make endurance works. While these maintained a formal sculptural quality they acquired a ritual aspect, as in the work in which she sat in a circle of salt surrounded by poisonous snakes. In another work created in an amethyst mining town in Brazil, she made and wore clogs from blocks of amethyst. Abramovic made a work for a tree sited in a public square in which she placed a number of speakers playing birdsong so that it seemed that the tree was teeming with tiny birds.
!Acconci Vito.
US artist born 1940s, Acconci made several endurance works in the early 70s, notably Seedbed, for which he built a false gallery floor which sloped up form the door to the rear of the space. Acconci was reputedly sealed in the void under the floor masturbating. In another work he constructed a shelf high up on the gallery wall in such a way that he could not be seen by those in the gallery from any angle and stayed on the shelf for the hours during which the gallery was open. In these works he made the gallery space and the viewers own feelings about being in that space the subject of the work, by his invisible presence having the maximum possible effect on the viewer. 
A smaller scale work is a video playing on a monitor placed on a furry rug on the floor. On the screen Acconci is seen, lying on his stomach, his face right up against the camera, talking to the viewer and inviting them to lie down on the rug, to join him on the floor.
!Burden Chris.
Burden became known in the late 60s and early 70s for his performance work mostly involving physical endurance. In ‘Shoot’ he had a friend shoot him in the arm in the gallery at a specified time. The shooting was documented and like all Burdens works of the period the documentation became the work. He was not seriously injured. In Locker Piece he had himself locked in a left luggage locker at a railway station for 8 hours. In Freeway he lay in the middle of a freeway. He also had himself nailed to the bonnet of a VW Beetle through his hands and feet. Burdens was interested in making formal sculptural work using his own body and in the visual power of pain and danger. The purity and simplicity of the works made them very powerful. His later work he developed an interest in war and military capability though his pacifist beliefs and in environmental damage. A work involving hundreds of tiny model submarines, a large meteorite like lump suspended from the gallery ceiling with model railway track running all over it and in and out of it. A large and complex computer controlled machine for making and throwing paper aeroplanes.
! Joan Jonas.
American performance artist and musician 70s also worked with Judson church dancers Yvonne Rainer and Merce Cunningham a performance with a cello.
! Dara Birnbaum.
US performance artist known for her performance in which she dressed as wonder woman.
Some women artists made work that although not performed was perfmormative in its intention. They included Judy Chigaoc and Mary Kelly. 
!Chicago Judy.
American artist Chicago born 1950s changed her name from the patronymic to that of her birthplace as a feminist gesture in the 1970s. Chicago’s work ‘The Dinner Party’ 1972 became a iconic feminist work. It is a narrow table arranged in a triangular format, with place settings for all the famous and unsung heroines of American life, for example Mrs Lincoln. The place settings are actually embroideries depicting plates and each one is made by a different woman artist. Similar to the way women make church kneelers. Chicago wanted to draw attention to the under-representation of women’s achievement by staging this imaginary dinner party of these great women, and to use traditionally female but culturally undervalued materials and methods to make it. She brought the women together my means of the dinner table because this is the means by which women had traditionally brought people together. The work came to be seen as representative of the struggle for recognition of women artists as well as in other areas of life of the time, and was influential for several succeeding generations of artists. 
Kelly Mary
US artist who made works dealing with feminist issues from the 1970s onwards. Kelly’s seminal work is the Post Partum Document, an extremely influential work made in 1973 which documents the birth and emergence into language of her son Kelly. The work takes the form of about thirty framed panels, each containing an object and a text. The object might be a soiled nappy (which helped to gather tabloid attention at the time), an item of clothing. The text details the child’s weight and length, age in weeks, and describes his development with notes on his linguistic development. This is a work which documents the birth of her child and her care for his life as a creative activity, making the point that the birth of a child, as revealed here through the banal objects and detail, is a profoundly creative act. This startlingly simple and obvious statement caused a great deal of criticism and publicity, the Post Partum Document was declared not to be art, to be offensive, to be cruel. Questions about the artists motherhood were raised, and from all this it can be deduced that the work made its point very successfully. Motherhood is a taboo, it is work which is not to be considered work, not on a par with artistic achievement and not to be revealed in all its messy detail. 
The works form was important. it was predominantly a text work and this was another of Kelly’s points – that text, or language, is how the subject is constituted and that we are not subjects until we are speaking subjects. The emergence of a child into language is therefore the most important moment in our existence and this is made possible by the mother (or primary carer, as we should add today). Kelly drew on the works of Lacan the French literary theorist who developed the theory of the other and the difference between  the male and female subject. and of psychoanalytical writers like Freud, Klein. Lacan's theories on the constitution of the subject were particularly influential for a whole generation of artists in the 1980s, mostly but not all women.
Kelly’s later work developed into institutional critique always wall and text based. She made a number of highly crafted metal medallions or wall plaques.
!Darboven Hannah.
Artist working in the 1960s and 70s who made works using text. Darboven was interested in catalogues, indexes and diaries. One work for example took hundreds of index cards on which the artist had written and displayed them carefully categorised. The text on the cards however consisted only of ‘x’s. This display of an organising system which contained no information served visualise a practise which is in use in daily life but which is never actually seen and rarely considered. Darboven’s obsessive writing projects are always made by hand.

Wilke Hannah
American artist born in the 1940s who became known in the 1970s for her photographic and performance work. Wilke often appeared naked, which was controversial at a time when feminist artists were reclaiming the female body, traditionally painted naked by men, for themselves. For one performance she appeared naked but wearing high heeled shoes on the steps of a public building. Wilke’s Chewing Gum Scarification Series is her best known work, in which she applied a large number of ‘scars’ made from chewed gum to her naked torso and photographed herself. These scars were also exhibited as sculptures. Wilke made a video work which shows her in her apartment, mostly on the phone to a man with home it seems evident she is flirting. The film is a study in body language and how the viewer interprets it.
Rosler, Martha
Amercian artist who began her career in the seventies and made a number of seminal photographci and text works. Rosler juxtaposes text and image to make work that highligths social inequity and challenges assumptions. 
She is best known for her seminal work the Bowery project. This consists of a series fo photographs paired with text which juxtapose words or phrases used to describe people who live on the streets with images of the area in which they live. So that an image of a banking hall doorway will be paried with the phrase ‘down and out’. 
An early collaborative work called Metal Box Factory is a sytematic survey of a number of employees in a factory aroudn 1970. Rosler asks the workers to write down their daily schedules both at work and home. These are displayed and a comparision made the time spent working and at home. The shcedules reveal the amount of work done in the home after work moslty by the women to be almost as much as the work done in the factory. Clocking on cards and produciton line rotas are also part of the display of material that the installation consitss of. Rosler made a series of collages showing inventer domestic interiors which are (ofthn humourous) comments on the division of labout in the home and the role fo the housewife. 
Rosler is one to the earliest artists to make politicised feminst work which presented a critique of society

Performance art often combined video and installation. Some artists like Nauman had a multi-dsciplinatry pracitse. 

Nauman Bruce
American artist who began his career in the 1960s making performance, installation, sculpture and video work. In fact Nauman made some of the earliest video art, in the days when the technology was new and only TV broadcast equipment was available. This was bulky and heavy, and editing equipment was not readily available. Nauman was also one of the first artists to consider the TV monitor as an art object in itself, as seen in his seminal corridor piece. This consists of two 4 metre high freestanding white walls, placed next to each other with a gap in between. The walls are at a slight angle so that the gap between them narrows gradually until it is the width of a monitor, which shows only white interference. The viewer approaches the work from the wider end, the light form the monitor creates a hypnotic effect so that the viewer feels compelled to enter the corridor and walk as far as they can before they no longer fit the space. In some ways the piece represents the familiar tunnel with a white light at the end that is often described by those who have had a near death experience. 
IN the 60s and 70s, Nauman made several performed works that are usually seen as video documents. The most well known is Taking a Line for a Walk, in which Nauman is seen walking around a square marked on the floor. Another work, a development of this piece, is a 4-meter high white box round which the viewer can walk. A CCTV monitor is placed at each corner, on which the viewer sees himself or herself passing the previous monitor. The film playing on the CCTV monitor is played back at slight delay, so that the viewer always sees themselves following them, but at the same time, since they are walking round a square, the implication is that they ought to be able to catch up with themselves. 
Nauman made other video works, for example of himself dressed as a clown laughing uncontrollably for 30 minutes. The way the video is presented is always important; in this case for example the monitor is in a wooden cubicle. In the 1980s he began to make more static sculptural work, and began a series in which he cast dogs in rubber and displayed them in various ways, for example hanging from the ceiling in a carousel like structure. 
In the 1990s Nauman made a large-scale video installation for which he filmed himself head and shoulders, talking to himself, and presented a number of these films, on 2metre high flat screen monitors upside down.
Cinema and the work of experimetnal film-makers had a great influence on many artists and with the advent of early video technology the distinctino between art and film became blurred.
Paik Nam June
American artist who was one of the first to make and show video as a sculpture. Paik was perhaps the first artist to broadcast his work on national TV, which he did by purchasing advertising slots. The work most often associated with him is a robot out of TV monitors. Paik foresaw the ubiquity of television culture from its earliest days. 
Wegman William
American artist who made a number of videos in the 1970s featuring himself and his pet dog, a Weimaraner named Fay Wray. The dog performs a number of actions, such as licking up a line drawn in milk on the studio floor, which Wegman himself also carries out considerably less efficiently. Two similar dogs sit face on to the camera following something off screen intently in perfect unison, the object of the dogs attention moving sometimes slowly and sometimes dramatically and violently. The videos are filmed in black and white in a single shot with no editing.
!!EXPERIMENTAL FILM.
!Kenneth Anger.
Underground filmmaker born in the 50s whose best known film Scorpio Rising had an influence on a whole generation of artists and filmmakers.
A number of aritsts made site specific work that, like performance art relied on photogrphic documentation for dissemination and permanence. This becaeme known as land art. 
LAND ART
Oppenheim Dennis
American artist working from the late 1960s onwards, much of Openheim's work was site-specific land art with a performative endurance element. For example in one of Openheim’s best-known works, Trench, he suspends his outstretched body by pressing with his hands and feet against the top of two opposite walls of a purpose built trench. In another he lies in the sun until he is burned and changes colour, calling this the act of making the artwork.  
Smithson, Robert
American artist who began working in the 1960s and made Spiral Jetty, a major land art work, in 1970. Smithson's work is nearly always site specific and involves him in altering the natural environment either by rearranging it or by placing mirrors so that the landscape reflects itself. He is interested in the perspective of the map, in terms of making the ground his work surface, or picture plane and creating ‘images’ which like the topography represented by a map, are only visible for the air. In one sense, then, his work is intentionally invisible. Smithson’s use of mirrors is a development of this interest in making formal rearrangements of the natural world. The mirror works occupied a variety of locations, including rural ones and one in Manhattan. Large mirrors are stood on the ground at specific sites.
Spiral Jetty is an earthwork made by taking several tonnes of small rocks to a salt lake and building a walkway, which leaves the beach and follows a very large spiral, crucially only visible form the air. Spiral Jetty has over the years been submerged by the lake and now ceases to exist. However it was documented by photographs and a film like all Smithson’s work and this is the form in which his work is most commonly experienced. In fact Smithson is very interested in the dichotomy between the reality of his work experienced in situ and in a photograph (which shows the work in an entirely different way). He made a number of other earthworks and plans for earth works, as we as work using maps.
Miss Mary
American artist born in the late 1940s who made a number of earthwork sculptures, for example a cube shaped void sunk into the ground its top exactly level with the surface, entered on ramps from ground level. She subverted minimal sculptural forms being made by her contemporaries by making them absolutely concrete but invisible. 
! Agnes Denes.
US artist who planted and cultivated a field of wheat in Manhattan on a two acre landfill site in 1982. Denes wanted to make a point about world trade, the economics of food production and famine. 
!Walter De Maria. 
Known for his permanent installation Lightning Field 1987, in Arizona in US, a specific area which has the highest frequency of lightning strikes in the world in which he set out a square mile of lightning conductor poles linked by conductive wires. When struck the field becomes a spectacle of blue light and electrical activity.
!!SITE SPECIFIC ART
! Gordon Matta Clark. 
American artist born in 1940s working from 1960s onwards. Matta Clark is best known for his work House, a suburban clapboard house that the artist cut in two vertically down the centre. The two halves lean outwards and away from each other. Matta Clark made a number of other works which involved cutting through buildings, for example a project at the opening of the Pompidou Centre in Paris he cut several circular and elliptical holes through a nearby building that was about to be redeveloped. Although major in terms of their execution, Matta Clark’s interventions are visually extremely simple.
!Christo and Jeanne Claude.
Beginning in the 1970s French artist Christo and his partner Jean Claude began wrapping objects, to transform one thing into another, solidify forms, change a hard form to an apparently soft one, graduating to larger and larger objects. They began to work with fabric on a large scale, either wrapping buildings or the landscape. They used parachute silk, vinyl and other kinds of industrial fabric with the required properties. Several works exemplify what they do best. For example, valley curtain, made in the early 80s, a large orange curtain was hung across a valley somewhere in US, probably California. Running fence is a mile long fence of red fabric that follows the contours of a hill. A large number of table umbrellas were installed dotted over a hillside. The island skirts surround a number of minute islands, following the shoreline and floating on the water for 30 metres or so. As with all their work, the proportions of the intervention always take the view from a distance into account, since the work by its nature temporary finally only exists as a documentary photograph it is therefore made with this photograph in mind. Thus the island skirts were designed to be wide enough to be clearly visible in an aerial photograph. And the locations are very carefully chosen with the final photograph in mind. In the 1990s Christo and jean Claude completed their most ambitious projects, the wrapped Reichstag and the wrapped Pont Neuf. These structures were wrapped in a fabric that was light enough to fold and gather around the forms they covered, and bound with rope. The Pont Neuf was wrapped in a gold fabric. The resulting transformation was a triumph of planning, negotiation, construction, and there are still postcards of the work on every Parisian postcard rack.
!!FIGURATIVE PAINTING 
In Britian early twentieth century many fugruatvie painters egnaged in a gritty realims that echoed the post-war reality. 
Spencer, Stanley
Britsih painter who was working between the 1930s and 60s. Spencer painted English rural life and religious subjects, often combining the two. He often showed god or Jesus in a scene set in an English village. The paintings are sometimes large and the composition is crowded. Spencer’s figures are often solidly built with heavy limbs, almost statue like, and he kept to a palette greys, browns and ochre’s.
!Hitchens Ivon.
British painter working from the 1940s onwards, died in the 1980s. Hitchens painted landscapes and vegetation and flowers with loose flowing brushstrokes leaving parts of the canvas unpainted. He favoured subdued earth colours, purples, blues and greens. Hitchens 
!Bratby John.
British painter working in the 1960s and 70s, one of a group known as kitchen sink painters for their choice of domestic subject matter. Bratby's interiors showed his own home and family, painted with thickly encrusted painted often squeezed direct from the tube and with a palette knife, in an almost naïve style.
Lowry A S
British artist Lowry lived and worked in the North of England and painted scenes of everyday and working class life in the cities of the North. He became known as a kind of peoples painter, a painter of ‘matchstick men and matchstick cats and dogs’ immortalised in a popular music song. Lowry’s showed the grey light of northern winters and the relentless coming and going of daily life, showing buildings and a multitude of tiny people walking past. The people are painted in two or three brushstrokes, suggestions of figures so that Lowry could paint a great number of them on one painting. The paintings are often predominantly grey or white the figures in dark colours with little hints of bright colours.
!Freud Lucian.
British figurative painter born in the 1930s, Freud began his career precociously in his teens and was still painting in 2003. He paints portraits, usually of models, sometimes of friends, family, and sometimes of well known people, although he rarely accepts commissions. His models are almost always nude, or semi-clothed and painted in accurate detail, but with a psychological perspective. They are often lying down. The predominant colours of the paintings are consistent: skin tones, and the greys and browns of his studio are the background to all his works since he never paints anywhere else. A painting may take up to 20 or so sittings, and some sitters are not able to continue. He has often painted a favourite model repeatedly. 
Some of Freud’s paintings might be of two people lying together, or there may be a dog, often a greyhound. He favours gaunt loose skinned physiques but has also painted larger people. He has painted many self portraits. 
Freud’s subjects include his lovers, his models (sometimes the two are the same), his daughters, artist Leigh Bowery, the Queen of England, and the fashion model Kate Moss.
!Bacon Francis.
Very influential British painter born in 1920s. Bacons paintings are conceptual but theatrical figurative works with abstract qualities. They are nearly always portraits, in which the figure is distorted, often to grotesque effect, perhaps looking if they have been melted or brushed away, appearing to be in discomfort. The figures are rendered with rough brushstrokes, and are placed on a contrasting flat colour background, often within a delineated ‘three dimensional space’. Bacon made many self-portraits, one of his best known works is the diptych Three screaming Popes 1960? Three large canvases each with a portrait of a pope in a cope and mitre, within a kind of abstract geometric frame structure, on dark orange and red grounds. The faces of the popes are blurred and distorted. He also painted a dog which is either curled up or chasing its tail, as with Bacons other work, there is an impression of movement as if a photograph has been taken on a long exposure and the sitter has moved, leaving a trail of the movement on the image.
