Bacon was almost as well known for his lifestyle as for his work. A heavy drinking habitué off bars in Soho in London he lived a solitary and obsessive working life, rarely spending a day without painting. His studio was famously untidy and dirty. Bacon died in the 1990s.
Patingin had begun to aspire to the condition of photography, particularly in America. This was to become a major theme in the work of Richter and others later on but intially there were only a few interested artists. Photorealist painters literallly mimicked the appearance of a photograph.
! Chuck Close.
Close initially made photo-realist paintings but he is best known for his gridded paintings which although they pre-dates digital technology have the appearranced of pixellated images. Inspired by the printing process and the way an image is broken down into dots of three colours, Close developed a way of placing a grid of squares of about 3 x 3 cm over the image he wanted to paint, very often a self portrait. He then produced the image by painting each square a different colour so that from a distance the image would be readable but close up the coloured squares would be predominate. In doing so he was investigating both the effect of printing and painting, making a work that looked photographic, as painting had been trying to do, but that was neither painting in the most obvious sense or photograph. This was after all another way of doing what painting has often been concerned with, namely applying colours in such a way that an image is effective from a distance which inevitably means that it wont be so close up. All this work was made before the digital revolution; after this Close’s paintings look like exactly like pixels. As well as paintings, Close also worked on paper in watercolours. He made a series of self-portraits using black and greys that emulate black and white photos. 
!Hockney David.
One of the best known of all British twentieth century artists. Hockney was born in the 1940s and began his career in the 1960s, being successful while he was still at college. His paintings are figurative, at this early stage he often added text as in a work titled We Two Boys Together Swinging with the title in the painting in white letters.
 Hockney made a painting called The Bigger Splash, which is instantly familiar. It shows an empty swimming pool and diving board with a villa in the background. Hockney uses acrylic paint and the pool is rendered in flat aquamarine. There is a splash in the water as if someone had just dived in, painted in white paint, perhaps one of the best representations of a splash in the history of painting. Hockney went to live in Los Angeles and the swimming pool became his favourite subject. He became very good at painting water and also painted in watercolour on tiled sheets of watercolour paper. He continues to paint in watercolour in 2003, perversely making large scale full length portraits in this apparently unsuitable medium. 
Drawings constitute a major part of Hockney’s oeuvre. He used a single confident line, pencil or pen, and also made a number of lithographs and etchings. His drawings are restrained and monochrome in comparison to his often bright paintings. He drew his friends and lovers incessantly, and his mother, as well as still lives, flowers, landscapes, and animals, in particular cats and dogs. In fact Hockney’s work is extremely traditional in terms of his subject matter, it was rather his means of execution that attracted attention.
Another well known painting by Hockney is Mrs and Mrs Clarke at Home, a double portrait of Ossie and Celia Clarke, fashion designer friends of Hockney’s, seated on opposites of a bay window in their London home, a cat next to them.
Hockney also designed a number of stage productions fro opera companies, notably the Magic Flute and The Rakes Progress. He painted backdrops and flys, as well as designing costumes. 
Hopper Edward
American artist who worked from the 1940s to the 1970s. Now dead. Hopper painted American life, mostly but not all urban scenes. He was particularly good at painting night scenes. His painting showing a bar at night is very well known. The bar is long and low, green on the outside with mostly black on the canvas, but glowing with bright yellow light from its interior. Inside is a woman waitress and a few solitary male customers. Another painting shows a petrol station at night, another a truck on a lonely road stopped by a payphone, caught in the headlights of an oncoming car. Hopper seems to depict a melancholy loneliness that he found in modern life. His paintings are extremely popular. They are painted in a realistic yet painterly way.
Rockwell Norman
American artist who made paintings that represented the American way of life, and who became synonymous with a certain idealised view of America. Rockwell painted rural scenes depicting life on farmsteads, domestic interiors, fireside scenes and landscapes. A particularly well-known work is a painting of a meadow filled with poppies in which a little girl is sitting. H
Rockwell painted in a highly realistic illustrative style avoiding any reference painting as object. He spent his career making impeccably painted record of America between the 1940s and 60s, any many of his images have entered the national consciousness.
Kahlo Frida
Mexican artist born in 1920s who made figurative paintings, largely self portraits. Kahlo depicted her somewhat tragic life in her paintings, which are graphc and detailed. She shows herself in many guises, with her distinguishing eyebrows which meet in the middle, and her long braided hair and native Mexican dress. She details her gynaecological problems and back problems stemming from a trolley bus accident as a young girl (which eventually took her life prematurely in the 1950s) in her paintings. In particular a number of miscarriages (she shows the foetus outside her womb, still attached by the umbilical cord, for example) and also the restraining corset which she wore to support her spine in her last years. Kahlo's discomfort and anger at her body’s inability to fulfil its biological destiny, coupled with a feminist fury at the obstacles faced by a woman trying to succeed as a serious painter at that time, also feed into her work. Kahlo’s paintings are disturbing, often horrific but she adds to this her bright colours, her love of pattern and beauty. 
Kahlo lived with the Mexican muralist Diego Rivera for part of her life. 
!Guston Philip.
American painter born in the 1930s who made abstract expressionist work at the beginning of his career in the 1950s and early 60s. Guston had a crisis of belief in abstraction and began to paint figuratively. He began to be successful from this point, but was only really recognised much later, in the 1980s. Guston developed a distinctive personal iconography that included cigarette butts, ashtrays, horseshoes, shoes, and Klu Klux Klansmen’s pointed hats, painted in a cartoon like style with heavy black outlines. The objects in particular can be seen as self portraits. Paintings include a pile of shoes, old and battered boots with the odd horseshoe, an ashtray overflowing with giant very fat cigarette butts, and a series of paintings of figures wearing Klansmen’s hats the figures often facing each other as trying to converse. Guston typically used either a monochrome palette of grey, brown, black and white (for example in a painting of a pile of horseshoes), or a bright palette. His bright colours are blue, red and a fairly bright light pink, the pink often used as a background. Some of the Klansmen paintings are black on white. 
Guston depicts a lonely world, yet not without a bleak humour, spending long hours in the studio smoking and trying to communicate with the rest of the world.
!Fernandez, Sam.
American artist working in the 1960s and 70s, Fernandez made psychedelic paintings, which were sometimes used for record covers. They consist of a richly dense background of patterns and flowers flowing into the main element of the image. Fernandez’s work influenced graphic design and was re-discovered in the 1990s by a new generation of artists.
Spero Nancy
American artist who makes paintings which have small figures and are narrative. Spero also made a work called A Tribute to Anna Mendieta (the wife of artist Carl Andre who died when she fell from the 30th floor of a building, reputedly pushed by Andre). This work is a pair of red handprints at the top of the gallery wall, which are dragged in a red smear down the wall as if someone with bloody hands was trying to hold on.
!!EARLY TWENTIETH CENTURY SCULPTURE.
Moore Henry
British artist born at the beginning of the twentieth century. Moore began as a figurative sculptor, but later became a pioneer of semi-abstract sculptural form in Britain, and made many public commissions. Like his peer Barbara Hepworth, Moore’s sculptures often have holes through them and hollows; he too was interested in representing the ‘void’ sculpturally. He worked in stone, bronze, and clay. Unlike his contemporaries, however Moore did not make smooth surfaced works. His roughly modelled sculptures are like giant bones, and always refer clearly to the human form. For example a reclining nude (a favourite form for Moore) might be abstracted to from a series of voids and hollows, as if it were a piece of driftwood that had been worn away. A standing figure might have a large space where the belly would have been. Heads are featureless and the proportions of the body adjusted to suit the form that Moore was trying to achieve.
Moore’s sculptures are often sited in rural landscapes and parks, and indeed it was the way in which the human form could look as if it were part of and came from the landscape, and the landscape seen through the sculpture, that interested Moore. A late example of this is an arch (around 1960) made for a lakeside site in Hyde Park in London which seems to be constructed of three monumental bones, two standing on their ends, the other balancing across the two.
Moore made a large number of drawings both as preparatory sketches and in their own right. His drawings are realistic and anatomically accurate. As well as drawing the figure, he made a notable series of impressions of people sheltering from bombs in an underground station during world war two.
!Epstein Jacob.
British sculptor working between the 1950s and 60s, Epstein is now dead. His best known work is the quite sinister ‘Rock Drill’ in Tate Gallery in London, the head and torso of a futuristic automaton-like creature with a head shaped like an animal skull. The breastplate ribcage is carved with angular geometric lines. The head is turned to one side. Some of Epstein’s work reflects the agony of war. Epstein worked mostly in stone although he did make some bronze casts. Jacob and the Angel is a massive male and a female figure both with huge thick limbs embracing each other. It is carved from a tan coloured but translucent stone. Epstein made a similar carving of a prone figure, a little like an Egyptian mummy.
!Frink Elisabeth.
British artist born in the 1930s and who from the 1960s onwards has made bronze heads, busts and equestrian statues. The surface of the sculptures is usually roughly modelled. Frink is particularly known for her distinctive thin figures riding a horse bare back whose tiny heads have strangely flat features. These can be seen in many public sites in London. 
!De St Phalle Niki. 
French artist who makes large and small (ranging from table sized to outdoor public sculptures) papier mache and fibreglass sculptures of human and animal forms. These are somewhat like totem poles or other tribal art objects, being mainly highly stylised white smoothed forms which are richly decorated with hand painting particularly on the head and limbs. 
!Bourgeois Louise.
Bourgeois is an American sculptor born in the 1920s. She has had one of the longest careers of any female artists of her generation. Bourgeois has made works that are both intimate and monumental in scale. She has made carved and cast sculpture and installations. Notable are her carved marble works of the 50s and 60s often table top, organic curvy shapes suggestive of the inside of the body or of plant growth. One is in pink marble. Installations include room structures that have furniture and objects in them. Her work has a strong female sensibility. Most recently she has made a monumental 30 metres high bronze spider. Bourgeois has many repeatedly used motifs, the shoe, the spider etc 
!Flanagan Barry.
British sculptor working from the 1960s onwards, first mainly making semi-abstract stone sculptures until the 1980s when he began to make figurative work and took the hare as his motif. Flanagan achieved great success with a series of sculptures featuring the figure of a hare roughly modelled in clay, its legs exaggerated in length, cast in bronze, often painted in ultramarine, in a running leaping pose, set upon a plinth made of a crisscrossed rectangle of lengths of planed wood. This hare and plinth combination was repeated in many different materials.
Keinholz Ed
Artist working in from the 1960s to 80s, Keinholz created several installations including the Beanery, a tiny kiosk set up as bar which the viewer could enter. The space inside was disorientating. Keinholz's installations usually featured life-size figures cast from plaster, unpainted, and real furniture, fittings and fabric.
!Hanson Duane.
American artist who began making his very well known life casts of people in the 1970s. Hanson’s fibreglass casts of himself and others are sculptures equivalent of the photo realist painting. The casts are painted, have real hair added and are dressed to look as if they are real. They do indeed prompt a double take in the viewer. Hanson chose very ordinary subjects, for example a police officer, a museum attendant, a pair of overweight and badly dressed American tourists, a motorbike accident victim, prostrate with his bike, a cleaning woman. They are uncanny in their ability to fool the viewer.
Jones Allen
British artist born in the 1940s, Jones achieved notoriety in the 1960s and 70s for his sculptures of women as furniture. Feminists in particular found his work hard to accept and he fell from popularity during the 1980s. Jones continues to make his work, the images of some of his sculptures are known all over the world. 
Jones’ sculptures are made by taking body casts of women. These are cast in fibreglass, spray painted realistically and other materials added. The sculptures are very beautifully made. A characteristic work is a coffee table – A glass top is placed on the back of a kneeling woman who is wearing a basque, stockings and suspenders and extremely high heels. The clothes and shoes are real. Other furniture includes a similar table top placed on the shins of a woman lying on her back with her legs bent at hip and knee, shins parallel to the floor and bound with cords to form a ‘z’ shaped support. 
Jones explored the idea of woman as support creating stools (a woman crouched over and bound with an specially made upholstered leather seat strapped on to her back, for example) and other kinds of furniture as well as paintings. The women are rarely depicted without sex shop lingerie, vertiginous heels and a lot of makeup. They always have perfect figures, in short they are images from pornography. The sadistic quality and implications of Jones’ work did not endear him to generations of women in the 70s and 80s although his work was enduringly popular. Jones himself refuted any criticism and rarely acceded to the demands to justify his work.  
Spoerri Daniel 
Italian sculptor who was part of the Arte Povera movement in the late 1960s and early 1970s. Spoerri is best known for his upside down table wall piece, a table top strewn with the remains of a meal – wine glass with dregs, dirty plate, brimming ashtray, all fixed to the table and defying gravity. 
!Cornell, Joseph.
American twentieth century artist who made work in boxes. Cornell’s boxes vary in size and materials. They sometimes have doors, or interior shelves or sections. The boxes are often painted, covered in fabric or leather, and contain found items and printed material. They are three dimensional collages.
Abstract sculptors found a wide range of new materials and working methods, expanding the definition of sculpture.
!Benglis Lydia.
An American sculptor working in the 1970s, Benglis made sculptures from brightly coloured polyurethane foam, poured onto the floor and allowed to expand and cohere.
!Bontecou Lee.
Working in the 1960s and 70s Bontecou made wall based constructions from metal, acrylic sheet, perspex and rivets. These were often flower like but due to their materials their appearance was more hard core, like an exploded aircraft fuselage.
!Hesse Eva.
American artist born in the 1940s died in 1969. Hesse’s prodigious output is seen as an important counterpoint to the prevailing, new and radical minimal sculpture of the time, and as a way point for women artists who were in the minority, particularly in this field. Hesse combined current sculptural preoccupations with unorthodox, and traditionally feminine materials and methods. Her achievement was recognised very early and she was included in several seminal exhibitions including one curated by Lucy Lippard, defining the new work. 
Hesse began her career around 1960 as a painter, training under ??. She made figurative paintings and self portraits, gradually turning to abstraction, until she had a crisis of confidence in her ability to paint. Hesse ceased to make work until she and her husband travelled to Dusseldorf where a German industrialist collector had offered them a studio residency in a disused factory that he owned. There she found an abundance of materials and machine parts lying around, and she began to draw these parts and experiment. Working in the factory Hesse produced a number of odd abstract drawings based on the machine parts. She began to make wall based three dimensional paintings, modelling the surface of the work and adding string following the shapes, as well as projecting elements, the whole painted. Then she went on to make sculptures with the materials she found on the factory floor. Her first successful ones were cubes of steel mesh sheets, open on one side. Hesse pushed equal lengths of rubber tubing through the holes in the mesh in the manner of rug tufting, to create a furry effect on the interior of the box. This painstaking task produced 2 sculptures (one with black tubing, one with transparent tubing), which combined the (traditionally female) techniques of handcraft with those (traditionally male) of industrial fabrication used to make minimal sculpture. Hesse continued to make wall reliefs from modelling materials, string and paint including Rosy, a breast like form on a buff coloured round with a spiral of string painted in graduated shades from pink to red. Hesse’s interest in concentric circles was worked in these reliefs and in her Dot graph paper drawings, delicate pencil or pen drawings in which rectangles are filled with row of tiny small circles or rows of concentric circles coloured in with pencil. 
The string forms resulted in a wall work consisting of a row of about 25 hemispheres 30 cm in diameter covered with string spiralling round, placed in a horizontal line on a long strip about 40 cm wide, at picture height on the wall. From the centre of each hemisphere, cords project and extend to the floor where the ends lie. The whole piece is painted grey.
Hang up (1967) is a seminal work by Hesse. It consists of a two metres by one and a half metres empty frame on the wall, bound with cloth bandage and painted light grey. Form the frame projects a heavy gauge wire also wrapped with cloth bandage attached on the bottom right and top left corners and looping out about two metres form the wall and back again. The viewer may stand inside the loop, in which case they seem to be lassoed by the work. This work is an abstract but concrete representation of the nature of looking at paintings.
String was one of Hesse's favoured materials along with latex and fibreglass. She made a string mesh consisting of lengths of cord dipped in latex and strung from the ceiling to form an abject enlarged untidy web. She took net shopping bag and stuffed them with scrunched up plastic sheeting, hanging them in clusters, which are strangely anthropomorphic, on the wall. She photographed herself lying on a bed covered with a mass of string like spaghetti. 
Hesse also used rubber tubing which she filled with plaster to create sausage like forms. Her work often made allusion to the male and the female body. 
The preoccupation with tubular forms and repetition characterises Hesse’s later works, Accession and Accretion. Accession consists of two black 50 cm square panels mounted on the wall with a space between them filled with a mass of silver wires. Accretion is a row of one and a half long fibreglass tubes, slightly crumpled, leaning against the wall placed at regular intervals.
Similar works are a casual arrangement of caved in fibreglass cylinders about 70 cm high and 50 cm wide standing on their ends on the floor, and rows of latex spheres on a sheet.
Hesse died prematurely of a brain tumour.
!Flavin Dan.
American artist working in the 1970s and 80s, Flavin took fluorescent tubes and the coloured light they produce as his medium. He created sculptures by leaning the tubes against the wall sometimes two or three, sometimes a whole row, sometimes placed casually at different angles, sometimes measured and at the same angle. Some tubes might be placed on the floor. The transformers and cables are very visible and are part of the work, something which is commonplace now but was not when Flavin made these works. Examples of works are a row of cold white tubes leaning at a slight angle against the wall, a formation of cold blue coloured tubes mounted on the wall in a cruciform, which shines out its light giving the impression of something celestial or perhaps extraterrestrial. One work uses multiple bluish white tubes placed on the wall to form a crucifix shape. Flavin became skilled at using the colours of the light as sculptural form.
Lin Maya 
Lin, an American artist, was chosen by competition to design the Vietnam war memorial in Washington. She designed a long highly polished black marble wall, inscribed on which in columns are the names of all those who lost their lives in combat in the Vietnam war. The wall is set into the ground at ground level, and appears to be gradually revealed as the ground in front of it is excavated to slope down. The top of the wall remains level parallel with the ground and the viewer descends passing along the wall which becomes higher in relation to them the further along the wall they walk.
The memorial is extremely popular, it ahs been profoundly successful in conveying the feelings of a generation of the bereaved and indeed of those who did not believe that America should have fought in Vietnam. Perhaps this is due to its simple formal qualities and its gentle interactivity with the viewer. The memorial invites participation and takes the viewer down below ground level, to see the names symbolically buried but rising up to meet the light. Its surface is reflects the viewers, so that a multitude of face appear on its surface. 
The tradtional methods of casting and carving continued to be poplular, but many sculptors began to use welded steel. 
Kelly Ellsworth
US sculptor working born in 1930s best known for his 1960s – 80s work, wall mounted steel shapes, distorted rhomboid, parallelogram, triangular, immaculately spray painted in matt primary colours and black and white, and butted up together one colour and shape against another to create abstract works which are both sculpture and painting and have a contemplative lyrical presence. 
Kenny Michael
British artist born in the 1940s died 1990s. Kenny made wood and metal abstract sculpture.
Smith David
British artist working from the 1960s onwards who made large welded steel sculptures.
King Philip
King is a British artist who made large steel sculptures between the 1960s and 80s. King made a number of public commissions many of which can still be seen, for example there is one in Fulham in London, a red painted tubular steel structure a bit like a climbing frame. King also made the designs for the decoration of the walls of Embankment underground station in London
!Dalwood Hubert.
British sculptor born in early 1930s died in the 1970s. Dalwood made carved, cast and constructed sculptures from stone, bronze, acrylic resin, acrylic sheet and metal. He also made a few paintings. His early works were totemic stone objects that owed something to Jacob Epstein. He began to make tabletop sculptures that were a bit like mini abstract sculpture gardens, cast in aluminium or bronze. He used tubular or square forms and wavy lines placed on a rectangular base that would be cast in one piece. He made sculptures from Plexiglas with metal wire threaded through. 
As he became successful Dalwood began to be commissioned and he made a number of public works. One example is a construction in a park made from brushed steel tubes of a large diameter fixed vertically to the ground in a cluster, reaching about 5 metres in height, curving around at the top like giant geometric plant stalks. Or an egg shaped bronze sculpture about one and a half metres in length lying on its side with some detail on its surface.
!Calder Alexander.
British sculptor working in the 1940s to 60s who became best known for his kinetic sculpture and mobiles. One of the very few artist to have used the mobile successfully as a form in sculpture. Calder’s sculptures generally use circles spheres and triangular shapes, often the triangles have curved sides, and rods. They are metal, painted typically in black and white with red. yellow and blue.
!Caro Antony.
British artist who began his career in the 1950s becoming very well known in the 1960s for his welded steel sculptures. Died at the very end of the twentieth century?? Caro was the most successful of a group of British sculptors who made large scale abstract steel sculptors in the 1960s and early 1970s. These were either cut out of steel plate, or made of round and square cross section steel tubes, rods and I-beams. They were sometimes left to rust, but sometimes spray painted primary colours. Caro was one of the first sculptors to make semi-abstract works on such a large scale. It was really only Henry Moore who had attempted this in Britain but his work was much more??. Caro was influenced by the scale of ambition of his American contemporaries but his subject matter was initially the English landscape. His sculptures although they appear abstract depict landscapes. Caro used steel to make very open structures, often rather long and low, about 12 metres long and two metres high, that describe a kind of vista in space. One might be painted bright green, another bright yellow. Smaller works might be made from shapes cut from sheet steel put together. The ragged edge from the cutting heat tool or saw is often left unfinished.
In the 1990s Caro made a number of small steel sculptures of a chair based on the chair in Van Gogh's painting of his own bedroom. Caro became synonymous with a school of sculpture that was predominant in Britain for a decade, not least due to his years of teaching at St Martins school of art in London, then a leading sculpture school. This type of sculpture was mainly made by men and it began to be less popular as the effects of 1970s feminist activism in visual art took hold in the 1980s. Furthermore the status of the antonymous art object began to be questioned during this decade. 
!Chadwick Lynn.
British artist who worked in bronze, making figurative sculpture from the 1950s to the 1980s. Chadwick's bronzes range from life size to very small around 30 cm high human figures. These are angular, with stick like legs and flattened irregular trapezoid bodies. The heads of the figures are often pyramid or cube shaped to denote female and male figures. Chadwick was interested in the difference between male and female forms of / in body language. The bronzes mostly have a blue green coppery patina. 
!Chamberlain John. 
Working in the 1960s US artist Chamberlain made sculptures from compressed dumped cars which he either retrieved from the compressing dump or took to be compressed. The resulting cubes were exhibited as sculpture. He sometimes extracted compressed parts of cars which he combined to make into sculptures. The Spanish artist Angela de la Cruz’s trashed canvases can be considered in relation to Chamberlains work.
!Chillida Eduardo.
Mexican Sculptor born in the 1930s who made monumental steel sculptures usually site specific, often in natural environments. For example, one of his characteristic M or comb like structures is placed on a cliff top to rust and acquire its orange red colour. 

!Dion Mark.
 American artist who is interested in anthropology, material culture, ecology and natural history. His usually large scale multi part installations are the result of research projects into specific locations or subjects. For example in London he searched an area of the muddy Thames riverbank above and below the timeline with the painstaking care of an archaeological dig. The resulting ‘artefacts’ or trash was cleaned up and exhibited in a number of vitrines and display cases in the Tate Gallery.
!Gilbert and George.
British artists born in the 1950s who have lived and worked together in a longstanding partnership. They began to work together in their twenties, when they moved into a house in London’s East End and began their life as art existence rigidly regulated by both banal and eccentric habits. They became as well known for their lifestyle, and their deadpan personae as for their work. In the early years of their careers Gilbert and George rarely spoke about their work or lives together unless in a very cryptic manner. George, who has a strong eastern European accent, did all the talking. Gilbert spoke only in the most stilted manner in an upper class accent. They parried all interviewers’ questions by assuming an impenetrably eccentric manner. It was never clear whether or not they were having the last laugh. 
Gilbert and George lived together in a sparse house, wore tweed suits, hats and overcoats, ate in the same café every day of the year, and were never seen without each other.
Their early work was performance. They held many soirees with invited performers, poetry readings, all conducted with an anachronistic formality, including the letterpress invitation cards. They became well known for their work ‘underneath the Arches’, made in about 1975, for which they became ‘singing sculptures’. They stood, their clothes and bodies entirely covered in silver paint, on a plinth underneath the arches of One of the bridges over the Thames and sang the music hall song of the same name continuously. 
In the 1980s Gilbert and George began to make the collaborative photographic work for which they are now known. They began to make photo collages by taking self portraits, in which they appeared full length, usually one on each side of the image. The kind of symmetry found in religious diptychs seemed to be a reference point. 
Initially these were small black and white works, the subject matter images of London, flowers and plants, often repeated in a mirror image. Later they began to photograph young men and boys that they had met around London. The compositions began to have a scatological and sexual theme and were often directly abusive. Swear words began to appear. The artists themselves usually featured, the other men and boys often seemed to be submissive.
By the 1990s, Gilbert and George were making works that set out to shock, using images of human faeces and naked men, including themselves. 
The images are printed in square sections which are placed together in a grid to make a large image often 3 metres square. Each section has a thin frame usually red, and there is no pretence at being one large image.
Eventually what had been shocking about the works became its trademark. The Gilbert and George photo collage became an instantly recognisable trademark.


! Cosey Fanni Tutti.
British video and performance artist who has been working since the 1960s. Fanni Tutti’s most recent work is painting. The work for which she is best known is a series of live events made on her own and with the band COUM transmission (later to become Throbbing Gristle). Fanni Tutti often appeared half naked. One image is always associated with her showing her reclining on a bed, wearing underwear but bare breasted and wearing a blindfold. She made a number of photographic works imitating soft porn photography.

! Gillian Ayres.
British artist who made abstract paintings throughout the 60s to the 90s. Her work is brightly coloured, abstract impasto
! Richard Artschwager.
American artist born in 1940s who made sculptures of furniture in the late 70s and early 1980s. This was around the same time as Ettore Sottsass's Memphis group was making furniture from laminate in Milan. By letting a table be represented by a box covered in printed laminate legs and covered with a pink tablecloth, he rendered a useful object into an unusable sculpture constructed from banal materials used for cheap furniture and never for sculpture. Artschwarger also made a series of Chippendale chairs from laminate, their form flattened out and clad in unlikely patterns and designs. In these works he was interested in making unlikely combinations of generic form with generic material, forms from the classical era or from antiquity with modern materials. In doing this he was following the ideas of the architects Alice etc who wrote a book about the architecture of Las Vegas. The strip
!Abdul’allah, Faisal.
Artist who made an installation that invites the viewer to enter a large box like structure, those with light coloured eyes are to enter to the left, those with dark eyes to the right. Once segregated by genetic characteristic, the viewers find themselves in one of two spaces. One is a small room with highly polished metallic floor walls and ceiling in a copper coloured metal that functions like a mirror and creates a glowing light. On the floor is an inscribed text that addresses the viewer personally and asks them to consider their feelings on arrival in this space. 
The other space, which is the larger remainder of the box like structure, is pitch black. On entry it can be seen that the small room projects into this space, and is in fact constructed from a material that allows one-way vision. From the dark space the viewers in the small space can be seen clearly. Although this would seem like a privileged space due to the materials of its construction, the people in it seem to be imprisoned, regarded secretly by the watchers in the larger space. 
!Abts,Tomma. 
German painter born 1970, living in UK. Abts paints abstract geometric works which rely on a long period of reworking and on very carefully chosen colours. The surfaces of the works show reworking and over brushing, evidence of the time taken to make the paintings.
!Acres Reverend Ethan.
Ordained clergyman from California who also works as an artist, making live works that combine a religious service with performance, usually involving a sermon, and other performers.
!Act Up. 
Group of gay rights activists consisting of artists and others, worked in US in the 1980s, chiefly NY. They did Poster campaigns, subverted advertising, made use of official statistics to point out discriminatory practise. See guerrilla girls.
!Add N to X.
A musician duo of one male one female, both born in the 1970s, who made video works in the late 1990s to early 2000s, for example a continually changing colour grid controlled by the soundtrack which accompanied it. Also Angela Bulloch
!Affrassiabi Shahin.
British artist born 1960. Affrassiabi conceptual installations employ household items and materials to make formal sculptural constructions which reference other works of art.
!Aitchison Craigie.
Scottish painter born 1940s. Best known in the 1980s for the paintings of his pet Bedlington Terrier. The dogs are painted in profile on large brightly and flatly coloured canvases.
!Aitken Doug. 
American film and installation artist born in 1975. Aitken first worked in commercials and used the experience gained in this field to make a series of multi screen and projected video installations, winning a Golden Lion at the Venice Biennale in 2001. Diamond Sea featured a deserted mining town in Namibia, which is not marked on state maps to ensure that its location remains secret. A sense of the people who worked there is evoked (and their exploitation implicit) and a haunting empty beauty. Aitken makes his own soundtracks, which form an integral part of the installations, sometimes several films will play at once resulting in a blend of soundtracks. Another work, a film of people attending a travelling cinema in India, was screened in a tented room with sand on the floor. Aitken’s work deals with location and presence, with a sense of place being a priority.
!Akkerman, Philip.
!Allen Philip.
British painter born 1970s. Allen’s almost abstract paintings of the late 90s and early 2000s are a change from his previous representational work. The paintings have a roughly painted background and a multicoloured scallop motif on top, referencing landscape and abstract painting. 
British painter born in 1970s moved from figurative paintings to abstraction, making brightly coloured abstract landscapes painted roughly and lumpily, with more neatly painted lines of scallop shapes over the top. He makes reference to the impossibility of painting, a dilemma for many late 20th early 21st century artists.
!Allington Edward.
Allington is a British sculptor who was part of the group of artists born around 1950 making large scale ambitious work in the 1980s and 90s. His best known work is Horn of Plenty, a cornucopia of found objects. He uses the cornucopia extensively as motif, and was one of the artists invited to propose a work for the empty plinth in Trafalgar square in London in 2001.
!Almond Darren.
British artist born in 1970s. Almond is interested in time. He made a large clock of the type that has the 24 hour time display in figures that flip over. It shows the time going very fast so that a minute is a second and an hour is reached in a minute, and is to be mounted in a public institution or railway station. He also made a large aeroplane propeller painted white and mounted it horizontally on the floor of a gallery so that its blades nearly touch the walls leaving just enough room for a person to pass. He set up a live video link between a gallery and an empty? prison cell, permitting the viewer a fly on the wall experience of visiting a gallery to visit a prison cell. Almond also made a video titled Ghost Train, which is filmed in black and white in a ghost train at a fairground somewhere in Austria. Shot from one of the cars, the film is grainy and visually lyrical as the low light conditions of the video alter what the visitor can see. The sound of the train and the sound effects of the ride, which are edited with a musical soundtrack when represented, somehow demand a different attention from the viewer. Another work is a recreated bus shelter in a town where there was a Nazi concentration camp. The viewer is able to ‘wait for a bus’ to take them as a tourist to visit the camp, whilst pondering on the wait for transit
what else?
!Althoff Kai.
Born around 1960, makes bright water colour paintings on paper.
!Alys Francis.
Born in the 1950s, Brazilian or Mexican artist who made a series of works in the early 90s commissioning a sign painter to paint a sign, which he then set up in his studio. He then asked the painter to paint the scene including the sign. Interested in appropriation and reproduction.
!Andrews Michael.
British painter
!Antoni Janine.
American artist born in the late 1970s who has made a series of performance works and installations. Chocolate Gnaw and Lard Gnaw involved the artist chewing as much chocolate as she could from a one metre cube and melting the resulting chocolate to make chocolate pieces. The lard chewed from a similar sized block she made into lipsticks. The residual blocks are displayed as part of the work. She also cast busts of herself from the chewings. 
The chocolate and lipstick products were displayed in luxurious packaging in a mirrored and gilt edged display case of the kind that might be seen in a department store. She thereby made a connection between the basic act of chewing and eating, female relationships to food and body image (fat and chocolate) and cosmetics, and the role of packaging in the stimulation of desire. In ‘Harmony Hair Colour’ she used her long dark hair as a brush to make paintings with hair dye on large pieces of paper on the floor, referencing Yves Klein’s blue paintings made using women’s bodies as brushes. ‘Sleep’ was an installation in which Antoni slept at night, wired up to a machine which recorded her R E M patterns. She transferred the resulting line on the graph drawn by the machine on a continuous sheet of paper onto a specially constructed wooden loom in the same room and wove on it a white fabric with the sleep patterns as a blue line. The resulting fabric length was as long as the sleep graph whose length was defined by the number of days Antoni slept in the gallery. As soon as she had enough, Antoni made the fabric into a nightgown in which she then slept, sleeping clothed in her sleep as it were.
!Applebroog Ida.
American artist born in 1930s, Applebroog's paintings and free standing works are concerned with memory, childhood and narrative. She often uses the device of the predella form, coupled with the film negative, in an allusion to the votive object and the memory preserving photograph. The narrative of her work is often about childhood and hints at abuse, and it is very much the work of someone who is a mother. The paintings are often constructed from several canvases butted together. Freestanding works are propped on the floor upright or placed horizontally. Applebroog made a film with her daughter, film maker and artist Beth B, which is a loose narrative of unhappy childhood with a dreamlike quality.
!Aramesh Reza.
British artist born 1960s who works in mixed media, made paintings of the branches of trees, and installations with furniture and real stuffed animals.
!Arnold Liz.
British artist born 1963 died 2002. She painted women and anthropomorphised cats, very often with flowers, inhabiting story book universes, in strong colours with lots of black in a neat way. Arnold’s creatures inhabit the real world in an unreal way and often seem to have very serious but mysterious business to undertake. They are fashionably dressed and wear jewellery and have long eyelashes. Sometimes they were lab coats and there is a kind of science fiction atmosphere.
!Art and Language.
British artist duo born around 1950 known for their dry conceptualism dealing with art and text as a subject. A number of paintings with text added.
!Atkinson Conrad.
!Attila.
Russian artist born in 1960s. figurative, one of the few Russian artists to be commercially successful in the west during the 1990s.
!Audeoud Fabienne.
French artist born around 1968, Audeoud’s work comes from her interest in classical music specifically opera. Video works show Audeoud as a diva singing to the point of collapse, and as a fading diva on a self destructive binge in her bedroom. There is a spare and often painful quality to these works, which are superficially abject and comical making them serious in their effect on the viewer. A project to unlearn the piano took many months, in which she set herself the task of unlearning everything she knew about playing the piano. This, like many of her works, was done daily, all day, in a gallery. She filmed a brass band rehearsing, they are static but their discordance is at odds with their calm appearance. Other performances. Manifestos. She also made several collaborative painting exhibitions with former Bank artist John Russell. These were room sized canvasses painted quickly and fairly badly, figurative, vaguely narrative with text added.
!Baechler Donald.
!Bag Alex.
US artist who worked in video in the 1990s. Made a video about being an art student.
!Baker Bobby.
British performance artist born 1950 who came to prominence in the 1980s with her food performances. Baker returned to art in her late 40s after raising a family and training as painter, but began to make performance art when she found that a painter’s life was not compatible with bringing up a family. She also found that painting and its history did not offer her what she needed to make work about what interested her, namely the creativity inherent in domestic and family life. Baker sought a way to represent this creativity, which is routinely unacknowledged and deeply unfashionable as a subject for artists. In her first performance, ‘Drawing on a Mothers Experience’ she made a ‘painting’ by telling the story of the night one of her children was born through the food that was consumed by her before and during her labour, producing this food and using it to make a painting on a bed sheet and black treacle, steak used to ‘print’, milk, jam. 
The resulting work bore obvious and intentional comparison to an abstract expressionist work specifically Pollock. She then obliterated it by sieving icing sugar over it and rolled herself up in the sheet. It was all conducted with much humour and joking and audience interaction. Baker made a point about the traditionally male ‘work’ of painting by creating a painting which far from being a commodity is merely the residue of a creative act, ready to be washed and dried. In an Edible Family in a Mobile Home, Baker constructed a family from cake and icing and placed them in a caravan. She staged a performance in her own home, and invited the audience into her kitchen to watch her discuss menial domestic tasks and what she enjoys about them, for example the freshness of washed cabbage leaves and the fun of icing (she poses on a revolving cake stand). After each action she pins a symbolic item onto her white coat so that she is a living relic of her day at home. Apparently casual yet very choreographed. She also did a performance in which she gets into a bath of chocolate custard. Baker draws on the work of 1970s feminist artists who made the domestic their subject to point up the inequality of domestic work and its consequent effect on themselves as artists. However in her re-appropriation of this work focussing on food, eating and the artists body, she added humour making it substantially different.
!Balkenol, Stefan.
German artist who carves large, often over three metres high, figures from whole tree trunks. The figures are carved roughly and all the chisel marks are visible, no attempt being made to smooth the surface of the wood. The sculptures are painted. Balkenol has made site specific work such as the six metre high figures he placed on wooden piles in the river Thames in London. His carvings refer both to folk art and to historical plychromed sculpture whilst the figures are clearly of their time.
!Bank.
British collaborative artists John Russell, Millie Thompson and Simon Bedwell, born in the 1960s, worked as Bank in the 1990s until Russell left in 2001. Thompson and Bedwell continued to work as Bank until 2003. Bank produced a large body of painting, sculpture, printed matter and texts as well as curating several seminal group exhibitions. They directed their attentions at the art world making ironic and satirical work underpinned by a detailed knowledge of and love for art history, a sense of humour and a disrespect for the establishment. In fact this last could be seen as Bank’s trademark. 
Bank’s painting spanned over a range of styles including highly realistic still life featuring the detritus of their studio done in old master style, ‘bad’ figurative painting, ‘serious’ abstraction, and self portraits. They explored the world of painting as iconoclasts. Most exhibitions accompanied by a printed text in the form of a polemic on the state of art, of the world and of artists. Irreverent and downright rude, those (especially their targets) who failed to get the joke found nothing in Banks work for them.
Bank Newspaper was self-published for 10 years, featuring news about the art world, drawings and photographs on a tabloid model. Slanderous, even libellous, the paper had several favourite targets and no one was safe from their cruel wit.
In 2000 Bank published their ongoing fax back series as a book. For several years they had been returning press releases to the gallery that sent them, edited, corrected and with suggestions. These served to highlight the pomposity, inaccuracy, bad grammar and inflated claims made about artist by their dealers to hilarious effect. 
Curated exhibitions the first of which was in a bank giving the group their name were mostly held in Bank’s studio in London’s East End. Other shows were titled galleries poo poo, cocaine orgasm featured a life size self portrait sculpture of the group members on crucifixes?? Also skip By who? Lolly Batty, etc They showed a group of old master still lives in Cork Street and a group of abstract paintings featuring poured paint and some screen prints with text. In 2003 Thompson and Bedwell posed semi-naked for a series of photos dressed as classical figures from paintings but using their untidy studio as a background and anything to hand as costumes and props. 
!Banner Fiona.
Banner is a British Artist born in 1965 who uses text as her material, first coming to prominence with a work called ‘ the Hunt for Red October’ in which she wrote out the entire synopsis of the film of that name by watching it. Then she typeset this and printed it large scale on paper in landscape format. The net effect was of a text that could be seen to represent the entire film but was not really possible to read due to the line length, once the reader got to the end of a line it would be necessary to walk back along the work to find the beginning of the next. This produced a tendency to read vertically, skipping from line to line. Thus the viewer produced their own reading of the text which gave the impression of the film. 
Banner followed this with several similar film synopsis works, culminating in The Nam, a huge paperback book which contains her synopsis of the film Apocalypse Now. She also made an audio work of her reading this synopsis, which is produced in a flat vocal style rendering it difficult to listen to, but its strength lies in the effect on the listener…etc. Banners work has most usually referenced film, the book and the printed page, but she has also made work about songs and music, including a Bob Dylan song in a large work on canvases. The words of the song are spelt out in large stencil style letters on unprimed canvases three metres high, then cut out so that the canvas is mostly gone. The song stretches over several canvases.
Lately Banner has made several sculptural works in which she produces three dimensional versions of punctuation marks in particular fonts. These are carved from polystyrenes and painted or actually cast in bronze. She was a turner prize nominee in 2002 and showed a number of works dealing with pornography, including some in which she removed all the text from a narrative leaving only the punctuation.
!Bar Amotz Guy.
Israeli born artist born 1970s living and working in London. Bar Amotz’s work deals with sound and music. He makes sculptures that often incorporate speakers made from household objects, or especially made fibreglass structures, which have a hard core futuristic look about them. A giant karaoke machine to be entered by the viewer. An amplifier which is worn as a backpack housed in a rigid fibreglass lacquered shell. Bar Amotz sometimes uses metallic car spray to finish his objects giving them an ambiguous but functional appearance. In 2003 he made a machine to be worn by a dancer whose movements triggered various sensors, thus the movement created the music.  
!Barker Sally.
British artist born in 1960s who assumed an alter ego; Tela, a goddess or warrior queen. Barker performed as the goddess sitting on a throne, with acolytes, dressed in a cross between Roman and Viking costume, looking a bit like Boudicca, with gold sprayed greaves, wristbands and a helmet..
!Barney Matthew.
US artist living and working in New York born in 1960s. A sports enthusiastic, super fit from working out and rock climbing, his prolific work includes video, film, and sculpture. Came to attention at Documenta, Kassel in 1993 with a performance Drawing Restraint in which he was tethered to the back wall of a squash court with a rubber bungee and using his own strength to get himself to the opposite wall, leaving marks as a drawing. He also scaled a lift shaft in a multi storey car park naked, using climbing equipment, with a surgical/machine tool implement inserted in his anus. These performances were documented on video. 
They are followed by the sculptures made from petroleum jelly. This is known both as a medicinal aid and a lubricant with sexual associations, and Barney used it to cast sculptures of pseudo gym equipment, rendered very realistically and shown in refrigerated spaces. Like and perhaps even in a tacit acknowledgement of Joseph Beuys use of lard, petroleum jelly became a significant element of Barneys later work. The sculptures have a strange atmosphere – they seem made to be used, yet unusable, familiar yet mysterious, everyday yet perverse and slightly sinister and to allude to sexual practises.
Barney made a series of feature length films Cremaster I to V. These are shot in 35 mm to commercial cinema standards, but made by a tiny crew with the exception of the final film on a shoestring budget. The series explores the world of Barney symbolism, perversion, fetishistic, oneric, surreal. Started by making 2, then 1, 4, 5 and ends with 3. The films conceived as a whole and made in the order the artist wanted to over a period of 10 years. 
Cremaster 2 is set on an American football pitch. There is a long sequence of synchronised marching shot from above on a crane and from pitch level, featuring women dressed as glamorous grown up cheerleaders moving in tightly choreographed formation. They begin at the edges of the pitch and gradually form the shape of one of the Cremaster logos. Jewelled harp?
Cremaster 1 is filmed on a frozen lake on which a kind of snow fort has been constructed.
4 is filmed on the Isle of Man and take the Manx symbol the three legs of man and the annual TT motorbike race as its main subject. Barneys character is a kind of fawn with red furry legs and pointed ears, as well as a kind of goblin and a peasant girl and her parents. There are other exotic creatures and all have strange mutant genitalia, introducing a consistent theme throughout Barney's work. Much is filmed on giants’ causeway, and there are shaking giants footsteps. Barney says the film is based on a Celtic myth of a pebble being thrown into the sea and becoming an island and something to do with the giant of the causeway. 
5 set in the Guggenheim museum in New York and in the interior of an airship with stewardesses in very chic uniforms seated round a table with a large display of grapes. One of the stewardesses is under the table on a small plinths curled round the tables pedestal holding on precariously while trying and succeeding in making a hole in the table cloth above using hairpins and then gradually pulling grapes through. The grapes are red and white. She eats the grapes. This is intercut with animated film of grapes in lines forming various symbols. In the Guggenheim Barney, dressed as a Scottish piper in full ceremonial dress, slowly ascends the outside walls of the huge spiral stairway using climbing ropes and shackles. On each level different people and groups are encountered, for example a long line of can dancing women who place one extended leg on the edge of the balcony for Barney to crawl under, an extremely beautiful woman, and a small child.
Segment in empty warehouse with Norman mailer and lots of water and Ursula Andress in a black jewelled outfit. 
V is shot in the G??baths in Budapest, and features an opera singer. It is acknowledged be the least successful of the 5 and is the most ordinary in a sense. Barney wanted to make a film about singing. Features Ursula Andress
3 the most recently made is shot entirely on a set and features the Chrysler building in New York. The film opens with a long sequenced involving a hermaphrohditic almost dead person recently disinterred from a living grave being deposited in a black sedan in the centre of a hexagonal room which also contains a crow. The arena is eventually recognised as an empty floor high up in the Chrysler building, and the corpse to be Gary Gilmore. As we imagine the crow to be pecking out the eyes of whoever it is in the car, 5 large apparently driverless cars emerge from the corners of the space and begin a kind of ballet, repeatedly hitting and gradually crushing the black sedan until it is no more. Bar staffed by a stereotypical Irish comedic barman who in pouring Guinness becomes overwhelmed by foam. The main character (Barney) is a construction worker on the building involved in steel erecting of the top structure.
He is a young handsome Irish character who has some mysterious secrets, revealed when he visits 2 ‘doctors’ in a room in the building who reveals under his tattered clothes which appear to be Masonic – a leather apron with Masonic items in the pocket his mutant genitalia. He excretes a petroleum jelly ‘turd’ which immediately mutates. This is a scene of intense peculiarity.
As the top of the building is constructed the man is involved in a kind of wedding at the pinnacle, under a floral arch with ribbons which are finally unfurled and thrown over the side of the building to float down until they are caught by the workmen and drawn in throw the windows in a carefully choreographed order to create a spiral of ribbons on the exterior of the skyscraper as if it were a giant maypole. One the pair who is married has their head crushed by falling steel.
Barney says that this film brings all the others together and is about nationality and origin (he being the grandson of an Irish emigrant labourer who did indeed work on the Chrysler construction crew). The mutant genitalia, gym equipment and logos and symbols of all the films relate to the title of the sequence, Cremaster. The Cremaster is a muscle located somewhere between the scrotum and the anus, and it is somehow significant in both sexual performance and procreation, but most people are unaware of it. Barney treats the muscle as if it were one that could be worked, and as a metaphor for his own ideas about creativity and the individual.
!Bartolini Massimo.
Italian artist born second half of the twentieth century.
!Basquiat Jean-Michel.
Graffiti artist from the Bronx in New York, Basquiat was born in 1973 and died in 1998. He was discovered by a dealer who fostered him and provided him with studio and materials, enabling Basquiat to paint on canvas. He became hugely successful and his work commanded high prices. Feted by the art world for his work, his looks and his rebelliousness. He used graffiti tags combined with idiosyncratic signs and symbols creating a way of making a painting that successfully combined street and Afro-Caribbean consciousness with abstraction. The outline of a crown, a reference to his own self-determined kingship, was his signature, the skull often appeared. He mostly painted in black and bright colours on white ground, drawing in outline and rarely filling in, sometimes used white on black to give a blackboard effect. The apparent naïve style used to issue a series of commands and demands on the world and his viewers, which are both egotistical and irresistible. Perhaps unable to cope with his success he became addicted to heroin and died of overdose before he was 30. Andy Warhol befriended Basquiat towards the end of his life and they made large collaborative paintings together. Basquiat was later mythologised and a feature film of his life was made.
!Batchelor David.
British artist born in 1950s who uses coloured acrylic sheet to make strange utilitarian low trolleys in a multitude of colours which are then arranged to make an installation, or stacked to make a tower of coloured light.
!Batty Lolly.
UK artist born in 1960s made a series of abstract sculptures of three dimensional polyhedrons, carved in polystyrene and painted to a smooth and seemingly solid appearance so that they appear to have been carved. A kind of solid waist high diagrammatic snow flake in appearance, very smooth, clean and white.
!Baxter Glen.
British artist born late 1930s, cartoon drawings accompanied by text of events, happenings and people that illustrate a typical Englishness with a self deprecating humour, and a surreal, slapstick narrative. These cartoons were used by advertisers in the 1990s and Baxter’s work although anonymous became extremely widely known. 
!Beattie Basil.
Messy figurative painter of the Glasgow school, born late 1930s died 1990s. Themes of fishing boats, chip shops and Scottish machismo painted lyrically and colourfully.
!Beban Breda.
UK performance artist born 1950s.
!Becher Berndt and Hilla. 
German husband and wife working together born around 1950. Their work became known in the 1980s series of black and white photos of industrial sites, silos, water towers etc. that imbue the subjects with a formal sculptural quality notable for absence of any form of life.
!Beecroft Vanessa.
US performance artist who uses professional models to make her room sized installations. The models are chosen for their perfect bodies and dressed alike, usually in underwear or naked but always wearing shoes and make up and usually wigs. In an early work they are wearing yellow bobbed wigs and items of school uniforms. A more recent work has natural hair but identical face and body makeup to give them an ethereal gleam, and high heels. The models stand, sit or lie, however they feel comfortable, for the duration of the work usually 4 hours, and may engage the viewers eye or not as they choose. They do not speak. Although the viewer is free to wander among them, none does as taken as a group this experience is intimidating. This of course is Beecroft's intention, to show that the supposedly objectified naked beautiful bodies of women can, simply by their presentation and context, be rendered very powerful permissively because of our objectification of them and our habit of looking at naked women for pleasure. The control of the situation is given to them by their very muteness, their make up and their beauty, traditionally seen as means of oppression. Of course there are those that would argue that in appropriating and reusing these means Beecroft is doing no better, merely relying on our curiosity and our desire for vicarious pleasure. 
!Benn Tony.
British figurative conceptual painter born late 1950s.
!Bickerton Ashley.
American. Bickerton was part of the Neo Geo group of artists who worked in New York in the 1980s. He made a number of wall mounted sculptures that consisted of closed metal boxes, shallow but projecting some distance from the wall, which he covered with stickers and logos, giving them the appearance of both an item of luggage and a sign, its function entirely ambivalent. The stickers themselves were created by Bickerton and were generic types that had no actual reference in the real world to a company, an instruction or an advertisement. Bickerton also made a work in which a life-size rubber shark was hung from the ceiling with block and tackle. Hanging from the ropes were also a number of small yachts fenders along with sealed transparent plastic film bags containing some kind of blue gel. This ambiguous work seemed to represent the sea (the blue gel) and its dangers (of yachts shipwrecked, shark attacks), as well as having a particular tough almost fetishistic aesthetic.
!Bijl Guillaume.
Belgian artist Bijl is known for his perfect recreations of very ordinary spaces. Each installation he creates in a gallery is a recreation down the last detail of a particular place, for example a gym, a supermarket, a beauty salon, another gallery, a bookshop etc. These spaces would be usable for their projected purpose, such is the attention to detail, but their remain functionless and empty, and such is their purpose. They are three-dimensional facsimiles, all the more unreal for being so real. Bijl is interested in expanding the boundaries of 
!Bik Lisbeth and Van der Pol.
Dutch artist partners who make collaborative investigations of a sociological nature, setting up forums for investigation into contemporary art practises and in particular encouraging and facilitating cross-nationality projects and interaction. 
!Billingham Richard.
British photographer born around 1960 who has made a series of photographs of his own family. These reveal a chronically alcoholic father who is either comatose or the life and soul, and an overweight mother who likes to laugh. The pair watches a lot of TV, drink, smoke and play with their other children and their dog. Their house is untidy. The photographs are graphic and show his family for what it is, revealing his parents asleep, happy, sad, playful, and drunk. The degree of intimacy that the viewer is given is startling, and the photographs ask the viewer to consider their own preconceptions and to think about making judgements based on appearance. For on closer inspection, a family that might have been considered dysfunctional appear also to be very happy and loving. The photographs are all taken in the house with ambient light. To get the image he wants, Billingham often places the camera low down or high up, using any angle necessary to get a certain part of the room or a certain number of people. They have a immediacy, an unstudied air, yet the series as a whole is a comprehensive insight into other peoples life. 
Billingham followed the photographs with a film, made in the same way, which reveals that the father is evidently going to die of smoking related disease, and that the younger brother is very good at speaking on camera. Billingham’s work is characterised by its combination of casualness and thoughtfulness. It could not have been made by anyone other than a family member, as it otherwise would have been intrusive, vicarious. Equally it could not have been made unless the convention of fly on the wall documentary had been established and a fascination for the minutiae of ordinary lives become commonplace. Billingham was one of the first artists to combine this with portrait photography.
!Bjornsdottir Olaf.
!Blake Peter.
British pop artist working from the 1960s onwards, Blake made representational paintings that are very detailed. They often contain elements of patterning, decorative borders and are meticulously painted. Blake was chosen by the Beatles to paint the cover image of their Sargent Pepper album, depicting the band members dressed in uniform, amongst a group of fabulously dressed people. He often chose to portray British national identity in some way. He included a self-portrait in many paintings – he always appears with long hair and a long beard. Blake is still painting in the 2000s.
!Bleckner Ross.
Bleckner one of a number of painters in working in New York in the 1980s and early 90s making monumental scale works. Seminal solo exhibition at the Guggenheim in 1992 in which he showed a number of abstract works that looked like starry pieced domes, wavy stripes with ribbon rosettes dotted among them and fuzzy chandelier and flower like compositions. He also works on paper. Easy to interpret Bleckner's work as having a memorial quality – this is the era when many leading figures in the visual arts died from AIDS related illness.
!Bohm Katrine.
German artist born in 1970s who made social environments as gallery installations. Bohm designed a colour coordinated café/meeting/waiting space, with various printed paper items, paper cups and pens to match. She and placed literature and other printed material in the space. An anthropological sociological interest is the main part of her work in which the viewer is made into a participant. 
!Boltanski Christian.
East? German? Artist who made large scale installation and site- specific works particularly interested in socio-political issues, in the memorial, and in the legacy left for the living by the dead. One work was sited in a gap in a terrace of houses, a bombed out house never rebuilt. Boltanski discovered the names of the people who had lived in the house and inscribed them on the walls that still retained details of the interior of the house such as fireplaces, wall paper and paint. 
Leather suitcases WW2 aesthetic.
!Bolton Nick.
British artist who has made installation and video, born in the 1960s. A telephone box with etched glass has a sensor attached to the mouthpiece. When someone speaks on the phone, the light in the phone box becomes brighter or dimmer related to the pitch of the speakers voice. 
!Borland Christine.
Scottish artist born around 1970 who made a number of works featuring hand guns. She displayed a police blanket with a bullet hole in it. She presented a caravan in a gallery, containing some evidence of a crime or the suggestion that a crime could be committed, with a handgun lying on a table. Borland’s work displays an interest in how material evidence can be represented as an art work and in how that visual evidence can be used by the viewer to construct a narrative. 
!Bowery Leigh.
British fashion designer, club promoter and artist whose work defies definition but was very influential. Bowery died in the 1990s in his late thirties. Bowery always wore his own designs, usually involving heavily padded lycra to change his shape substantially, mask like make up often with big black eyes, rubber and fetish wear, extremely high heeled shoes and boots. His look morphed between male and female, sexual and asexual. His club night Taboo inspired a generation of people to dress up, and Bowery became an icon for many. Bowery exhibited himself in the window of a London gallery, a living work of art. 
!Boyce Sonia.
British artist working in the 1980s onwards who made work dealing with racial awareness and black identity. 
!Boyce Sonia.
British artist working in the 1980s onwards who made work dealing with racial awareness and black identity. 
!Bradshaw Philippe.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes large scale installations often using chain curtains. These have photographic imagery on them made through a commercial technique of using different coloured links to make the image. An installation in New York in a darkened room contained several curtains, some with photographs on, one with a nineteenth century landscape on, another with a Franz Hals painting on, placed around the space in the centre of which was a trampoline. Video monitors displayed a montage of imagery and also a film of Bradshaw jumping on the trampoline naked, which has been a performance as part of the installation. The effect was that of multiple images, hard to grasp (the chain curtain images are hard to see at close quarters) and disorientating, but visually very rich.
!Brakhage Stan.
Late twentieth century Canadian film maker, Brakhage has made a large body of video work. He is interested in visual narrative and its disruption. One work consists of a fictional tale performed by actors of the infidelity of a modern sea captain’s wife. This is shown as one projection but in fact consists of several plot lines woven together, leading the viewer to make choices about the direction of the narrative. Another work takes 24 hours to view. A film set in a deserted house shot in the dark by the light of one torch is presented on a freestanding screen so that the viewer must walk around it. It is shot in black and white and seems to be extremely sinister. This is to do with Brakhage's use of cinematic convention, not to do with any imposed narrative, thus acknowledging the viewer's subjectivity.
!Breakwell Ian.
British artist whose career began in the 1970s, Breakwell is best known for his written diaristic work. He made a number of pieces with large amounts of densely handwritten text, often detailing his own life and observations about life. He also used photographs. In the 1980s he made a performance in which the audience was confronted with a stage curtain. When it opened they were faced with a painted audience and the back of a proscenium arch, and in fact they themselves were on stage.
!Brietz, Candice.
Artist born in 1970s.
!Brown Cecily.
British artist born in the 1970s Brown paints very large splashy, dribbly and apparently messy works with tightly controlled colour choices. At first glance these appear to be abstract expressionistic paintings but almost immediately they reveal themselves to be figurative. Hidden in the mass of brush marks and paint splashes is a meticulously constructed figure, or figures often in pornographic poses. The works therefore set up an expectation for the viewer and then almost immediately confounds it. It also makes an analogy with abstract expressionism and male sexual gratification. 
!Brown Glenn.
Brown is known for his excessively meticulous paintings that reproduce exactly other works of art, or more accurately that reproduce reproductions of other works of art. Brown scales up a reproduction, chosen from among many, all of which are different in colour due to the printing processes. He then paints this reproduction faithfully in oil paint, using the tiniest of brushes, reproducing not only what the work depicts but also the actual brushstrokes, giving the painting a trompe l’oeil quality. In fact it is debatable whether Browns paintings are copies our trompe l’oeil since they do seduce the viewer into thinking that their surface is thick paint rather than flat and smooth. This is particularly evident in a series of de Kooning copies, where the thickly encrusted paint is entirely realistic. However on closer inspection the ‘impasto’ has a plasticised quality. Brown's de Kooning series culminated in a number of painted ‘lumps’ – apparently lumps of paint palette scrapings which are probably what de Kooning used to have in his studio but in fact bronze casts painted by brown to give the illusion of paint scrapings. Brown subsequently copied Fragonard's Boy in a Hat, and girl on a swing, which he showed upside down, and a number of science fiction paintings by Chris Foss, which were the subject of a copyright legal action. Brown's work deals with reproduction and its importance in the dissemination of the images, question whether what people know best is the image itself or its reproduction, and questioning the importance of the hand of the artist. His main idea is to make painting the subject of itself.
!Bulatov, Eric.
Russian artist working in the 1980s and 90s who combines socialist realist imagery, blue skies, fields, with large text painted across the image.
!Bulloch Angela.
British artist born in the late 1960s. Bulloch's sculptures and installations deal with the social structures of society, the rules by which we live and the deconstruction of art and image making. Bulloch’s 90s works include a mudslinging machine which created a mud spatter ‘painting’ on the wall, a drawing machine which drew a blue spattered line along a wall. These machines were viewer activated in some way. Often a piece of furniture designed by the artist would trigger the machines to work if sat on. The machines would be presented in an environment which invited contemplation, the artist would fill a space with bean bags made to her design, often large enough to seat groups of people. The bean bags symbolised communality, and were a striking alternative to the customary stand and don’t touch regulations in galleries. Bulloch was one of a number of artists in the 90s who were exploring the relationship of the viewer to art but more particularly to the institutional context in which they found it. She questioned the social norms and the normalising structures in which we live by presenting such structures in new ways.
Her Rules works are an example of this – presenting rules designed for specific purposes as if they are general rules to live by, rules that may be written in a small book are put on a wall using transfer lettering, giving them a perverse authority
In early 2000s Bulloch made some small sculptures out of chewed gum which was then cast and painted. She continued her series of works made from pixel boxes, an innovation of her own which consists of a wooden box with one opaque acrylic face, inside which is a light source with the capacity to change colour infinitely. This is linked to a computer, which is able to control the change of light colours, using as its instructions of the changes the pixels in a video image, or a piece of music. Bulloch stacks the pixel boxes vertically in a variety of formations and also horizontally to create the effect of an illuminated dance floor and minimal abstract sculpture. In the latter case the pixels were controlled by sound. Bulloch often works with sound, and in 2003 she made a number of small sculptural objects, smooth amorphous shapes sprayed with white lacquer, which on handling revealed an inbuilt and incongruous means of generating sound, perhaps in response to light levels for example.]
!Burr Tom.
American artist working in the 1990s onwards.
!Burton Scott.
American artist who made work with furniture.
!Bustin Jane.
British painter born 1960s makes severe abstract works that are both investigations into the effect of colour and pigment, surface and also into votive painting and the painting as symbolic or spiritual object of contemplation. Bustin uses natural colours, dark reds, blues, black white. There is never a figurative element in her paintings, merely subtle shifts in tone that may hint at a form. 
!Caivano Varda.
Painter working in the early 2000s. Caivano paints still lifes that have a look of late nineteenth and early twentieth century.
!Calame Ingrid.
Calame's apparently abstract paintings are in fact taken from the outlines of stains she finds on the street and in public places. She photographs the ground, draws the outlines of the stains, then paints the canvas in subtle colours to create an abstract image from something very mundane and ephemeral.
!Callan John.
British sculptor born in 1960s who pushed paint through pin pricks following the lines on pages of text in an open book to create painted text. Built a small house or dog kennel out of bricks and mortar. 
!Calle Sophie.
French artist born in the 1950s, Calle has made a large body of project and research based work. Her Following Piece is perhaps her best known work.
