In this performative project made in Venice, she selected a man to follow for a month. He never became aware of her constant presence until the project was over. Calle adopted the techniques of undercover surveillance in her project and kept a detailed diary with photographs, which she made into a series of text panels and presented as a documentary installation. For another incognito work, Chambermaid, Calle got a job as a chambermaid in a hotel. She searched the rooms and luggage that she cleaned and made lists of what she found and what she deduced about the occupants of the room – the kind of people they were, what their occupations were, why they were staying in the hotel. She photographed the rooms and things in them that interested her. This work was presented as text and image on the wall. Calle's interest in these works was in what we can discover about an absent subject from the visual world that surrounds them and in how we can reconstitute that subject. This process gives rise to an intense awareness our own subjectivity which is included in the final work by the use of her own voice in the text of the work. The viewer of the work is made aware of their position as voyeur which they share with the artist.
Calle subsequently continued her interest in subjectivity but began to work with people rather than observing them unseen. The Blind involved asking blind from birth and those who had lost their sight since birth to describe images. These descriptions are presented in place of images on the walls of a gallery giving a privileged place to the images of the mind's eye, images that would never normally become concrete.
For a similar work made with museum attendants Calle asked those who worked in a certain museum to describe the paintings that they observed daily in the course of their employment when those paintings were not present because they were on loan. The resulting descriptions were placed on the wall in the place of the loaned exhibit. The discrepancies in the descriptions made the viewers subjectivity very obvious; descriptions of the same work would vary greatly, placing an object in a painting that was not there for example, adding something new to a painting or changing the composition of the colours. 
!Callery Simon.
British artist born around 1960 Callery made a series of white paintings of banal urban landscapes in the early 1990s which relied on impasto and tiny amounts of colour in the white to create the almost intangible image.
!Cantor Ellen.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes video and drawings.
!Cardiff Janet and Georges Bures Miller.
Canadian artist and musician/sound engineer partner working from the 1990s onwards. Cardiff made a work for the Whitechapel Library in London which took the viewer on a walking tour – the viewer was given a personal CD player which directed their steps and used sound to interpret and accompany what they were seeing. She made a gallery installation with a choir in which the choir members, performing a medieval choral work, were recorded individually, and then played through individual speakers placed on speaker stands at ear height in a circle in the gallery. Thus the viewer could choose how to experience the work by where they stand, and hear each voice individually. This gave a sense of what it is like to be part of a choir, and also a sense of the physical space which a choir occupies and how that space is experienced aurally. The gallery space is also articulated by where the sound is coming from – this varies according to whether the whole choir is singing, or just the sopranos in one segment of the circle, or a few of each type of voice dotted round the circle. The work gives the non participant a participatory experience and inverts the performer listener relationship. 
!Carnegie Gillian.
British artist born in the late 1960s who began her career in the 1990s. Carnegie makes paintings in two distinct ways. She paints her own and other female bodies - nearly always the back view, mainly featuring the buttocks - in a realist way using muted lifelike colours. These paintings are small, about 40 cm high. She also paints landscapes in extremely thick impasto, the canvases ranging from 40 cm to 150 cm high. These landscapes are usually woodland scenes, sometimes with lakes, rarely featuring people. The painting is sometimes squeezed straight from the tube. Close up the image is nearly impossible to read, in particular in the paintings which are predominantly one colour. This would often be blacks or very dark colours, appearing as if at night. In these paintings the image is revealed by the sheen on the squeezes and smears of the paint, the texture and the shadows created. In other landscapes more typical colours are used, blues, browns and greens, but in all the landscapes the vegetation is rendered in detail, as is the bark of trees and the ripples on water. There is a sinister atmosphere in the images, although they appear timeless there is somehow the implication of a contemporary narrative. Similarly with the nudes, although a traditional subject, the fact that they are often self portraits coupled with her obsession with bottoms, gives a contemporary twist  to the sexual projection of these images and hints at both onanism and voyeurism.
Carnegie subsequently began to paint floral still lifes, using a much lighter palette although still restrained. In these the background and any vessels holding the flowers are painted smoothly and the flowers are rendered in her style of thick impasto and squeezings, a few judiciously placed ones making them very convincing as flowers. Carnegies paintings to date make an investigation into the history of painting styles and subject matters, subverting traditional subject matter by making anachronistic and unexpected juxtaposition of style and subject matter. 
!Carter Tony.
British artist born around 1950. In the 1980s Carter made a number of installation works, based around an existing narrative or historical events and heavily theorised, for which he created highly finished elements often including printed text and drawings. One such work contained the silver buttons from an eighteenth century character’s jacket, for which he cast several silver buttons. Carter gave detailed attention to the display of the elements of his installations, fabricating highly finished display cases and plinths.
!Carvahlo Melanie.
British artist who is interested in how people conceptualise their world. In a work from 2002 she invited a large number of people from all walks of life to make a painting of ‘home’. She displayed the resulting works in a gallery as part of an installation. Carvahlo also makes complex photo collages that include people, buildings and brightly coloured exotic flowers and sunsets.
!Casebeere James.
Casebeere made a series of photographs in the late 1980s of scenes that he had constructed entirely from cut, folded and shaped paper. For example a rural scene with a river, trees and a canoe. This is photographed in black and white. There is not a particular attempt at realism or trompe l’oeil as in the later work of Thomas Demand, rather the scenes are a combination of fairytale and cartoon, given a sombre seriousness by the use of black and white. This relates them back to early twentieth century American landscape photography.
!Cattelan Maurizio. 
Sculptor and installation artist who uses stuffed animals in his work. Hung a stuffed horse from ceiling in a horse sling, stuffed to look slumped and therefore very realistic, as if it were either dead or tranquillised? An effigy of the pope toppling to the floor felled by a meteorite. A table football game for two teams of 11 to play simultaneously. A squirrel slumped at a tiny table with a gun showing an obvious suicide. A small boy wearing a hooded sweatshirt sits at a school desk and has speared a pencil through his hand. Cattelan’s work is often intended to be disturbing and provocative. 
A large format photograph of the back of the Hollywood sign reversed so that it reads the right way, apparently installed in a mountainous area, it is unclear whether a huge deconstruction was undertaken to make the photograph or whether it is digitally created.
!Celmins Vija.
Artist born in the 1940s or 50s who has made a large number of paintings and drawings of the sky and the ground. Celmins paints on small landscape format canvases about 60 cm wide or slightly more, using very small brushes. Or she makes drawings of a similar size using pencil. In either case she will draw the most minimal view – a patch of dust on the ground with a few tiny chips of gravel becoming as important as if it were the surface of the moon. She draws the surface of the moon. A tiny part of the night sky becomes a deep blue painting of tiny diamonds. Celmins work brings the minute and the gigantic together but the works have a deceptively modest unassuming appearance. They are painstakingly detailed in their execution. 
!Chadwick Helen.
British artist born in the late 1950s, died about 1998. Chadwick made sculptural installations and photographic work, often combining the two. Her work dealt with the female body and ideal beauty. One work consisted of light boxes on plinths with photographic images of luxurious and sensual arrangements of hair, ribbon and entrails, and for example length of intestine, braided together. Chadwick often associated the visceral with the luxurious and the interior of the body with its clothed exterior. She was regarded as a feminist artist and in common with other women artists of her generation produced much work dealing with the female body. Another work consisted of a series of wooden boxes of different shape and dimensions, produced to correspond with the size of her own body at various stages in her life. Thus the box for her birth is new born child sized, another is curved as if a child were curled up, others are standing adult sized. The boxes have photographic transfers in black ink of images of Chadwick at these different ages. They stand in a group. Around the walls of the space in which the boxes are installed are wooden panels with similar images with Latin texts running around the four edges of the panels. The texts describe the ages of a woman. 
The work for which Chadwick is best known is probably Piss Flowers. These are painted casts taken from the space made in snow when Chadwick and her male partner urinated in it. When cast as a positive this space is revealed to have a central column going down and a petal like circle surrounding it, made by the splashes of hot urine on the snows surface and the main stream melting deep into the snow. The male ‘flower’ has a much wider petal arrangement and a shorter ‘stalk’. The female ‘flower’ is the opposite, a result which fired Chadwick’s interest. The Piss Flowers are a nod to Duchamp’s casts of the negative space of male and female genitalia.
!Chalkley Brian Dawn.
British artist born 1950s, Chalkley works as both a man and a woman as he is a cross dresser. Sometimes his work is authored by both Brian and Dawn. He uses video and photography to investigate identity. For example a film in which Dawn is seen in various dangerous scenarios, alone in isolated places having possibly been attacked. 
!Chapman Jake and Dinos.
British artists born in mid 1960s the Chapmans are brothers who work together. They became known in 1993 for Disasters of War, a sculpture that recreated Goya’s disasters of war as a 1:100 scale model. This grisly scene was presented on a plinth the size of a table top, realistically made and leaving nothing to the imagination. Flayed corpses hang from trees, severed limbs are strewn about, headless torsos are propped up, heads stuck on bayonets. They later made a series of etchings based on this work in homage to the original.
Another early work was Little Death Machine, a working machine built in model scale and presented in a vitrine on a plinth. It was a rubber model of a brain that functioned as a pump to spurt out white liquid. 
On one hand the Chapmans' work is deeply rooted in art history, politics and subversion with implied criticism of Western capitalism and of the failure of art to deal with important issues. On the other hand the artists delight in the puerile and a childish humour and set out to deliberately and defiantly shock. 
It is the Chapmans' life size sculptures of mutant children that are most notorious in this regard. These children, which have the appearance of shop window mannequins made in what has become their main medium of cast fibreglass and realistically airbrush painted, have genitalia on their face and smooth skin where their genitals should be. A girl might have a vagina as a mouth, or several orifices on her body where none would usually be. a boy may have a penis as a nose. The children are posed in apparently compromising positions and situated in a beautiful garden built of model material. There are trees and plants with large fantasy flowers and grass made from a material that resembles a scaled up version of the green sand-like material used as grass by model makers and model railway enthusiasts. The children appear to be frolicking in a strange Eden. The Chapmans have in this work as in all their work taken a basic human urge, such as the urge to kill, the sexual urge and its many perversions, and urge to power and represented it in order to confront their viewer with the possibility of discovering such urges in themselves. In this sense their work is highly politicised, but their use of shock tactics sometimes works against them. 
The life-size mutant children are in fact a scaled up version of a 1:100 scale model, and led the artist also to recreate details from the disasters of War as life size work. On this scale the latter seems less shocking. The Chapmans made a porn film featuring people with these same prosthetics genitalia attached to their bodies.
The Chapmans continue to make etchings and other works on paper, and to make large scale installations and models. Their McDonald’s Concentration camp 2000 is a very large 1:50 model of a fictitious concentration camp no doubt based on a real one, which has McDonalds logos. The camp processes people, we see the tiny figures being brought in, living in squalor, being killed and ‘processed’ in various ways. Much of what the model depicts is familiar to us not through experience but through fictitious depiction on film and television and the model relies on this to animate it. The work makes the daring if literal analogy between a fast food company bringing cheap food to the masses with famously no concern for their detrimental and social impact, and the Nazis. They have also made larger scale sculptures again in their usual medium of cast fibreglass, realistically painted with an airbrush, which represents a kind of gothic heavy metal concentration camp monument with massed skulls, very black and dark.
The Chapmans' most recent work is a darkened room full of apparent African tribal art, carvings in wood which are painted and have added grass, beads and teeth. The room seems to be in a museum, darkened to preserve vulnerable and light sensitive materials, which lends a familiar reverence to the sculptures set on plinths. There is a deadened acoustic. On closer inspection the viewer can see that the carvings all in some way feature the MacDonald’s logo, the golden M, or represent a burger or Ronald McDonald the company’s clown mascot. These are cleverly worked as ‘African’ looking sculpture, Ronald McDonald a totemic figure, the burger often the mouth of a figure, the M appearing as a decorative element. The ironic pairing of the colonial collection with the symbol of capitalism.
!Chen, Gang.
!Chetwynd, Lali.
Artist working in London, Chetwynd has made several performance works in the late 1900s and early 2000s.
!Cheung Lisa.
Korean artist born in the 1970s who makes photographic works often shown as slide projections.
!Child Andrew.
British artist born in the 1960s.
!Childish Billy.
British artist, poet and musician born around 1960. Childish has made a large number of naïve figurative paintings often of himself and his friends on found wooden boards, the sides of boxes, and other unconventional materials. He is known equally for his poetry which is written with, and makes a virtue of his dyslexic spelling, and the punk band which he fronts, The Headcoats. Working in this way since the early 1980s, Childish achieved notoriety beyond his cult following in the late 1990s when he was immortalised by ex-girlfriend Tracy Emin in several works, including her embroidered tent ‘Everybody I Have Ever Slept With’. Since then Childish formed the Stuckists, a self proclaimed movement that promotes the idea that British art in particular the work of the artists that make up so called Brit Art, is simply ‘stuck’. The Stuckists set out to critique it, by means of pamphleteering, demonstrating, fly postering and other public protestation and interventions. 
!Chilvers John.
Born in the 1960s the British painter Chilver has made paint itself the subject of his conceptual paintings. He uses paint squeezed or piped in the way icing would be to decorate a cake, through a Square nozzle, to produce a three dimensional line on the canvas. He also adds paint from a traditional round nozzle. The paintings depict simple scenes, for example a fence with a bird on it. Or a style with a clump of grass. The fence or style will be piped in square lines often black or dark grey, the bird in many colours of thinner round lines, the whole giving the impression of casualness and speed of execution when in fact the reverse is obviously the case. Chilver often uses puns in his title, for example style for both the object and the other meaning. He investigates the nature of painting the painted mark, without actually painting at all since the backgrounds are entirely flat and the paint never touched by a brush or worked at all. 
!Chodzko Adam.
British artist born 1960s. Looking for Jesus a work made in the 1990s. Chodzko placed a classified advertisement requesting people who look alike to send him passport photos. Often they bore no resemblance at all. Chodzko made a work with Afghan refugees living in Hastings in 1990 in which he asked them to sort through and select from the archive of Cubitt, an artist run organisation in London. The selection was placed in a watertight box and buried below the high water mark on the beach at Hastings. Another work from 2003 was site specific, Chodzko placed a billboard in a field in Texas on which it was printed the directions to a billboard outside Cubitt in London, which again had printed the directions to the billboard in Texas. A kind of transatlantic endgame with the idea of encouraging a sense of place, geography, time and space. A photo of the US billboard displayed at site of the UK one and vice versa.
Chodzko's work is usually socially engaged and often involves research, correspondence or mass participation.
!Clemente Francesco.
Italian American painter born in the late 1940s who was extremely successful in the 1980s. Clemente makes figurative painting and works on paper, as well as small scale sculpture and ceramics. His work shows his interest in Indian art, in its colours and in its often spiritual themes. Clememte's main subject is the male body.
!Collins Anne.
US artist born in the 1970s who makes photographs, for example of women in urban and rural landscapes, with a precisely controlled banality. 
!Collins Susan.
UK artist who pioneered the use of holograms in art in the early 1980s, Collins has contoured to be at the forefront of technological innovation in visual art. Recently she has been working with sound and with the internet.
!Collishaw Matt.
British artist born in the 1960s who made a large format photograph of a bullet entrance hole in a persons scalp. 
!Condo George. 
American artist born around 1960 who makes paintings that use art historical references, often nearly remaking a particular work but with Condo’s often fantastic twists and distortions so as to create an entirely contemporary version of a painting from history. The paintings use a variety of styles and usually bright colours, and although they have a clearly historical reference they often have a science fiction/fantasy reference too. Sometimes they contain absurd elements. Condo also paints LP covers, turning them into large paintings around a metre high.
!Corcia Philipe-Lorca di.
Photographer who takes images of the everyday and shows them in groups.
!Counsel Melanie.
British artist born around 1960.
!Coventry Keith.
British artist born in 1960s who paints white on white paintings that reproduce famous paintings in oil paint without colour.
!Cox Stephen.
British artist born in the 1950s who made some carved stone tondos with classical imagery and inscriptions in the 1980s.
!Creed Martin.
Scottish artist born around 1960 who makes sculpture and installation from almost nothing. All his work is assigned a number and a description for the title, For example, Work No.XX: A door opening and shutting, Work No.XX: The lights going on and off, Work No. XX: One A4 sheet of paper screwed into a ball. Further works include a cube made of layers of elastoplast, a cube made of layers of masking tape cubes, a bulging protrusion from the wall of a room which is plastered and painted to be as part of the wall. 
The work is sometimes active, for example a work which consists of a room full of white balloons through which the viewer must push their way. This references Yves Klein’s ‘Void’, and empty gallery space. The door that opens and shuts is operated by a door closer on a timed switch. The lights going on and off is similar. This work has also been ‘performed’ by Creeds. band Owada – whenever the lights came on the band played, whenever they went off the band stopped, as if the electricity supply for the amplifiers were linked to the lights. Or the other way around. Owada which can be described as making minimal conceptual music gained success as an art band in the late 90s and early 2000s with tracks such as One to A Hundred, which featured Creed speaking these numbers in order and the band playing rising scales one note per number. 
!Crewsdon Gregory.
American artist who makes photographs of staged events that appear to be potential crime scenes. There is something of the supernatural about the images, which have an unreality due to the highly controlled lighting and decisions made in the printing. A small town petrol station is seen from above at night and the disposition of people and vehicles seems strangely static, leading the viewer to guess at possibilities, perhaps to wonder whether there has been or is about to be an alien invasion. Another work might show a person looking at the sky, presaging some unknown possibly extra terrestrial event. The photographs are complex and ambitious in their construction of a subtly warped reality, shot on location rather than in a studio. 
!Cross Dorothy.
Irish artist born in the mid-twentieth century who makes work from leather and fabric, a kind of feminist surrealism, for example a corset made from leather tanned from a cow’s udders with the udders sticking out.
!Crotty Russell.
American artist born around 1960 who makes obsessive biro drawings sometimes on large sheets of paper, also in very large books, at least 80 cm high. The drawings are made by colouring in with blue biro, using the pen fro the purpose fro which it is least suited, on a scale which is plainly absurd. 
!Cruz Juan.
British artist born in the mid 1960s who works with sound and text. Cruz made an installation performance in which he translated the text of Cervantes great Spanish novel Don Quixote in real time over a number of days. He also made a work based on the Duende, an untranslatable Spanish word that describes the ‘feeling’ needed to perform flamenco. Cruz is chiefly interested in language and translation.
!Cucchi Enzo. 
Italian artist who made large scale drawings of classical nudes using an obsessive cross-hatched style for which he became very well known in the 1980s. 
!Cullen Shane.
Northern Irish artist who made a large scale text installation for which he printed or stencilled or used transfer lettering to inscribe the letters written to him during a correspondence he had with political prisoners on remand in the H block, a notorious prison in Northern Ireland. These covered the walls of the exhibition space. 
!Cumberland Stewart.
British painter born in the 1970s.
!Currin John.
American painter born in 1962 who became extremely successful in the late 1990s and early 2000s. Currin made his name by painting women with unfeasibly enormous breasts. The paintings are made in a mixture of kitsch and old master style. The women have exaggerated cartoon like features yet the colours used are entirely realistic. The women seem to be there to hold the male gaze and fulfil the male desire. They are however often painted in pairs, absorbed with each other, ignoring the viewer. Currin also paints portraits of real people, still lifes and interiors. His knowing and slightly anarchic use of virtuoso painting techniques and undeniable skill in painting and drawing allied with subject matter that would have been entirely unacceptable in the 1980s or early 90s was what brought him to attention. 
!Cyril Griffiths Brian.
British artist born in the 1960s who has made a number of installations from cardboard, discarded materials, empty plastic containers and any number of banal materials. Griffiths’ space ship command module control panel made in 1997 was an adult-sized version of a child’s dream. Griffiths has made many objects that seem like things that the viewer thinks they could make themselves but the scale of them makes them much more than this. A world in which everything can be made from anything is created.
!Dalwood Dexter.
British artist born in 1960 who makes figurative history paintings. Dalwood began his career painting expressionistic autobiographical narratives, often depicting himself and members of his family, particularly his father. He spent a year working in India and the influence of Indian miniatures is clear in his work of this period. Dalwood began to use the photograph as a source for his work around 1989, when his work became less narrative. He used images of multiple reflections in windows, grids and all over lattice patterns to create veil and screen effects in which appeared various motifs, for example a painted wooden toy bird. Dalwood also began to make groups of small paintings which were hung together. One such group depicted various personal histories, including paintings based on photographs of his father as a young man. His style of painting remained eclectic using a variety of textures, methods of application and thickness of paint.
In 1995 Dalwood began a series of paintings of interiors which marked a change in his work. These mainly featured ceilings and light fittings, and are the only works of Dalwood’s that come close to abstraction.
Dalwood began to paint fictitious interiors, making a collage from magazine cuttings and then replicating it on canvas, including the cut edges of the paper and differences in colour of sky, for example, taken from two different places. The interiors are imagined for a variety of well known people, such as Elvis’ Living Room, Bill Gates’ Bedroom, and also famous venues such as Studio 54 night club, Liberace’s Drawing Room. 
These collaged interiors function as portraits of a person but they began to include the work of other artists. Dalwood combined the work of an artist whom he connected in some way with the person he was making the painting about and added a part of their work to each composition by collaging. Mary Shelley is combined with Munch and Friedrich, Nixon with late Braque, Che Guevara with Piero della Francesca and Mao with Antonello da Messina. A painting of the greenhouse in which Kurt Cobain committed suicide has cherry blossoms from a Van Gogh painting.
In this way Dalwood has made a series of portraits of famous people from a variety of walks of life. In 2000 he began to make the connection with history painting more explicit by making paintings that referred to a date rather than a person. 
Dalwood’s paintings are between one and four metres high and he uses a range of techniques according to the artist to whom he is making reference. 

!Dant Adam.
British artist born in the 1970s who makes wry satirical cartoon style drawings about art, artists, and the art world. Dant’s project is serious and comprehensive in its ambition to comment upon everything that happens but to make sure that art doesn't take itself too seriously. He began by making a weekly self published pamphlet, much in the vein of eighteen century pamphleteers soapbox speakers. The pamphlet was hand drawn and written by a pseudonymous character invented by Dant to represent himself (who was often shown in the drawings). It was a bit like a diary and featured topical news about art and advice written in mock serious style. It was photocopied and folded into a pocket sized booklet which Dant himself distributed wherever he went free of charge. He did this every week for several years, and it was the long term nature of the project that made it successful. Dant constructed a portable booth from which to distribute the pamphlet and became a presence at art fairs and museum exhibitions. By a simple weekly action Dant produced a major work. !Davenport Ian.
British artist born in the 1960s who began to make the drip paintings for which he became known in the 1990s. Davenport poured liquid paint onto a canvas from the top, allowing gravity to take the drip to the bottom of the canvas in a perfectly vertical line. He continued in this way across the width of the canvas using different colours until he had achieved a striped painting made without a single brushstroke, or any touch of the artist’s hand. At first sight the stripes would appear to have been painted with a brush perhaps even using masking tape, but on closer inspection tiny irregularities in the pour and the quality of the edge of the stripes made it obvious how the painting was made. Sometimes the paintings were in sombre colours, other times they were rainbow striped. Later Davenport developed various ways of making the pouring easier (fixing the paint pot to a runner for example) and he was able to make bigger canvases and even to pour directly onto a wall.
Davenport also made paintings by pouring a coating of one colour of paint onto a canvas placed horizontally, and then adding in a stripe, a line, and arc in a contrasting colour by pouring directly into the wet surface. The two colours when dry formed a perfect flat shiny surface, in the way that the technique of enamelling can be used to combine colours. 
!Davey Grenville.
British artist born around 1960 who made very large cast and lacquered fibreglass sculptures in the 1980s and early 90s. Davey created abstract forms that had a functionality about them, like giant lids, plugs or covers, often round, sometimes pairs that seemed that they might fit together. These were made from technical drawings, perfectly constructed and painted in shiny cream, grey, or white. The sculptures were placed on the gallery floor, often on their curved sides rather than on any flat plane they may have had. 
!David Enrico.
British Italian artist born in early 1960s who made a series of very large embroideries mounted on stretchers in the late 1990s. These were taken from collages and drawings he made from fashion magazines depicting women in fetish wear, extremely stylised clothes, very exaggerated bodies, very high heels, masks, drawn in a geometric style often with a 1920s or 30s feel. Although the images were of women they depict a non-specific sexuality, the women often have large masculine bodies and may seem more like cross dressed men. David used woollen thread to fill in the figures with satin stitch, and due to the scale of the image often 2 metres high or more, the stitches sometimes needed to be over a metre in length. Sometimes the stitches represented rays of light, too, or in one case silver thread was used on a blue canvas to represent the tight binding on the body of a model.
David used anything from fashion, craft and design that he likes to create these creatures. One had a butterfly entirely concealing her face, like a mask. Another is like an African queen. They are decadent often sinful and sexual images, their materials (those of handcraft) at odds with the subject matter. He also made items of furniture and lamps. He continued to make collages and began to work on some photo collages in 2000, using photos of himself wearing coloured paper constructions – red and blue, black and white, something like the costumes designed by the dada artists for cabaret Voltaire. Other collages used images from magazines and often the background paper was cut in a certain way so that the found image could be raised from the background. He used brooches and other found jewellery and began to use carpentry, furniture making and decorative arts materials.
In 2000 David began to draw men, (although it was not really the case that he had been thinking of the characters in his previous work as women). A man in a top hat and a morning suit, some kind of dandy, appeared in his drawing. A man in evening dress appears before the closed curtain of a theatre in a drawing in back and white that looks very much like a woodcut. A sculpture of a male figure is made of plywood coloured with wood stain; the profile of the figure is cut twice from sheets of ply and slotted together at right angles to each other. Turned wooden knobs are added to represent testicles. 
A large rag doll three metres high is made of canvas, stuffed, and embroidered with facial and bodily features and decorative patterns, and presented sagged against a plywood cabinet and lying on the floor. A gallery door is replaced by an etched glass and metal door that has an art nouveau style. A very long plywood screen covers the length of a gallery wall, on it a repeating interlinked pattern of a stylised male figure that has a 30s look is made by incising the wood and using wood stain to create a kind of faux marquetry.  A giant leather medal hangs on the wall. 
David was briefly a member of Donatella a group of artists and musicians who produced music videos and CDs, and made work in collaboration with fashion designers Eley Kishimoto for catwalk shows. 
!Davies Peter.
British artist born around 1970 who began his career making paintings consisting of many coloured dots on a white background on huge three or four metre wide canvases, creating patterns of waves, like an abstract pointillism. Davies always uses acrylic paint applied as flat solid colour. He went on to make text paintings in the later 1990s. For example The Hot 100 is a numbered list drawn up as a table in two columns with the lines painted in white, of the artists personal hundred favourite artists, painted in white on a multicoloured ground, a different colour for each line of text. The list functions as an illustration of subjectivity presented as a formally considered abstract painting. Davies subsequently made a diagram painting describing twentieth century art in terms of interlinked artists and movements. The text is painted in white on coloured elliptical or circular shapes to fit each name or title, which are linked or cross linked by coloured lines, the whole on a white background.
Davies then began to make a series of very large pattern paintings at least four metres wide. The paintings are abstract three dimensional patterns that cover the picture plane and create a surface pattern, some painted in bright colours, some in monochrome, a bit like geometric doodles that play with simple perspective and convex and concave illusion. Like the text paintings these are painted in flat acrylic paint, with hard edges to the colours painted by hand rather than masked. Some of them allude vaguely to Robert Delaunay’s paintings and to 30s textile patterns, others to futuristic cityscapes. 
!Davis Peter.
British artist
!Dawson Verne.
American artist whose paintings show an apparent utopia, a kind of modern pastoral world, with tiny people on the scale of the people in a Bosch painting populating woodland scenes. 
!De la Cruz Angela.
British artist who makes sculptures from collapsed and destroyed paintings. De la Cruz method is to construct a canvas which may be any size (from tiny to the largest which was a large scale installation of a canvas about 15 metres squared). She paints the canvas in a plain colour, and then destroys it by breaking the stretcher and then twisting the whole into a heap. This is then fixed and either displayed on the wall or the floor. These deceptively humble objects assume the presence of a sculptural object, thus a painting is transformed.
!Deacon Richard.
British artist Deacon began making very large sculptures in the 1980s at a time when sculpture was at a zenith of popularity. Deacon and his contemporaries such as Bill Woodrow, Grenville Davey etc. made abstract works largely from cheap easily available materials. Deacon used reclaimed wood which he cut into strips, plied and glued and bolted together to make large hollow snail shell like structures, which he later began to design on a computer. The nature of the materials belied the complexity of the shape and the effort required in their construction. The means of construction were very evident, for example glue spilled over from the joins where they had been compressed. What would usually be hidden or on the inside of such a large work, was actually the surface of the work. 
!Dean Tacita.
British artist born in 1960s who uses 35 mm film to make installations and simple projections. Lighthouse. Donald Crowhurst. Stormy ship blackboard drawings.
!Deller Jeremy.
British artist born in the late 1960s who is interested in different social groups and how they are creative. The authorship of Deller's work is always in question (an essential part of Deller’s work) – the creative work is as much the participants’ as it is Deller’s, who acts as the facilitator of an idea. 
In the mid 1990s Deller began working with groups of people brought together by a particular shared passion. One of the first of these was his Manic Street Preachers project. Deller asked young fans of the band to write and draw their feelings about them and their music. The resulting document was collected into a book and is a testament to the power of music to represent the feelings and hopes of a generation. Deller subsequently drew a chart linking acid house music with brass band music via many other forms of popular music. The chart was hand drawn in white on black employing circles containing text linked and cross linked by arrows.
Acid Brass was a project in which Deller commissioned a brass band to play acid house music. The resulting hybrid was performed in concerts halls and galleries, and released as a CD. Deller’s most ambitious work, Battle of Orgreave, was a full scale re-enactment of a battle fought between the police and striking miners at the peak of the miners strike in the early 1980s in Britain. The police charged the miners on horses. Deller arranged for several historical re-enactment societies to come together to stage a full scale re-enactment of this event. The battle and the filming of the battle was filmed by Mike Figgis, a film director, and intercut with original news footage. The resulting film was a very accurate portrayal of what happened that also revealed the lengths to which the governments went to ensure a media cover up of the brutality of the police and their determination that the scale of the protest should be played down. The Battle of Orgreave was shown on national television. Guest list was a work made for Imprint 93 an artist pamphlet series published my Matthew Higgs, which reproduced the guest list for an exhibition opening at the Institute of Contemporary Arts in London. The list which featured a number of celebrities revealed a degree of social ambition and perhaps sycophancy on the part of the institution.
The Folk museum was made by Deller with Alan Kane. Together they collected hand made craft and folk objects of no intrinsic value and displayed them as if they were valuable collection of artefacts. 
At the Venice Biennale in Deller made button badges with a stop/end sign (red circle with diagonal line bisecting it on a white ground) and the word Vogue on it. No Vogue referred to the magazine – Deller commented of the ubiquity of the magazine’s presence at any art event, and the ever increasing tendency of the art world to collide with the world of socialites and celebrities and be photographed doing so. Cardiff.
!Demand Thomas.
British artist who makes photographs of complex paper ‘sets’ that he constructs himself. The sets replicate a real space, for example an office, which Demand then remakes entirely in card and paper. The resulting construction is carefully lit and then photographed in large format and presented as a very large (about two metres high) print. The image provokes a double take – it seems to be a plausible space although oddly empty. Once the viewer begins to try to decode the image, and look for clues, it becomes apparent that for example the surfaces of the furniture are too homogenous, without detail. Even then it is difficult to establish why the space depicted seems unreal. When the process behind the work becomes clear the work can be viewed more as an achievement in terms of its construction.
!Dieroff Xenia.
German artist living and working in Britain, Dieroff makes photographic work. A slide installation projected onto a hospital screen uses silhouetted figures and clever dissolves to create the illusion of a patient undressing that morphs into a striptease. 
!Dion Mark. 
!Djikstra Rineke.
A Dutch artist who became known in the late 1990s for her photographic portraits of young people. Like Thomas Struth and Thomas Ruff Djikstra asked her subjects to look straight at the lens and not to smile. The resulting images are a true document of a generation. In 2000 she began to make video portraits. A film portraying young people at a club shows them standing against a white backdrop, moving slightly. They are listening to music which the viewer cannot hear, but Djikstra has asked them to stand still. Their body language reveals a lot about them, as do their clothes. Although it is a film, the participants have been captured in time as if it were a photographic portrait. Their youth is very expressive.
!Docherty Willie.
Northern Irish artist, Docherty made a number of photographic series and video works in the 80s and early 90s at the height of the troubles in Northern Ireland. These featured empty roads, empty landscapes, cars and checkpoints. A video work in his Turner prize nomination exhibition was filmed from a car driver point of view and purported to be a night drive with sinister intentions, although this was merely suggested with the greatest economy of means. Docherty's work has an oneric quality although it deals with political issues. He gives a glimpse of life on the border and of political strife in urban and rural landscapes.
!Doig Peter.
British painter who grew up in Canada, born 1957. Doig is chiefly a landscape painter and was among those British artists who created a resurgence of interest in painting in Britain in the early 1990s. His landscapes are often oneric, usually populated by a single figure if at all and have a strong sense of the unknown and of adventure. Doig paints the seasons, the mountains, forests, lakes, vernacular architecture, and occasionally sports such as fishing, skiing, snowboarding. He paints thinly and loosely, scattering the surface with blobs of thicker paint when the image is complete. This often serves to represent light, for example the white blobs in an early work The Architects House. He also painted a Corbusier housing block in a similar way. Both are seen through trees and use a lot of green brown, back and white with touches of yellow white and red. Often the touches of paint on the surface do not read as part of the image but become very apparent on close inspection. They may even be bars of colour that are not part of the image at all, or splatters.
 Another early work, the Bean Eater, shows a boy walking in a field empty except for a red wooden house or barn. A leafless branch of a tree with some kind of seed or flower painted like glowing orangey white bobbles. Later works from 2000 onwards depict a steamboat apparently steaming past a jungle on a river reminiscent of (apocalypse). This is a very green and lush painting. A tree in the moonlight with a girl sitting on a branch high up, painted in greys and lilacs, as if it were a night scene or a nightmare A lake with a canoe on it paddled by a man with long black hair and a beard, the canoe is orange and the lake very blue. This image is taken from an album cover. A painting of a mountain ski slope called Red ski jacket is very white with only the minute people bringing in colour. One of a snow boarder shows the boy in the air upside down at the top of the canvas with a group of people watching at the bottom.
Doig became extremely influential in the mid 1990s. 
!Donachie Kay.
British artist born in 1970s who made process paintings during the 1990s, using a roller to transfer an image to a prepared canvas, resulting in an often haunting shadowy painting, the image always seeming slightly removed from reality due to the technique. Donachie often painted isolated houses, a tent, a Christmas tree, the interiors of Paul Klee's studio. Somehow everything looks unpopulated. She uses a monochrome palette, choosing a predominant colour for a painting. The transfer technique brings the concept of the photographic print into the painting although no such process is involved. The viewer’s uncertainty about how the image is made is paralelled by the uncertain status of the subject of the painting. Following this Donachie began to paint figures, for example groups of young women, and although not necessarily using the same technique, she gives the image the same once removed quality that places it in an uncertain time. Lighter colours but still monochrome begin to appear. The paintings are small, mostly under a metre squared.
!Donagh Rita.
British artist who makes geometric drawings.
!Donegan Cheryl.
US video artist of Irish extraction, Donegan's work investigates the sculptural and painterly possibilities of everyday activities. In Sunflower, she spins on grass with a bottle of yellow paint attached to her waist, it sprays out and slowly paints a circle. In Kiss My Royal Irish Ass she paints her buttocks green and sits on sheets of paper, three times on each to make a clover leaf painting. In another work she suggestively and lasciviously drinks milk that is spraying slowly from a hole made in the side of a plastic bottle and when her mouth is full she sprays the milk on the wall. In another she holds a French stick between her knees and eats it as far down as she can reach. In many ways Donegan's concerns bear comparison to the work of Sarah Lucas, with her interest in how the banal and the domestic can be both sexually suggestive and formally sculptural and humorous. Donegan also makes reference to abstract expressionism and to the ejaculatory possibilities that can be seen in the work of Jackson Pollock, and mocks the machismo inherent in his work with her own abject attempts. 
!Drummond Fiona.
British artist born 1962 who takes portrait photographs and still lifes. She often uses flowers and references seventeenth century Dutch painting especially in terms of light, using studio set and lighting. She also paints dogs and flowers. 
!Dubossarsky and Vinogradov.
Russian painters born in the 1970s who paint collaboratively. Dubossarsky and Vinogradov are among the few Russian artists to gain commercial success in the US and Europe. They are accomplished painters and draughtsmen, and their canvases allude to both history paintings and to Russian Socialist Realist art. They often ask other artists to paint parts of the work for them and they may make several versions of the same painting or small versions of sections of larger works, for example a book on a snowy muddy ground with a frog diving into a hole.
!Dumas, Marlene.
!Dunham Carroll.
Abstract paintings made from the 1970s to the 2000s, brightly coloured but using a lot of white and the forms outlined in black, featuring blob and disc shapes, painted thickly, like lumps or pompoms.
!Durham Jimmy.
A Native Australian artist who makes junk sculptures, which appear to be fetish objects. The sculptures often have text applied. Durham’s work is concerned with the displacement of the Aboriginal people and their culture. 
!Eachus, Paul.
!Edmier, Keith.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes resin cast sculptures of plants and flowers. Edmier scales up a plant, such as a water lily, and models a version that is four metres high, casting it in pale pink or yellow resin. The sculpture has very realistic detail and despite its size looks very fragile. Edmier has used a variety of plants in this way, and sometimes makes very small sculptures.
!Eichmann Volker.
German artist born in the late 1970s who makes photo collages using interior design magazines and strips of text.
!Eisenman Nicole.
American artist who was one of the so-called ‘bad girls’, women artists working in the 1990s in New York, using pornographic or otherwise potentially offensive imagery in their work. Eisenman made wall sized paintings depicting pseudo classical scenes. 
!Eliasson Olafur.
Norwegian artist who is interested in the effect of the environment, both built and natural, on humans and the reciprocal effect of humans. Eliassson makes minimal and sometimes barely visible interventions, as in his installation at Museum of Modern Art in New York. This consists of frosted transparent tape applied in vertical stripes, covering the whole glass curtain wall in the foyer, subtly but substantially altering the visitor's experience of this public area. Eliasson has also coloured the water in a harbour bright green, and in 2003 he made a work called the weather project for Tate Modern in London which consisted of a 30 meter diameter semi-circular yellow panel representing the sun, hung at the top of the museums entrance hall, with a ceiling panelled with silver mirror fabric to create a reflection of the sun as if it were in water. A mist made of sugar and water is fed into the hall. Viewers lay on the floor to watch their reflections and to look at the sun.
!Emin Tracy.
British artist who was at the forefront of the Brit Art group that came to prominence in the 1990s in London. Emin was born in the 1960s in Margate, a British seaside town which features prominently in her work. Emin's work is essentially a diary of her life, reflecting on her state of mind particularly in relation to her love life and her emotional life. Emin relates experiences that mostly remain personal, such as her abortion, her rape, her breakdown, drunken and regretted sex, good sex, the break-up of relationships, her problems with alcohol and her feelings about her absent father. These are represented in a wide variety of media: video, drawings, mono-prints, text, neon signs, textiles, embroidery, and installation. Emin’s consistent poetry and diary writing underpins her work.
Her video works include ‘Why I never became a dancer’ made in the early 1990s shows Emin dancing in Margate. In 2000 Emin made a video in which she appears (achieved with editing) twice as good Tracy and bad Tracey, sitting together and chatting on a couch. The two dress differently and behave differently, both are likeable in different ways, one is drinking a cup of tea and the other a glass of wine. It seems to be an interview of some sort, or perhaps a therapy session. The glass of wine emerges as the main difference between the two Tracys, as this film is a tale of the Jekyll and Hyde effect of alcohol.
Emin achieved notoriety when she embroidered and appliquéd the inside of an ordinary one person tent with the names of all the people she had ever slept with. This was quite a long list of mostly men including several male artists, but also siblings and women friends who she had shared a bed with. In order to view the work the viewer had to crawl into the very small tent (which had only room for two), recline since there was no room to stand and become very intimate indeed with both Emin’s work an possibly the other occupant of the tent. 
Emin also embroidered an upholstered chair and made a series of appliquéd and embroidered quilts. The quilts bore text legends and patterns, using patterned and plain fabric and basic embroidery stitches onto bedspreads and blankets. The legends were of life and love and art, (they also featured in the neon signs); ‘I need art like I need god’ being one of the most memorable. The quilts were usually dated. The use of such homely, amateurish and not spectacularly well executed craft techniques allied with such explicit personal statements brought Emin much attention.
Emin had a number of works made in neon, either from her writings or her poetry. The neon letters - either a single sentence or a verse, often in pink, were mounted on the gallery wall. 
Emin consistently made drawings and mono prints, often accompanied by descriptive sentences or titles as part of the image. These are often explicit, showing her menstruating, lying in a drunken heap, happy and in love, masturbating. They are very simply drawn in a childish hand in pen, pencil or a blue or black ink for the mono prints, that belies their effectiveness.
In the mid 1990s Emin opened a shop with another artist, Sarah Lucas, selling trinkets, souvenirs and t-shirts all of which were made by the artists, for example an ashtray with a picture of Damien Hirst's head (which has since become a collectors item). When the shop closed Emin established the Tracey Emin Museum, essentially a live work space which she kept open to the public regularly each week and sold work from. Her insistence on non-exclusivity as manifested by the shop and the museum, coupled with her appropriation of ‘anyone can do it’ craft methods and materials captured the zeitgeist of the time, and contributed to a surge in public interest in contemporary art. Before this was seen as elitist and even if this interest was often prurient, Emin was ultimately persuasive. She also took up many of the areas of investigation of women artist in the 1970s and updated them. Emin has been extremely influential to a whole generation of artists, especially women.
The work that crowned Emin’s reputation (for notoriety or otherwise) was Bed, exhibited in the 2000 Turner Prize exhibition in London’s Tate gallery. This is the bed in which Emin spent many days during her breakdown, drinking, smoking, eating, having sex. The bed and the surrounding detritus, empty bottles, food plates, glasses, pill packets, dirty clothes including underwear, overflowing ashtrays, a soiled tampon, books and magazines, was exhibited on a plinth to great public outcry.
Since 2000 Emin has continued to make works on paper and also to make more sculpture, including a rickety wooden tower which can be climbed, and a stuffed bird on a plinth. Birds have been a part of her personal iconography throughout her career. 
!Espaliu Pepe.
South American artist born in the 1960s who made a piece of work with a large quantity of old clothes, involving him cycling round the trunk of a tree on a platform that encircled the trunk just below the canopy.
!Fabre Jan.
Belgian artist who is very interested in insects. Since the 1970s Fabre has been making large scale theatrical performances usually featuring himself and others dressed as insects or in costumes covered with insects. Fabre also makes objects and paintings encrusted with insects, particularly beetles. The dark iridescence of their wings and shells fascinates him. 
!Fahlstrom Ovind.
!Faier Jonathan.
German artist who made a video with Johannes Rust and one other artist in which they put together a large number of clips from news footage and advertisements featuring corporate logos. They edited it and added an ironic and humorous soundtrack to represent their appropriated material as a fast paced and amusing look at global capitalism and consumerism. This video is an example of a way of working that emerged in the late 1990s as video editing technology became universally accessible so that anyone with a camcorder and a TV could edit video. Johan Grimonprez’s D-I-A-L history is another example.
!Fairhurst, Angus.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes drawings and collages and digitally animated drawing videos.
!Fairnington, Mark.
!Faithfull, Simon.
British artist who has made a number of works for the web.
!Falconbridge, Brian.
Born around 1950, Falconbridge has made very small and detailed finely constructed sculptures, often relying on balance and tension in their construction.
!Farquhar Keith.
German artist who makes drawings in marker pen on found surfaces such as pieces of laminate. The drawings reference the graphics on the covers of 60s and 70s text books.
!Fecteau, Vincent.
Artist working in the 1990s onwards who makes sculptures from wood, sometimes painted, with strong references to modernism.
!Feinstein, Rachel.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes sculptures from particle board and other timber sheet material, polystyrene, paint and a variety of other media. The sheet material is cut into shapes and assembled as the framework of a sculpture. Sometimes it is spray painted. Feinstein's work has a theatrical feel to it, as if she were making a kind of prop or stage set. She made a number of works that looked like futuristic flowers. Later works are made only of painted board cut into shapes, often silhouettes of people. The sculptures are usually no more than two metres in height.
!Fend, Peter.
Artist born in the 1960s who made a film of himself jumping around with only a jumper and a pair of shoes on.
!Fetting, Rainer.
German painter born in the 1950s who made large figurative paintings, for example one of a row of men in the shower, painted in blues and whites, in an expressionistic style.
!Finland, Tom of.
Pencil drawings of gay men variously dressed in leather, naked, sometimes in a state of sexual arousal. The drawings have something of the style of the drawings that used to be shown in barbershop windows to demonstrate hairstyles, realist, detailed, and making use of the full range of pencil drawing techniques. The men in Tom of Finland’s Drawings are often shown in pairs, but sometimes alone. They document specific pleasures and codes of dress very simply and straightforwardly, but for all their sexually explicit subject matter they have a strange ambiguity in terms of their purpose. 
!Finley, Karen.
Karen Finley, an American performance artist whose work, like Mapplethorpe's attracted the attention of the US Christian Right. Finley received NEA money and became notorious when she was banned from performing a work in which she inserted pieces of yam into her vagina. When she brought this work to London the Institute of Contemporary Arts was forced to bow to local authority pressure and ban it. Finley continued to make controversial performance work centring around themes of child abuse, sexual identity and the female body, often in the form of a monologue by a character who was in turns aggressive and profane.
!Finn Kelcey Rose.
British artist born in the 1950s, Finn Kelcey made a number of performance works in the 1980s and early 1990s, one using shadow puppets. In the late 1990s she made Matador, a work for which she trained as a matador in Spain and took part in a bullfight. The work itself existed as documentation. She later made an installation titled Steam Bed, which consisted of a large square brushed steel trough from which copious amounts of steam were emitted. The steam was sucked towards an extractor fan of the same dimensions as the trough, creating an illusory solid form, a cube of steam between the two.
!Fischli and Weiss.
Swiss artists born in the 1950s who work in partnership. Peter Fischli and David Weiss make installation, photographic and video art. They are interested in nature, animals, cause and effect and the human condition. Early works consist of the artists dressed as animals filmed walking in the countryside, a series of realistic replica interiors for example a store room, the artists’ studio in which every item and piece of furniture is modelled from clay and cast in rubber, in a variety of solid colours, producing a simultaneously convincing and perplexing version of reality. 
Fischli and Weiss made a number of photographic works in which they created art works with food. For example, Still Life with Cold Meats shows an arrangement of sliced sausage, frankfurters and gherkins. Photographic work is always part of their practise, most usually presented as a slide projection or light box. Indeed much of Fischli and Weiss’s video work is very static and closer to photography. 
A slide projection featuring hundreds of images of flowers, shown grouped into seasons, each slide dissolving into the next so that a complex double image is created, takes about an hour to view. It has a mesmerising effect. An illuminated table, about 15 meters long and one metre wide is used to display hundreds of images of land and city scapes, each one about 6 cm wide. At first off putting this work also draws the viewer in as they try to make connections between the images and inadvertently construct their own narrative.
Video work is often similarly open-ended, as in one installation of about 12 monitors placed in a room, all showing different images of landscape, but not simultaneously so that some screens were blue while others are playing. The viewer is directed to move about the room by the monitors’ apparently random showings.
Fischli and Weiss’s best known static video work is Pussy, a single shot film of a black and white cat drinking milk from a saucer. This was made to be projected on a huge LED screen above Times Square in New York, where it achieved a monumental quality.
Perhaps their best known video is The Way Things Go, a film of the progression of a series of inanimate objects caused to move by a cause and effect chain of events. Using simple science, physics and common sense, and materials such as wood, metal, a variety of vessels, balls, weights, fire, steam, salt, batteries, and ice, the artists made a series of structures, balances, ramps, slopes, water channels, tubes, pipes and levers that are set in motion be each other over a period of about half and hour. Basic principles of physics utilising steam for expansion, melting, burning, balancing, rocking, tipping and velocity. This is for example used to propel water down a slope into a bucket which tips filling a tank with something floating on the surface which when it reaches a ball cock pushes a weight down on a platform which sets a ball rolling and so on. This piece is filmed in an empty warehouse space. 
Fischli and Weiss often make questions part of their work, as if they are involved in some kind of research. The question with which they are most often associated is ‘Are animals People?’ At the Venice biennale in 2003 they presented a work in which they simply projected a series of questions on a wall in enormous letters, one of which was ‘Will Happiness Find Me?’
!Fleury Sylvie.
French artist working from the 1980s onwards. Fleury is interested in consumerism and in the relation between fashion and desire. One work consisted of shopping bags from different high fashion shops, placed in a casual arrangement on a plinth. The bags themselves took on a significance beyond their actual existence as carrier of luxury goods, they seemed to be as good as the good they might have contained, to even transcend them, so clearly did their names, logos, colours and designs advertise a particular way of life, style and aspiration. Fleury also made works with shoes.
!Floyer Ceal.
British artist born in the 1960s who has made many works using light as her main medium. However Floyer's earliest works are a pair of brass door fingerplates with the inscriptions pushed and pulled on them, and a red dot of the kind galleries use to indicate that a work has been sold, which in reality was a hole in the wall filled with red pigment. Both are poetic interventions into the mundane, which also characterises Floyer’s whole body of work. 
A lot of Floyer’s early works are slide projections. Light switch, made in the early 1990s, is a slide projection of a photograph of an ordinary household light switch, projected onto a wall where a light switch might be. The projector is not concealed at all. The projection looks exactly like a real light switch prompting a double take, and the viewer, surprisingly, does not readily associate the projector with the image. Floyer achieved full suspension of disbelief by presenting something utterly ordinary in a magical way. Light Under The Door consisted of a projector placed in front of a door, projecting a strip of light the exact dimensions of the space under the door, at this space. The effect was of an incredibly strong light was coming from behind the door, enough to make the viewer want to investigate, when in fact the light source was readily visible at the viewers feet.
In a similar vein, a photograph of an illuminated light bulb was projected on to a switched off light bulb, making it a light source in a new way. A light was projected down through a light bulb, projecting the text on the bulb (brand name, wattage etc) onto the ground below, again prompting a kind of double take since when this work is viewed it seems like something that should happen every time a light bulb is switched on.
Floyer began to work with sound in the late 1990s, presenting a work in which the sound of a slide projector is recorded and pressed onto vinyl, which is played on a turntable on a plinth. The viewer looks for the slide projection, which is not there, and eventually associates what they are hearing with what they see. 
This slippage, between what is heard and seen, or what is seen and understood is Floyer’s main interest. Around the same time, she made a video of a dog just outside the frame, jumping into the frame regularly leaping for an invisible ball. Once again it takes a certain amount of watching to work out what is happening. An apparently abstract static image suddenly moves and turns out to be a white net curtain against a window which occasionally flaps in the breeze, and when it touches the window becomes more transparent. A piece of screwed up white paper, fluttering in an eddy of wind in an empty cul-de-sac, resembles, when the film is looped, a white butterfly forever fluttering in the same place. 
Floyer’s interest in light extended to an interest in all things white, and indeed she has not used colour at all unless the object she wishes to use is coloured. A till receipt is placed on the wall and seems to be nothing more than that, until the viewer realises that every object on the list is white in colour (milk, flour, lard etc.) 
In 2003 Floyer made a work for the Tate gallery in London. She installed a bell ringing at regular intervals, a bit like the bell that summons an audience into an auditorium. This created a sense of expectation, and a feeling in the viewer that they ought to obey the abstract summons causing them to look for a direction and finally to look towards where they perceive the sound to be emanating from. This act of looking is what the artist wanted to achieve. Another sound work is a recording of a drip in a bucket, played on a cassette player placed in a bucket, causing the viewer to look to the ceiling or if not to think about the sound they are hearing as being generated by a drip coming from the ceiling which they know is not there. Floyer’s project has been to reveal the beauty in the nearly intangible.
!Ford Laura.
British artist born in 1960s who made a number of installation works in the 1980s concerned with fairy tales and the imagination, including one performance featuring a person in a bear suit. In the 1990s Ford made a large installation of actual size stuffed toy children, made in chintz fabrics of different colours, in a range of poses.
!Forg Gunther.
German artist born in the 1950s who makes paintings, photographs and bronze sculpture. Forg typically places a large (two meter high) black and white photograph in a frame butted up against an abstract painting of a similar size, consisting of very broad straight single brushstrokes and rectangular areas of colour in for examples orangey red and green. His photographs are very often of women (head and shoulders) who look serious. Forg’s bronze sculptures are the same size as the paintings and photographs, in fact they could be described as sculptures of paintings. The bronzes are about 30 cm deep and two metres high rectangular panels standing, in portrait format, on bases which slope straight down from, and are one piece with the panels. The surface of the bronze is covered with impasto marks presumably made in clay with a palette knife, as if to indicate that this might be a cast of a painting. These monolithic objects are placed around the gallery space in relation to the works on the wall.
!Fortnum Rebecca. 
Birtish artist born in the 1960s whose abstract paintings made in the 1980s and 90s have been described as feminist abstraction. Fortnum often painted a cut or slit as a metaphor for the female body, the interior, a metaphor employed by many women artists in the 1980s and early 90s (which can also be seen in the work of Cathy de Monchaux). This arose from readings of Lacan, a French psychoanalyst whose ideas about ‘lack’ in the female psyche and about language and femininity were taken up by feminist artists and theorists. Fortnum was also interested in a general discussion about the female body as a ‘text’ (in the sense of the term used in literary theory) and the ‘writing out’ or performing of that text being essential to the representation of femininity. Fortnum’s paintings, symbolise this lack, and the writing out of the female, by the painted slit or cut, (never an actual cut) and by text she writes on the canvas, sometimes unreadable writing like marks, sometimes actual words. 
Fortnum’s paintings are very large, up to three metres high, and she predominantly used dark reds and browns. The paintings are a fairly flat colour with brush marks, except where she has chosen to paint the slit, which is made with very thick paint squeezed and brushed. Writing is done with a small brush in a flowing hand, usually a single line of text running horizontally across the painting.
!Francois Michel.
Belgian artist born 1950s who makes large scale photographic installations. 
!Frankland John.
British artist born around 1960 who makes sculpture and installation. Frankland's early works were a series of site specific installations in which he used plastic sheeting, stretched over battens, to line the walls of a space. In such a way he created a second skin inner wall that appeared to be very solid and shiny but was in fact only a thin layer of cheap plastic. He did this in black plastic in a cellar, in silver grey plastic on a freestanding form that looked like a giant metal wall, and in gold metal along a wall in a gallery. In this last work, Lift, he arranged the battening and supports so that there was the illusion of minimal gold metal lift doors set into a gold wall, with a hemispherical convex form pushed out from the back of the plastic to represent the button for calling the lift the only detail.
Following this, Frankland made a work in which he painted a section of a tree trunk in bright yellow high gloss paint, using so many layers that the details of the trunk are smoothed out, giving it a first impression look of a model tree trunk. The trunk is mounted on the wall at chest height defying its tremendous weight. There is a contradiction between the possible lightness of the trunk if it is a fibreglass cast, and the reality of the weight of the wood.
Frankland later made a site specific work for a sculpture park which consisted of his selection of a one ton boulder from a quarry placed in a site of his choice.
!Fritsch Katharina
Artist working in the late 1990s and early 2000s.
!Frize Bernard.
German artist who makes process paintings, using multiple clear and bright colours of thin paint in several distinct and apparently continuous brushstrokes, wet into wet, to create a simple repeated pattern. The means of execution of the painting is puzzling as it appears that it would be impossible for the brush strokes to be juxtaposed in the way that they are.
!Gallaccio Anya.
British artist born in the 1960s who has made a number of installations since the 1990s. One of her first installations involved paining the walls of a gallery with chocolate. The colour was reminiscent of a dirty protest, the smell in contrast overpoweringly sweet. She also made a curtain to hang in a gallery window from dark orange gerbera flower heads strung on threads the height of the window.  
!Gang Chen.
Chinese artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings of luggage seen on airport x ray machines.
!Gaston Vidya. 
Artist working in partnership with another artist who has made work in the 1990s and 2000s consisting of woollen pompoms placed on Perspex boxes. The pompoms are made in the way that children make them, by threading wool around two circles of card with a hole in the middle and then cutting between the circles. They are made in a range of sizes and colours, usually muted, from about 2 cm to 60 cm in diameter. The plinths are various sized too. The two artists have made other kinds of work together.
!Genovese Ana.
British sculptor born in the 1970s who make small scale models of large environments or tracts of land, for example deserts. A desert complete with an oasis might be modelled on a domestic scale. Genovese also makes fibreglass ‘puddles’.
!Genzken Isa.
German artist who makes sculptural objects and collages. The objects might be concrete forms reminiscent of building materials placed together to form rudimentary architectural structures of about three metres in height. Or long wooden spear like forms four metres tapered at both ends. Other works include collections of metal cooking utensils, such as sieves, whisks, knives, colanders, graters and peelers, strung together with chains and hung in clusters on the wall. Presented in this way these banal domestic objects seem somehow sexual and perverse. Genzken also made a series of 30 cm square collages of nudes cut up and re-joined to make strange human forms. 
In 2002 Genzken made a series of small sculptures that look like maquettes for skyscrapers, made from found materials such as fruit crates, cellophane, printed matter, cardboard and glass, and roughly formed with copious amounts of glue oozing from the joins. This work is about the re-building of Berlin, the changing from a faded modernist architecture to the new skyscrapers
!Gersht Ori.
Artist working in the 1990s and 2000s who took a panoramic photo of a London football stadium and presented it on 25 metre gallery wall
!Gerz Jochen and partner.
German artist who worked with his wife. The Gerzs realised several public sculptures most notably two holocaust memorials. The Sinking Monument is a lead covered square column which is gradually sinking into the ground on which it stands. Over a number of years it will eventually fill a hole exactly its size and disappear for ever. The soft lead surface invites graffiti, as it is very easy to scratch into. In a reversal of the customary civic practise of removing any graffiti or making it difficult to carry out, the Gerz’s column asks the viewer to add to it. The idea is that much of the graffiti will be concerned with the holocaust and that people will engrave the names of those they lost. The monument will take these names underground and they will remain there forever, a subterranean plinth to a memorial plaque on the surface. 
The other memorial is sited in front of the Reichstag in Berlin. The Gerzs researched with help from student volunteers into a number of Jewish cemeteries known to have been destroyed and built over. Lists of the names of those buried in these places were obtained. At night they removed cobblestones from the street in front of the building and carved the name of one of the deceased on the bottom. The stones would then be replaced. Eventually all of those whose graves had been lost were symbolically reburied in a permanent and subversive act.
!Gillick Liam.
British artist who born in around 1960 who works in a variety of media. Text is integral to Gillick's work both visually and because he has written a number of books which he also makes the designs for, for example Erasmus is Late (with a yellow cover, the title punched through in ticker tape lettering), and published texts which are part of his practise. In both texts and visual work he explores the legacy and iconography of modernism, in particular its architectural and graphic forms, and urban and social space. There is a strongly liberal political aspect to Gillick’s work. He often uses quotation relating to politically significant historical events (such as the English civil war) in his work, an approach which ultimately gives Gillick’s work a didactic quality. 
For Gillick the design of printed material is of paramount importance, equal to the construction of his sculptural works.
For example Gillick’s ‘Conversation Platforms’ are panels made from squares of apparently random coloured Perspex sheets set in an aluminium framework to make a grid. The panel is then hung above head height and the viewer will be standing under it. These works have expanded into more complex and architectural structures, featuring sections of wall as well as platforms, and even a huge suspended ceiling, always using different colours of Perspex. 
Gillick has also made a large number of works on paper, many of them proposals for graphic design projects or architectural designs for the facades of buildings. In addition to his ideas for transforming public spaces he makes proposals for social events, some of which are carried out, as for example when he scheduled a programme of yoga classes to take place in a gallery under and around his Perspex platforms.
Gillick’s approach is ideological; part social scientist, part philosophical theorist, part artist, part liberal political. He presents a vision of the world which has clean lines, perfectly considered typographic style, well designed building, where history can be reconsidered and there is a lot to read. 
!Glass Kersten.
British artist born in the 1970s who paints images of female models from magazines, juxtaposed in the manner of a collage, with a text title running across.
