The women are painted realistically, and are fashionably dressed and made up often in punk and Goth style. The paintings have echoes of 80s design. 
!Gluzberg, Margarita.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes very realistic detailed pencil drawings of women, about two metres high. 
!Gober Robert. 
American artist born in the 1960s, Gober’s work consider the human body and landscape. He has made a series of works using wax casts of his body parts, for example legs and hands. These are realistically finished down to the last hair, and installed in odd ways, for example a leg and foot may project from the wall at a right angle. He has made many installations featuring body parts, for example a room papered with the artist’s own design of wall paper, a forest of trees with sinister things going on among them and leg sculptures sticking out of wall. Another theme is that of ‘orifices’. Gober will often make circular openings with metal facings and plugholes, highly finished but ambiguous in terms of position and function. It is as if the external world is seen as a metaphor for the human body, and the orifices in the world might lead to a mysterious interior which provokes both curiosity and repulsion. 
Everything Gober makes has a highly detailed finish and exactly specified materials. In the early 2000s he began to make huge scale installations, for example a large gallery space which is empty apart from a small grill in the floor. Looking through this grill it is possible to discern an apparently real river bed and pool stretching under the floor, creating the impression that the whole gallery floor is covering a piece of nature. Somewhere there is a figure of Christ.
Gober also made a piece of work featuring a bridal dress.
!Godfrey-Isaacs Laura.
British artist born around 1960 who made wall reliefs that eventually led to polyurethane foam sculptures reminiscent of the work of Lydia Benglis. The foam in Godfrey-Isaacs’ sculptures is brightly coloured with forms often jokily alluding to the female body. Godfrey-Isaacs ceased working as an artist in the late 1990s and opened her own home as a gallery and venue.
!Golden, Pamela.
British artist who makes paintings.
!Goldin Nan.
American artist born in the 1950s. During the 1980s and early 1990s Goldin photographed her own life obsessively. Every detail of the lives of her friends in New York was recorded as she spent time with them. People are photographed sleeping, eating, sober, drunk, fixing up, having sex, getting dressed, in the bath, on the toilet, kissing, cooking, talking, singly and in groups, meeting the camera or looking away. Most of them are artists, musicians, actors and their friends. There are straight and gay couples, single parents, families and children. She creates a sense of a window on private lives but never a sense of intrusion. A subject might have a black eye, or be menstruating. The subjects are always relaxed in the presence of the camera.
The resulting hundreds of images were exhibited in groups, usually printed fairly small, approximately 50 cm high. The groups of images became larger and they were installed in grid formation. Ultimately they were shown as a continuously running slide projection of hundreds of images. 
Goldin’s work became a testament to and a document of the lives of a generation. This is particularly poignant because many of her friends were gay of the generation that was most drastically affected by deaths from the AIDS related disease. Thus this body of work is also memorial. However it is not in any sense melancholic, rather it is experienced as a record of the intimate lives of one generation of mostly young New Yorkers. 
The success of Goldin’s work led to her being sought after for commissioned projects, which she resisted, preferring to continue to record her life and the lives of others. 
!Goldstein Jack.
American artist born in the 1950s who has made a number of 16 mm films and videos. Goldstein’s work often has a formal simplicity that belies his choice of medium. He may choose to film something that is barely there, that hardly moves. He uses animation on top of the film, for example in a film of a willow pattern plate, which show simply the plate seen face on, in a static shot, one of the birds in the pattern comes to life and flies off the plate. In another a figure of a person is outlined in tiny red lights and moves, the lights shining blurrily.
!Goldsworthy Andy.
British artist born in the 1950s who makes land art – site specific sculptures placed in the landscape. Goldsworthy began in the 1980s to make small sculptures from leaves and twigs, which he would photograph in the landscape. The photograph is the document of the work. Sometimes he arranged leaves, for example grading the different colours of fallen autumn leaves and arranging them in a pattern. Constructing sculptures and floating them on water. But he also used growing plants, staking the branches of willow trees to form a living structure, weaving twigs into circles. He then began to use snow to make sculptural forms. 
Goldsworthy’s work always relates the natural materials and the environment in which they are found to the final form that the artist creates for them. It also makes associations with the history of ritual and nature and the resulting artefacts.
Gradually Goldsworthy’s work became larger scale. He travelled widely in search of new landscapes to work with, for example to Alaska. 
!Gontarski Steven.
American artist born in the 1970s who makes figurative sculptures. Gontarski began in the late 90s to make life-size figures made from fabric sewn tighter in the style of a stuffed toy on top of an armature. The figures were distorted human forms made from shiny plastics and vinyl, elongated and featureless, their limbs often joining and intertwined. The combinations of fabric alluded to garments. Sometimes a part of a garment might be incorporated into the sculpture. The lumpen feet might have a stripe referring to, but looking nothing like a trainer. Gontarski's soft sculptures are dancers, made from a combination of formal sculptural forms of example Henry Moore with club wear and fashion. Along with the sculptures he made drawings on acetate, and later on Perspex sheet. These were simple outlines in fibre tip marker featuring people that he had met and odd lines from their conversation.
In 2000 Gontarski began to make similar figures from cast fibreglass, finished in one colour of a high gloss lacquer often pearlescent or metallic. The figures began to take on a molten morphology, like a mixture of Rodin, Boccioni and the molten baddy in the film Terminator 2. The figures twist and dance, and are placed on plinths made of the same material and of a part with the sculpture. The plinths are sprayed with graffiti, by graffiti artists that Gontarski has invited. 
Gontarski also made a series of busts in a similar way. These refer to the generic bronze bust which one might see anywhere. In Gontarski’s smoothed out and rendered version, there might be a hole right through the face. One is a virgin Mary figure painted in white.
!Gonzales-Torres Felix. 
American artist born around 1960 who made sculptures and installations. Gonzales-Torres died in the late 1990s. During his lifetime his father died as did many of his friends from AIDS related illness. His work is preoccupied with death. 
Gonzales-Torres used a variety of media, usually with minimal intervention on his part. The work he did was in choosing his material, decisions about quantity and placement. For example he presented a simple stack of his own weight in sheets of newsprint. The weight of his dead father in ivory coloured sweets, is laid out in an amorphous shape on the floor, the sweets are the same colour as bone and the visitor is invited to take one away. A way of giving out the body of his father in an obvious parallel with the Transubstantiation. This is one of a series of candy works, using different colours of sweets arranged in different abstract shaped heaps on the floor according to the subject of the work (given in the title) always to be taken away so that the work changes through interaction. Another series of works are bead curtains, floor to ceiling, strung across the width of a gallery to bisect or divide it creating a new space, partially transparent, the beads are of certain specified colours. One work included a performative element. A Male dancer naked except for silver shorts and boots danced on a podium surrounded by lights. When the dancer wasn’t there the podium remained as the work, a plinth for an absent sculpture, the dancer, symbolic of death, the final curtain and the performance of life.
Gonzales Torres also made a series of light bulb works, strings of standard light bulbs to be strung in specific locations, in a tree, a stairwell for example. He also made photographic works, the most well known of which is an empty bed with white sheets each of the pillows bearing the impressions left by a head. Another is a photograph of flowers growing in bare soil, which is titled The Grave of Gertrude Stein. Gonzales-Torres’ work deals with death, the fleeting nature of life, and the idea of the memorial, by using the simplest sculptural forms. 
!Gordon Douglas.
British artist born in the 1960s who works in a range of media, including photography, video and text. Gordon is fascinated with the visual representation of psychological states, particularly in film.
He made an installation for which he covered the walls with a list of all the people he knows, their names applied in self adhesive lettering in columns. Gordon often uses this type of lettering. A small work appeared in a book consisting of a text about looking which ends by stating that by the time the reader has read the text, they will have been reading for 3 minutes. He makes a comparison between the time taken to read and the time taken to look at images.
Gordon has made a number of video and film works. He often uses back projection onto freestanding or hanging screens in the gallery. One work shows a film from the thirties of two psychoanalysts talking to and subsequently restraining a hysterical female patient, finally pinning her down. The film is presented played forwards and backwards, resulting in very different interpretations on the viewers part. This deconstruction of found film led eventually to Gordon’s best known work, 24 Hour Psycho. The film of the same name is projected the gallery wall, slowed down so that it takes 24 hours to play from start to finish. The gallery remains open during this time. The Resulting film provokes all kinds of new readings, and the extreme slow motion renders the famous shower scene entirely different. 
Gordon has made many works on paper including a number of photographs of film stars with the eyes cut out or replaced with plain paper, and a number of photographs of apparent actions such as his black finger tattoo, a photograph of his finger which is entirely black. 
!Gormley Antony.
British artist born in the 1950s who became very well known in Britain when he made a large scale public commission called The Angel of the North. This 30 meter high sculpture is a standing figure with no facial features or detail. Its outstretched arms have become aeroplane wings, the arms spanning about 40 meters. The sculpture, which is made from steel is sited by the motorway and railway line from London to Scotland. It can be seen for miles around, and is probably the most visible and best known piece of public art in Britain.
Gormley made lead casts of parts of his body for a number of years in the 1980s and 90s. He then began to cast his whole body and make sculptures from beaten moulded and welded lead sheet. The resulting figures are life size and have no detail or features. Where the lead sheets meet they are seamed as if they are made of stitched together pieces of fabric. Some are prone and some are standing. Gormley has conducted a most thorough investigation into the male human form, using a visual anonymity to allow the figures to stand for every person. 
Gormley became known to a much wider public when he made the participatory work Field in the early 1990s. Field consists of hundreds of 40 cm high clay figures, roughly alike, placed shoulder to shoulder in a mass on the floor of the gallery. Field was partly inspired by the terracotta army of seventh century Chinese sculptures found in a burial ground.
Wherever field is exhibited it is created anew by local people, each person making their own figure from local clay. 
!Graham Dan.
American artist working in from the 1970s onwards, Graham makes architectural sculptures, usually site specific, from glass and mirrors. He creates simple structures from plate glass that invite the viewer to enter them. The reflections or the surrounding landscape and of the structure itself cause a sense of disorientation, in particular in terms of which parts of the sculpture are transparent and which are reflective. In short, a confusion about what is a real image and the nature of that reality. The structures are extremely simple and formal, usually human height, often no more that a series of vertical planes placed together at angles and ramps. They are usually placed in the open air as the light of the sky plays an important role in the reflectivity that is part of the work.
!Graham Rodney.
Canadian artist born in the 1950s who since the 1980s has made works in film, video, installation, photography and sound. Graham made a video showing himself as Robinson Crusoe lying beneath a palm tree sleeping until a coconut drops onto his head from the tree. He sits ups, rubs his eyes, tries to focus, feels dizzy and lies down only to have the same thing happen again, and in fact the film is a loop. This work portrays the endless loop of time lived out by Crusoe while stranded. Another film shows a house burning down. 
Graham made a sound work in which he took a segment of a piece of classical music which is not usually played when that piece is performed, analysed it and added it to make a playable loop. He then arranged for an orchestra to play it and had it recorded.
Graham took a photograph of a tree in winter, and presented it upside down. The branches of the tree become the roots, and the roots the branches. On the theme of the inverted or retinal image Gordon also installed a pinhole camera in a horse drawn carriage, giving the viewer the opportunity to enter the carriage and view the scene outside through the camera upside down. 
!Green Gregory.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes installation and sculpture. In the early 1990s Green made an installation in a small room for which he constructed an imaginary IRA bomb factory from scratch by reading books and information freely available to the public. The process of bomb making is shown taking place in a small kitchen, the materials needed strewn everywhere, empty cups and ashtrays, food and food wrappers, plates the evidence of long hours of work taking place. The drain gin board contains canisters of weed killer and bags of sugar, the materials used to make explosives, and there are five litre plastic bottles with liquid undergoing some process of transformation. A table is crammed with electrical equipment and tools, mostly ordinary domestic materials such as light switches, plugs, fuses, wire, insulating tape, cable connectors. There is a bomb maker’s manual open on the table. The verisimilitude is such that the viewer cannot avoid the feeling that they are trespassing in a place where an illegal activity is taking place. The viewer feels jumpy, as if they might be discovered by the perpetrators who they feel might be dangerous. The effect of the installation is to show how close to daily life, how enmeshed in the everyday is the potential to kill.
Green also made several bombs complete but lacking explosive and detonators. These attracted the attention of the tabloid press and in some cases the gallery that exhibited was forced to remove them. One was a suitcase bomb, one was even a nuclear device, complete except for the plutonium.
Subsequently Green set up a pirate radio station and broadcast illegally to a free state that he had created.
!Green Renee.
American artist born in the 1960s, Green is known for her large scale interactive installations which have a socio-political genesis. In the late 1990s Green made an installation that filled a gallery with a maze like partition board structure consisting of a series of spaces, the walls painted in different colours, sometimes plain, sometimes striped. Viewers walked through the structure, choosing to stop where they wished. Videos are presented on monitors on the floor with bean bags and headphones strewn about. Written participation in a survey is invited. Photographs are on the walls. One space has sitting room furniture. One has a record collection and a record player. Overall the viewer enters a microcosmic world that represents daily life and is asked to consider and participate in activities that illustrate how identity is constructed and how it might be possible to think in new ways about its construction. 
!Grimonprez Johan.
Belgian artist who made a video work in the late 1990s D-I-A-L History. This projected film consists of vast amount of excerpted footage from newsreels, film and television that concerns terrorism, specifically the hijacking of an aeroplane. Grimonprez has edited this footage to make a short film showing the history of hijacking and the imagery that we have of such events. 
!Group Material. 
Toronto based group of artists/activists, producing collaborative works, solo works, publishing, running a gallery, and commissioning projects and books. Including among their number the painter Jochen Klein.
!Grunefeld Thomas.
German artist born in the 1950s known for his stuffed animal sculptures in which different parts of animals are combined to form one hybrid creature that could never exist. Grunefeld uses highly skilled taxidermists to renders these creatures objectively completely convincing as if they were the result of some kind of perverse genetic engineering project. The animals, for example a roster’s feet on a furry mammals body with the tail of a lizard, could have no imaginable purpose, thus they seem like contemporary fantastic beasts. 
!Guerilla Girls.
Group of anonymous feminist activists who were working in New York in the late 80s and early 90s, the Guerilla Girls were named for their wearing of gorilla masks when they went to organised demonstrations. They also used the banana as an emblem, along with the gorilla suit a prop of slapstick humour and a visual pun on both the diet of gorillas and the offering of an opportunity to institutions who fail to represent women adequately to ‘slip up’ (on the proffered banana skins). The group is thought to have included a number of members who went on to become prominent artists, for example Sue Williams. The Guerilla Girls were very much a product of an era, when feminism was well established and energy could be turned from discriminatory practises in everyday life, work and the home, to those in culture. The group produced a number of seminal posters and postcards for example a list of reasons ‘Why There are no Great Women Artists’, and a list of statistics about the proportional representation of women artists in art museums. These texts achieved widespread distribution because they were clever in their use of graphics and advertising to get message across, and because the appearance of the gorilla suited members staging demonstrations at institutions and relevant events was always newsworthy.
!Gunning Lucy.
British artist born in the 1970s who made a video titled Climbing Round my Room (mid 1990s). Gunning negotiated the perimeter of her room without touching the floor, which involved climbing to the top of a wardrobe and balancing precariously on various items of furniture and using door handles and steps. The resulting video presented a re-articulation of an ordinary domestic space. 
!Gursky Andreas.
Swiss photographer who takes pictures of banal scenes in which the minute is contrasted with the gigantic, creating a formal image which in some cases becomes almost abstract. Gursky heightens this effect by manipulating the image in the darkroom, often altering the perspective so that the image reads as a flat picture plane rather than a representation of perspective. There is usually a repetitive pattern created by the presence in the images of a mass of something whether it be people, windows, etc. These effects are subtle and subversive.
Examples of Gursky's work from the 1990s include a container park at the port of Ferrara, where the bright colours of the containers form a patchwork pattern on the background of red soil and glowing pink light. The floor of a Siemens factory is a mainly white image in which the black lines for the production line furniture and the bright colours for the electrical cable supplies create a pattern. The Japanese stock exchange is a pattern in yellow and black with white added by the presence of cascading tickets and the striped jackets of the trader. An expanse of plain carpet seems to be vast, skiers on slope are sticks of colour in a white space, a race course in Japan can be read as horizontal bands of colour, the display case in a Prada shoe shop is alive with glowing jewelled colour, a shopping centre is presented as bands of brass and greenery as the escalator travel down through the main atrium, a vast apartment block is a pattern of squares, a canal and dyke are horizontal stripes 
!Gussin Graham.
British artist born 1960s who makes drawings and sometimes installations. Around 1999 Gussin made a sound sensitive floor that made sounds in response to the viewer’s footsteps, producing a re-articulation of the viewer’s action in looking. Gussin made some drawings of the sound produced in this way using a computer programme that represented the sound waves as a perspective drawing.
!Gygi Fabrice.
Swiss artist born in the 1960s who makes installations. These consist of structures in metal wood rubber and plastic, which apparently have practical function but in fact are difficult to decipher. The structures or groups of structures might fill a room. They have frames made from some kind of metal pole, put together using clamps and off the shelf heavy duty fixings. There might be a screen like structure, a bench like one, a platform. The frames might be filled in with plastic or rubber sheeting, hemmed and edged with large eyelets and then laced to the frame. A bench might have an upholstered top. The elements reference the gym and the club, which here seems to be an arena that implies sex and possibly violence. Yet they are benign mysterious objects that invite human interaction.
!Hale Matthew.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes performance videos and assembled sculptures from household objects that hint at performative action. Hale was one of the founders of City Racing, an artist-run gallery in London in the 1990s.
!Hambling Maggi.
British artist who began painting portraits in the 1960s.
!Hamilton Finlay Ian.
Scottish artist who since the 1970s has made, and continues to make site specific outdoor installations. Hamilton Finlay carves text into stone panels and blocks, inscribed in simple classical fonts, single words and phrases that relate to landscape in which they are placed. His intention is to integrate words with landscape. These installations are sometimes small scale but may also be installed over a large area of land, such as in a park. The installation will often be permanent. Hamilton Finlay's work is probably best known as photographic documentation.
!Hammons David.
Artist whose work investigates cultural and ethnic identity.
!Hapaska Siobhan.
Irish artist born in 1965 who makes sculptures and installation. An early work was eight identical life casts of a clothed young man, painted in buff colour, bearing a coffin. Made in 1990s, this work seemed like a comment on the number of young men who were dying from AIDS related illnesses at that time. He has also made a vaguely heart shaped wall mounted ‘speaker’ constructed from teak veneer and purple felt, with a heartbeat sound playing from it. St Christopher Legless was included in a room sized installation featuring a fibreglass body cast or lifelike sculpture of the saint in rags and long black beard, sitting against the wall with amputated or cut off legs, an irony for this patron saint of travellers, or a cruel blow dealt to him deliberately. Echoes of kneecapping punishment in the conflict in Northern Ireland. There was also a fibreglass sculpture of a motorbike, whose contours and mechanics had been smoothed out and filled in so that the whole bike turned into a kind of futuristic vehicle. This was indeed reminiscent of the sculptures of Boccioni but also somehow of Rodin. It was sprayed in a pearlescent white and highly polished. Also a large bedlike structure, a futuristic sleeping platform made from shiny white lacquered fibreglass, the platform with ergonomic curved base, with a slit running all round the inset mattress area, from which water was pumped and ran over the sides of the base in a constant water curtain. The mattress of shaggy fur, with a harness and headphones suggesting that one could strap oneself in and be transported, not literally but mentally, by the comfort and the sound and the trickling water. The bed, like the motorbike, seemed to come from science fiction.
!Haring Keith.
American artist, now dead, who began as a graffiti artist in the early 1980s in New York. Haring developed a distinctive graphic style, using bold lines to outline his figures, animals and symbols, and filling them in with plain flat colour. Once he became known, Haring began to make his work on canvas and made a lot of printed works, books and ultimately merchandise. Today Haring’s work is very recognisable. He used a simple lexicon of symbols and figures, always outlining them in black and filling the background in with small marks. Hearts, hands, and figures, much simplified, often appear. The figures usually in active poses, rarely have features. One of his best known images is of a red heart on a yellow background, the heart surrounded by lines as if to indicate that it is beating. He most often used the primary colours yellow and red, and black.
Haring made a lot of political posters about AIDS, and his instantly recognisable graphic style was particularly suited to campaigning on various issues. 
!Harris Mark.
British artist born around 1960 who makes conceptual work.
!Harrison David.
British artist born in the 1950s who makes autobiographical sculpture, paintings and drawings. Harrison’s work has a naïve quality, the paintings are small, around 60 cm high, and sometimes awkwardly painted. The drawings are made in pencil. In his early work in the 1980s Harrison concentrated almost exclusively on painting cats with a highly stylised 50s appearance. Later Harrison began to describe his own life and lives like his more literally in terms of encounters with people, animals and landscape. For example, several paintings feature a caravan in a rural setting, and include the name of a purported guest to the caravan in the title, for example British footballer Paul Gascoigne. In some of the caravan paintings a mysterious white rabbit hovers, ghost like, as if it represents the spirit of something. 
Harrison’s sculptures are assemblages of objects to resemble animals, in the spirit of Picasso. Forks, table legs and any kind of discarded objects are given new roles as the legs of a heron, a bird’s beak. The sculptures junk like appearance belies their accurate resemblance to the animal they represent. 
!Harvey Marcus.
Marcus Harvey is a British painter born in the 1970s who is interested in using paint and its application to produce a hidden image, or conversely to produce an image by an unusual means of application of paint. Harvey made a series of works in the 1990s, which on first inspection appear to be abstract paintings with lots of impasto and brushstrokes. In fact superimposed are outlines of women taken from pornographic images painted in thin black lines merging into the abstraction. Once recognised, however the images are very clear. 
In 1999 Harvey made a painting of Myra Hindley, a convicted child murderer still serving her sentence at the time, which became notorious. This painting became famous by the constant press interest in the story. Harvey made the ten metres high portrait, taken from the police mug shot, from the handprints of children. The handprints in varying shades from white to dark grey form a kind of pixel effect, creating and image that only appears in full detail from distance, much like the effect of a billboard poster
!Hasewaga Jun.
Korean artist working in Britain, born in the 1970s. Hasewaga makes paintings of people on board, in shiny gloss paint with solid outlines, and then cut out.
!Hatoum Mona.
British artist born in the 1950s in a Middle Eastern country, who makes installation and video work. Hatoum’s work investigates the extremes of human nature and cruelty, alluding to the repressive practises of certain regimes in the Middle East and their effect on the life of women in particular. An early video work shows the veiled face of a woman with the shadows of Arabic text falling across her face. At a certain point a knife is drawn across her eyes as if to cut them open.
A later installation consists of a long totally dark corridor constructed within a gallery, at the end of which is dimly visible a red glow. The curious viewer will walk along the corridor and the glow becomes brighter, revealing itself to be several vertical bars of red light. Only at close proximity does it become apparent that the light source is electric heater elements. The effect of this is palpable, the viewer has the sense that they might burn themselves, that the red hot bars have somehow lured them towards them.
Another installation that uses a similar economy of means consists of three banks of wire mesh lockers, arranged to create a ‘room’. Hanging from the ceiling is an unshaded electric light bulb which slowly descends and rises into and out of the locker space. The shadows cast by the wire gridded construction of the lockers grow large and shrink in the most sinister fashion. In the late 1990s Hatoum moved on from the almost theatrical effects created by these installations to create more video work. In a video installation called Deep Throat displayed in a wooden kiosk, Hatoum showed a film made by a medical camera travelling through the various orifices of a woman’s body. Like all her work the visceral impact of this work leads to secondary reflections on imprisonment and torture.
!Head Tim.
British artist born around 1950, since the late 1970s Head has made work in a variety of media. He has often focussed on generic and graphic imagery which he has abstracted and reconstructed in various ways, both as sculptural form, painting and works on paper. In the 2000s Head began to work with digital animations, using the pixels that usually constitute the image of video and manipulating their colours to create abstract colour fields of constantly changing coloured squares. These are projected very large, and make immediate visual connection with pointillism and with the dot matrix printing process familiarised by advertising hoardings. Head has created a moving abstract work from pixels, literally the stuff that images are now made from. 
!Helbig, Thomas.
!Hemsworth Gerard.
British painter (born 1940s) who has taken generic graphic forms and visual jokes as his subject matter. Hemsworth's paintings are made with entirely flat smooth colour and hard edged lines. Typically he will choose one or several diagrammatic representations, such as a cloud, a rabbit or a vase for example, simplify this to the minimum number of lines required and paint it on the canvas in black. 
!Hepworth Barbara.
!Higgs Matthew.
British curator and artist born in the early 60s who makes works on paper. Higgs’s dry witty conceptual work re-presents book covers, pages from books and titles, making plays on words and puns. He will often make a text work in which a book, or a title is transformed by minimal intervention into a reference to the visual. One series of works involved obliterating all the words on a page except the words ‘There is a painting on the wall’ and placing this framed page next to a painting by another artist. 
Higgs also made an artists publication series called Imprint 93 (from 1993 onwards) for which he invited artist to make a small pamphlet. This activity bridges his practise as an artists and his work as a curator.
!Hiller Susan.
British artist born in the 1950s, Hiller makes installations usually with video which is often projected, works on paper and often three dimensional objects and structures. She is interested myth, religion, ritual, literature, grand narrative, and psychoanalysis. Belshazzar's Feast made in the late 80s takes the biblical reference of its title as a starting point for one work. A sitting room ‘set’ with sofas, coffee table, lamps and sideboard contains a television where the fireplace would be. On the television is a film of showing a burning fire, as if to illustrate that TV is the hearth around which we gather. On the wall is automatic writing (a reference to the writing on the wall by the unseen hand that takes place at Belshazzar’s Feast) done by Hiller.
Another work features giant shadow puppets of Punch and Judy and other folkloric characters, made into a film projected to cover all four walls of a gallery space. The silhouetted characters are looming and distorted. 
Hiller made a site specific work at the Freud Museum in London (the home of Sigmund Freud). She presented a series of cardboard boxes containing text and objects, and also made photographs of Freud’s ethnographic collection.
!Hilliard John,
British artist who makes photographs of landscape and architecture, presented on wall mounted light boxes. 
!Hiorns, Roger.
!Hirschhorn Thomas.
German artist born around 1960 who makes large scale environments and installations. Hirschorn’s work evolves from a life as art philosophy that leads him to create environments and make related publications. He uses found imagery, and all kinds of materials in his work, building large architectural structures in a homemade way from cardboard, silver foil, plastic bags, and any number of other found items and materials. 
!Hirst Damien.
British artist born in the early 1960s who became one of the best known contemporary artists in Britain when he exhibited a shark preserved in formaldehyde in a glass tank titled The Impossibility of Death in the Mind of Someone Living. 
Hirst is considered to have been the impetus fro a generation of British artists known as the Young British Artists (abbreviated to YBAs) because he curated an exhibition in an empty warehouse titled Freeze. This featured his contemporaries (they were all still students) at a time when it was nearly impossible for young artists to exhibit their work in London galleries. The term YBAs went on to become the collective name for all of Hirst’s generation, including Jake and Dinos Chapman, Tracy Emin, and Sarah Lucas. 
Hirst’s work was purchased by the London Collector and advertising executive Charles Saatchi, who financed the shark and subsequent formaldehyde works. Saatchi had the only gallery in London at the time big enough to exhibit these works. 
Hirst made a number of formaldehyde works including a cow and her calf sliced right through into two vertical halves, exhibited in two pairs of tanks so that the viewer could walk throng the centre of the cow and the calf. He also made a work consisting of a glass wall case filled with rows of fish of different species preserved by taxidermy, all facing the same way (Fish Swimming in the Same Direction)
Hirst has made many paintings which fall into three series, the spin paintings (done on a circular canvas spinning horizontally which has multicoloured gloss paint thrown at it), the spot paintings (multicoloured gloss paint spots on white arranged in rows) and the butterfly paintings. The first butterfly paintings were canvases covered with gloss paint in plain colours in the surface of which adhered many different types of butterfly. Most recently Hirst has been covering the canvas entirely with the wings of butterflies, arranged in geometric patterns. Hirst’s paintings are made by assistants to his instructions and produced in large numbers. The spot paintings have become Hirst’s logo, and these signature spots adorned first the restaurant that Hirst opened with Charles Saatchi in London, The Pharmacy, then a Thames riverboat which takes visitors between the two Tate galleries, and finally an unmanned space vehicle which has been sent to Mars.
Life and death are the major preoccupations of Hirst’s work made evident by his use of formaldehyde, insects, and medical drugs. 
Following his work with butterflies, Hirst used flies; a cow’s head was placed inside a three metres high glass tank, and maggots were allowed to breed in it. These turned into flies, which could fly around inside the tank and an interconnected one in which there was a desk and a chair. The flies eventually died, having lived their entire lives in the sealed tank. 
Medical drugs are used by Hirst to represent both life and death and he has made several works using bottles, cartons, boxes, tubes, and other containers of drugs. These are arranged in glass wall cases showing their predominantly white packaging with blocks of a huge variety of colours giving a particular visual effect when seen in the context of a gallery. Hirst made a room sized installation titled Pharmacy, which contained these cases on all the walls as well as some of the oversized red, green and blue coloured glass decanters that are seen in pharmacist’s windows. 
Hirst also made a feature film with many of his friends as actors, shot on 35 mm with high production values. In 2003 Hirst continues to make formaldehyde works, for example glass tanks containing animals’ heads with knives plunged into them, or with blood spattered on the wall around them.
Damien Hirst’s work is often lampooned by the national press in cartoons.
!Hobbypop.
Group of German and British artists who work collaboratively. Hobbypop are based in London. Their paintings are often installed both on the wall and propped at angles in the centre of a gallery space. The group also initiates events including guided walks, and theatrical performances.
!Hoberman Nicky.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes large (up to three metres high) paintings of children. These are done from photographs taken by the artist of children she meets. They are painted as if there is a strange, often green or bluish light cast upon them. The children have exaggerated features and seem to exist in some other world. There is something sinister yet saccharine about the children. Hoberman applies paint smoothly and thinly, all brushstrokes softened out. 
!Hofer Candida.
Photographer born around 1960 who has photographed zoos, libraries and various other institutions. Her images often show glamorous ornate surroundings yet have a cool restrained atmosphere. 
!Holloway, Evan.
!Holzer Jenny.
American artist born in the 1950s who works in a variety of media using text. During the 1980s Holzer made a series of works featuring popular sayings slightly altered that she titled Truisms. These political statements subverted commonly used truisms to make points about consumerism, subjectivity and art. The Truisms featured on T-shirts, Till receipts in shops, park benches, digital display boards about Times Square and Piccadilly Circus, LED displays in public places, posters and leaflets. Holzer employed the various methods of public display and dissemination of information for the presentation of her work. She was a contemporary of Barbara Kruger.  
!Home Stewart. 
British artist and writer has written about punk, art and performance, including writing and photographing a performer known as Cosey Fanni Tutti. Home has also made work on paper and installation, such as the Art Bed, an ordinary single bed with a bedspread on which the viewer is invited to recline. Home’s subject is art itself. 
!Hopkins, Georgie.
British artist born in the late 1960s. 
!Horn Rebecca. 
Artist born in the 1950s who makes sculptures that are largely concerned with the human body. Horn’s work is often presented as installations and she sometimes makes kinetic work. An example of this is apiece called The Conductor, which is a white non-sighted person’s stick suspended in space over a metal plate. An invisible magnetic field causes the stick to dance spasmodically, making a tapping and scraping sound when it touches the metal plate. It seems to be wielded by an invisible person, and appears quite threatening to the viewer. A piano hangs from the ceiling, its insides exploding out periodically, catching the viewer unaware as the lid springs open and all the strings sound and the hammers slide out.
Another work consists of a large number of hoses strewn over a whole exhibition space, all leading to something which is operated by an air bladder. The hoses are full of compressed air and the whole operation seems to be random. 
A work which is visually similar to this is a dressmakers dummy holding a dress made from transparent tubing which is full of red liquid, which is pumped around the dress as if the tubes were an arterial system. 
Horn has also made a dress, again displayed on a dummy, from large pieces of meat sewn together. 
Much of Horn’s work involves the construction of strange garments, such as the gloves with 50 cm long pointed finger extensions. She often makes work which impedes and transforms the function of the body, or places its inner workings on the outside. Her work has a fairy tale atmosphere that is quite frightening. 
!Horn Roni.
American artist working from the 1980s onwards. 
!Houshiary Shirazeh.
British artist who makes sculptures, often of metal, with references to Islamic architecture. The surfaces of the sculptures are often etched with Arabic text and Islamic patterning. 
!Howard Kate.
American video artist born in the 1960s. 
!Howson Peter.
Brigit artist who makes paintings and drawings. He went to several war zones as a war artist.
!Hoyland John.
British painter who makes figurative paintings.
!Huebler Douglas.

!Hughes Des.
British artist born in the 1970s who makes resin cast sculptures of junk. For example Hughes might model old nails in clay and cast them, painting them to look exactly like the real thing.
!Hugonnier Marine.
French artist who made a film over several months spent in Afghanistan.
!Hume Gary.
British artist who has represented Britain at the Venice Biennale. Hume is one of the artists in the group known as Young Births Artists or YBAs who emerged in the early 90s. Hume began his career making paintings from hospital doors, which he painted all over with white or cream coloured gloss paint. In fact gloss household paint has become Hume’s trademark and he never uses anything else. Hume went on to make figurative paintings mostly portraits in which he vastly simplified the elements of a face, so that it consisted of one or two areas of colour and no features. The paint was poured on, allowed to dry, and then the next colour added. The edge of the dry part acts as a barrier to the next colour. 
Hume painted the model Kate Moss, a virgin Mary and other images taken from famous paintings, and a baby. He featured plants, and vague interiors, but all in his vastly simplified blocks of shiny colour. The paintings nearly resist interpretation, which is Hume’s intention.  
He made a video in which he portrays himself as King Canute. Around 2000 Hume began to make sculptures, bronze snowmen, very smoothed out shapes painted in shiny pale colours. 
!Huttner Per.
Swedish artist who make socially inclusive work in which he often invites the viewer to participate. 
!Huwys, Bethan.
Welsh artist who makes very small scale sculptures from natural materials. 
!Huyghe Pierre.
Swiss artist who makes photographs and videos. One photograph shows a stone frozen in mid air over a motorway. The stone looks like a boulder implying impending tragedy as it will surely cause a terrible accident, until the viewer realises that it is a tiny pebble that has been photographed as it drops from a bridge over the speeding cars. 
!Monchaux Cathy de.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculptures and installations. De Monchaux made a number of wall mounted works in the early 1990s from machined metal templates, bolted together with velvet, fur and leather often red or purple or black which was clamped between the metal but spilled out in carefully contrived folds. These constructions had a sexual fetishistic quality, they hinted at a purpose but remained ambiguous. They have a slightly gothic appearance but also a heavy metal aesthetic. Put simply they contrasted the supposedly masculine with the feminine, the functional with the luxurious to create decidedly feminine and visceral forms, which often alluded to the female body and genitalia in particular. De Monchaux continued to develop this way of working by expanding the scale of her sculptures, making repeating units and larger more complex forms, using a wider palette of coloured fabric and other materials, and sometimes making floor based installations. 
ACTIVIST ART

often used an unconventional panorama format canvas.
!Rae, Fiona.
Britsih artist born around 1960 who makes abstract paintings and works on paper. Rae uses the brush stroke and the mark as her subject, and in her paintings from the late 1980s to the 00s she has consistently explored the possibilities of the gesture as a formal element of composition. Rae uses a huge range of colours and type of mark, from rough wide brushstrokes to thin solid lines. These do not follow any figurative logic but are discrete elements that make up the whole. The paintings are coloured grounds with decorative marks arranged densely over them, the marks refer
Rae’s work invokes post-modern ideas of mimesis and quotation, since she is not just making a mark but reproducing generic mark-making, as well as borrowing from the history of painting for her sources. The paintings are usually about 2 metres high. 
In the 2000s, Rae has introduced photo collage of found images and uses digital technology to reproduce and rescale painted areas that interest her.
!Raedecker, Michael.
Dutch artist born in 1961 who makes figurative paintings that incorporate thread. Raedecker, a former fashion student, uses thread as a drawing medium, to describe outlines, textures, surfaces and light. He first paints the background, very often using thick and even pooled paint, and then hangs the canvas so that he can sew through it. Sometimes the thread is pushed through wet paint, or paint applied over it so that it becomes impregnated. 
In the late 1990s Raedecker’s first series of thread paintings featured the interiors of hotel rooms in grey, ochre, pale pink and beige. A lampshade might be filled in with satin stitch, a bedcover with a textured thread, and a rug with tufts of wool. There is also a painting of an empty cinema auditorium. However thread is used more unconventionally to represent light and shade. Another group of works made at the same time show odd figures, often wielding guitars, their hair done in thread. These figures seem as if they might be the inhabitants fop the hotel rooms. They also refer obliquely to the rock group Kiss, who wear costumes and face paint.
The rooms are small paintings but the following group of work depicting modernist suburban houses are about 2 metres high. In these, a picture window at night might be filled in with lengths of thread across its width to simulate reflective light. Small knotted threads indicate shrubs and trees. Like the earlier works these are based on found photographs. 
Raedecker’s use of thread to depict plants, shrubs and trees led him to make a group of landscape paintings, often very large. These include a number of scenes of barren desert as if from a western, with stunted trees and parched earth. The thread detail is used to describe plants and also shadows. At this time Raedecker also made a number of more abstract works based on the shapes of male and female genitals, thread representing pubic hair, as well as a series of portraits of old men.
The occasional appearance of a bird in the landscapes is transferred into a number of still lives made in the early 00s. These use thread more literally. Areas of stitching and tufting vie for attention with increasing use of very thick paint, applied horizontally and left to dry. These often include a duck or a swan like bird. 
!Rainer, Arnulf.
German painter
!Rauch Neo.
German artist who makes paintings that have both figurative and abstract elements. The figures and buildings in Rauch’s are painted in a socialist realist style, but the are part of a larger, geometric abstract composition of coloured lines and shapes. The paintings are up to 3 metres high.
!Rego Paula.
British artist who since the 1980s has made semi-autobiographical narrative figurative paintings. Rego has a direct style of drawing, and paints loosely but boldly using a generally subdued palette. She often adapts scenes from her own childhood to represent collective rites of passage, or taboo subjects such as incest. Little girls feature in many of her paintings, often with a man wearing military dress, a father figure. Rego is interested in the psychology of her chosen subjects as well as representing an era. 
!Rehberger Tobias.
German artist who makes site specific sculptures and models for these which are based on garden design and horticulture. Rehberger might for example make a modular planting unit in which he plants the crops grown in the area, for example maize, artichokes and sunflowers, and place this in a university concourse. Or he may reconstruct municipal flowerbeds and through the planting and shape of the beds echo a municipal building. 
!Reinke Steve.
Canadian artist who make video work. Reinke’s work 100 Short Videos, made in the mid 1990s, is just that – 100 short films made or found by Reinke, which include film of himself as a child, film of a group of children making a news broadcast at the Canadian Broadcasting Corporation and film of gay men meeting for sex in a forest.
!Relph Nick and Payne Oliver.
Britsih artists born in the late 1970s who make videos about urban culture, for example one about public toilets in London which combines music with a written narrative and poetry edited with images from footage they shot in public conveniences.
!Rhoades Jason.
American artist.
!Rhodes Carol.
British artist who makes smallish (up to 2 metres high but usually less) paintings of aerial views of landscape, usually fairly ordinary areas such as airport terminals.
!Richter Gerhard.
One of the most important twentieth century German artists, Richter made paintings in a variety of style and on a range of subjects. He is a highly skilled and able to make photo real images, which form a large part of his work. He has also made a large number of black and white paintings. Richter is known as the artist who developed the ways in which painting was concerned with photography. Beginning with the photo real images, all Richter’s work in some way has been concerned with photography, and more specifically with what has become known as ‘the blur’. 
Richter’s colour photo real works which include one of his best known works, a portrait of his daughter from behind, wearing a red floral print top with her blonde hair tied back, are made very realistically but the paint is smoothed soft so that there are no visible brushstrokes. In this sense the paintings emulate photographs even in their physical surface. He made similar paintings of a candle burning and of a baby.
A number of monochrome paintings of blocks of flats are given a more exaggerated blur, as are his numerous black and white paintings. These include one of a chandelier, many landscapes and some trees. There are also two groups of painted photo portraits, all in the same format, one of the Baader Meinhor Gang and the other of noted German industrialists. These are painted as copies of their source, including any text that was attached to the photo and the white edge of the photo. Most of the photo-based works are smallish, around one metre high or less.
A series of paintings of mountains are made in a colour by numbers way, as if the image has been reduced to the essentials of light and dark. 
Richter made a series of colour chart works, which are immaculately painted multi-coloured shiny rectangles of colour within a white grid.
Taking the blur to its physical extreme his later work is a series of abstract paintings done with a giant squeegee dragged across the width of the canvas, spreading the various colours of paint into each other in one sweep of the squeegee.
Richter wrote a book, The Practise of Painting, which details what he was trying to achieve.
!Rist Pipilotti.
Swiss artist who makes video installations. Rist, who used to be a musician, collages together still images, video footage and music, which she composes herself to make dream like sequences. She uses digital effects to create saturated colour, colour changes, dissolves, blurring, mirroring, and other optical effects. Rist’s videos sometimes feature herself. The films are non-narrative and are looped.
Rist employs a variety of means of presentation; double projections that meet in a corner for example. In one exhibition she provided a 2-metre high 4 metre long sofa for viewer to clamber on to and watch a video wearing headphones.
!Ritchie Matthew..
American artist who makes work based on charts, graphs and plans. Ritchie makes large-scale drawings, up to 5 metres long. An example of which is his diagram using circles around the names of individuals, corporations and institutions which are connected by arrows, showing the extent to which the US government and the family President Bush is linked to the oil business
!Rondinone, Ugo.
Italian artist who makes circular target paintings with concentric circles in blurred colours that create an optical shimmer. 
!Rosenbach, Ulrike.
Artist who made video performance work in the 1980s, of which Spinning is an example. The viewer sees an apparently abstract circular image of spinning dots, which are reflective. The image is unsteady. On inspection it can be seen that the image is of a woman (probably the artist) wearing a wide net skirt with large sequins on it, who is spinning in one place, seen from directly above her head with her arms clasped around her torso so that only the skirt and the top of her head are visible. The effect is of as spinning glittering disc. As the spinning continues the unsteadiness of the image becomes puzzling as well as disorientating for the viewer. After ten minutes the woman falls down when she is too dizzy to continue. The viewer is also dizzy. When the woman is still but the image is still unsteady it becomes clear that the film is not shot from above but from the ground into a mirror placed above her head, and that the mirror is wobbling. 
!Rothschild, Eva.
Briisth artist born in the 1970s who makes sculpture and wall based work. Rothschild has made a large numver of works by weaving togheter two contrasting wall posters, cutting them in to strips and reassembling them, turniong two images into an abstracted third. She often leaves part fo the image as unwoven strips which hang down the wall. Rothschild’s sculptures are minimal abstract objects which have the qualtity of small monumnets. She made a work by placing hundreds of burning incense sticks into a rubber car tyre, justaposignt the spiritaul contemplation associtated with the incense sticks with a refernce to the burning necklace (a car tyre filled with petrol) placed roudn the necks of persecuter Buddhist monks in the far east.
!Ruff, Thomas.
German artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic portraits. These are head and shoulders images printed much larger than life size, the subject looking directly at the camera, expressionless. Every detail of skin and hair is in sharp focus. 
!Ruggaber, Karin.
Brisith artist born in the 1960s who makes scultpure. Ruggaber takes elemtns of furnitre and arichtectural fixtures and fittings and transfroms them into minimla sculputre, for example a hand rail upholstered in leather effect vinyl.
!Rummings, Neil.
Bristih artist born in the 1970s who makes large paintings up to 3 meters high and works on paper. Rummings juxtaposes imagery such as cross sections from medical books, tattoos, graphic design for rock band album covers, or graphic images of flames or flowers, for example, to make a complex abstract but decorative composition patined in hard edged britght acryulic colours. 
!Russell, John.
British artist, formerly a member of Bank, who makes large scale representational paintings which are very messy and badly painted. Russell also makes perfromances. Art, artists and the art world are the subject of his often polemical work. Russell sometimes collaborates with Fabienne Audeoud.

!Saville, Jenny.
British painter born in the late 1960s who makes monumental paintings, at least 4 metre high, of nude women and photographs. Saville’s subjects are often very large women and the subject of her work is really flesh. A paintign will consist largley of expanses of painted flesh, onto which in the early years she inscribed text as if it were cut into the flesh. In the 1990s Saville made paitngins of women lying on a sheet of glass so that their flesh squashed and distorted.

!Schnabel, Julian.
American artist who made a series of very large paintings (at least 4 metres high) in the 1980s onto which he stuck broken corckery, mainly white dinner plates. Schnable has always made huge paintings and in 2000 he made a 10 metre high painting, painted on the floor, which had footmarks on it. Schnable has also made feature films, notably a biographical film about Jean Michel Basquiat.
!Schor, Mira.
American artist who makes abstract paintings that have feminist and psychoanalytic theoretical concerns. Schor’s paintings often contain a painted slit or orifice that makes reference to the work of Fontana but is clearly a vagina motif.
