!Searle Adrian.
British painter and critic who made abstract paintings until the mid 1990s when he gave up painting to concentrate on writing about art. 
!Serrano, Andres.
American artist born in the 1950s who makes photographic work. Serrano is notorious fro his work Piss Christ, which caused a furore when it was exhibited. There were demands for its censorship on the grounds of obscenity and blasphemy from American right wing and religious groups. Serrano immersed a cheap plastic figure of Christ on the cross into a tank of his own urine with a light behind it and took a photograph. Christ is seen submerged in a red and golden light in an image that barring the substance in which he is immersed is a true image of glorification. Serrano, brought up as a Catholic, continued to make controversial images such as the Last Supper and a Pieta (both black statuettes immersed in tanks, with the bubbles adhering to them adding texture. He made a series of photographs of the blending in a dish of blood and milk, and of spurts of semen frozen in mid-air at the point of ejaculation – white streams on a black background, and a series of images made in a morgue of corpses titled by the way in which they died; drowned, poisoned, stabbed etc.) 
Serrano stated that all his work was an entirely respectful investigation into sex, life and death and the relation of these to spirituality and religion
!Shaw, George.
!Shaw, Jim.
American artist born in the 1950s who is best known for his collection known at the Thrift Store Paintings, an expanding collection of found art from which he select for exhibition, and the Dreams drawings, daily hand drawn records of his dream life, in the form of cartoon strips with text. The dreams very often feature art, artists, museums and dealers
Sighicelli Elsa
American artist born in the 1960s.
!Signer, Roman.
A German live artist who describes his works as sculptures, Signer is best known for works from the 1980s onwards. Signer’s materials are chiefly his own body, furniture, household and DIY objects and materials and explosives and his performances and actions are documented as short films and photographic sequences. They nearly always take place in a field or other natural environment. For example Signer stands on three boxes stacked one on the other in the middle of a field. Each box is rigged to explode in sequence. Signer remains standing as each one collapses, bringing him eventually to the ground. Something is placed in a stream, and the action of the running water eventually causes an explosive reaction. In both cases the resulting puffs of smoke is visually important to signer. He also used a bright orange ball in a number of works. 
Signer uses the formal language of sculpture, the body and the plinth, the landscape, and subverts it by adding the destructive power of explosives in a highly controlled way. The works seem slapstick and humorous but in fact they are precisely organised, even the puffs of smoke are carefully planned. 
Signer has also made some site-specific firework displays.
!Simpson DJ.
British artist born in the 1970s who make very large (at least 4 by 5 metre) machine carved panels. These are made as if they are abstract paintings. Simpson uses an electric router to gouge out channels to create lines, which loop and curve over the surface, as well as other tools that can inscribe the surface that he works on. He works on prepared and on found surfaces, such as laminate table tops, as well as building materials such as ply and silver insulation board, in fact any sheet material that is composed of layers so that the lines Simpson cuts out reveal the contrasting colours of the layer beneath the surface. 
!Simpson Jane.
Britsih artist born in the 1960s who is best known for her ice sculptures, in which she uses refrigeration and ice as a construction tool and material. One of the earliest made in the early 1990s consists of two halves of a heart made from metal and brass sheet, the separated surfaces refrigerated and water added so that ice forms, sticking the heart back together. Another work from the same period is a small refrigerated metal shelf on which is placed a block of butter. These works are conditional – they only remain in their intended state as long as the refrigeration process is taking place. From the beginning of her career Simpson has also had an interest in vessels and containers, starting with a collection of Tupperware, which she displayed in vitrines, and photographed, the photographs enabling the plastic mass manufactured containers to transcend their reality and appear far more precious. In 2000 Simpson began a series of works in which she modelled vessels depicted in paintings, for example those in Morandi paintings, casting these in rubber, and later making them from scratch in glazed porcelain. These vessels are exhibited in groups on shelves and plinths.
This interest in functional mass produced containers led Simpson to begin to cast similar containers in rubber, and then to cast furniture, leading to a series of floppy furniture works. Simpson cast stools, chairs, tables and sideboards in rubber, creating objects that absolutely refuse their function and flop in a heap on the floor almost seeming to be more like curled up or collapsed four legged animals. 
There is an abject side to Simpson’s work, which is contrasted with the grandeur of ambition in some of the ice sculptures.
An antique sideboard is cast in plaster and its top refrigerated, inviting contemplation of the function of such a piece of furniture, and highlighting the appearance of a layer of ice as a decorative surface. A structure somewhat like a cross between double-sided train seat, a fountain but chiefly resembling a urinal, has steel sides curving down into a trough. These are refrigerated and the water flow is continuously frozen.
These transitional works marking the beginning of a series of works in which Simpson refrigerated already existing objects, often in situ, in order to explore the qualities of ice as a decorative surface. These works include a refrigerated chandelier and ornate cast iron gates.
!Sinsel Daniel.
German artist who paints watercolours depicting young men and boys often naked or engaged in sex acts. The figures are often shown in rural landscapes. There is an idyllic atmosphere yet the figures in the paintings are clearly contemporary. Sense’s medium is traditionally associated with art of the past, with amateur art or with graphic art and illustration gives the images an ironically innocent quality. 
!Smith and Stewart.
Artist partners, born around 1970, who made a number of performative video works before turning to installation. Kiss is a video in which one partner covers the other with red lipstick kisses until there is no skin left untouched. The gesture of covering someone with kisses becomes sinister as the flesh begins to take on the appearance of having been beaten, bruised or flayed. In another work one partner is submerged in a bath, and the other periodically place their face into the water and breathes air into the submerged partners mouth enabling them to stay submerged. These simple acts of partnership, intimacy and trust have violence and control as their counterpoint. 
Smith and Stewart moved on to investigate ideas about control and implicit violence, for example in an installation made in 2003. This consisted of a 29-metre long 4-meter high breezeblock wall constructed diagonally across the gallery, not meeting the wall at either end. On one side of the wall the gallery was empty and dark. On the other side the space was also dark but a chair, various amplifiers speakers and cables could be seen. Projected onto the brick wall is a film of a hand covered in a black round shape, its surface bound with layers of shiny black plastic tape, knocking against a wooden board leaning against the wall. The film is made on the actual wall at the actual place where it is projected, leading to a double take in the viewers mind about what is real (the board is not really there). The lights turn on and off apparently in a random fashion. They are pendant light bulbs. The scene becomes sinister, the knocking like the sound of some possible torture or unspecified but unpleasant event.
!Smith Bob and Roberta.
The pseudonym of Britsih artist Patrick Brill, born in around 1960. Roberta is in fact the name of his sister, but he does not work with her. Brill makes painting, sculpture and installation using basic materials such as house paint, cement, clay, and timber. He has also made participatory works, inviting visitors to use clay provided to add to his collection of clay vegetables. His work draws attention to the role of the artist, the status of the art object and the role of the viewer through the copious use of hand painted text, and knowingly clumsy modes of construction. The text is always capitalised, painted in bright colours, a different colour for each letter, on a white background. Brill’s work is socially engaged in the sense that he acknowledges that the viewer is not to be deceived by art, also questioning the legitimising role played by commercial success and highlighting the mundane reality of most artists lives. He portrays himself as a fallen hero, a failed romantic busy with the gritty reality of the important job of the artist.
Brill operates a kind of grass roots art politics, often creating events or action accompanied by promotional material and badges. An example is his 2002 initiation to all artists to bring their old work to be destroyed in a massive purgative action. His hand painted slogans are a feature of these, and by the 2000s these became the predominant form of his work. 
!Smith Brigid
Britsih artist born in the 1960s.
!Smith Fred.
American artist working from the 1980s onwards who makes socially engaged installation art. Smith is interested in ethnicity, cultural identity and institutional representations of different cultures. He has often been invited to make installations in museums, for example he has swapped exhibits from ethnographic collections with those from nineteenth century decorative arts collections.
!Smith Kate.
Britsih artist born around 1960 who makes installation and photographic work, which has sometimes featured shoes. Smith’s work is rooted in her interest in psychoanalytical and feminist theory.
!Smith Kerry.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes moulded painted fibreglass figurative sculpture. She has made a pregnant school girl holding out her hand in the style of a charity collection box, and small girl who is fixed to project horizontally form the wall just about floor level.

!Smith Kiki.
American artist born in the 1940s who makes sculpture. Smith’s work is characterised by her use of ephemeral and delicate materials like paper, fabric, and beads. Smith often makes life-size human figures, animals and birds from paper; sometimes the figures are wearing paper clothes. Her work describes the condition of femininity and of childhood.
A naked female figure lies on her side on the ground, strings of glass beads issuing from between her legs as if toe represent blood. A peacock’s tail on the wall has the detail drawn on in pen. These are made like all of smiths work from white paper stuck together, and varnished, so that the final appearance is of a translucent yellowed skin. The three dimensional forms are hollow skins, fragile and ephemeral.
!Sorin Pierrick.
French artist who makes work about knowledge and information, including a film in which he purports to be an expert explaining theories wit the aid of a white board, which he is fact making up as he goes along. 
!Spence Jo.
Britsih artist born in the late 1940s who made photographic work from the 1970s onwards. Spence, a large woman, used herself and her male partner as models to make a number of works that explore preconceived ideas about ideal beauty, masculine and feminine identity and relations. The photographs were sometimes printed life-size. In the 1980s Spence developed breast cancer but continued to photograph her ravaged body during and after mastectomy and radiotherapy. Spence’s photographs are often accompanied with written text in a diary format.
!Spiteri John.
Australian artist who makes figurative paintings and autobiographical videos. In the videos he challenges himself alone with the camera. The videos are usually a response to a song and a location. For example in one film set in a bathroom Spiteri sings a rap song so fast that it is barely believable. It seems clear that this is a film that has been speeded up, but in fact Spiteri has practised until he could do it perfectly. In others, he samples a part of a song and uses this as the soundtrack for a self-interrogation, in which he faces the camera and talks to it, such as I Can’t Do That, a film based on a sample from a song by the pop singer Cher in which Spiteri is seen regarding himself in a mirror repeating ‘I can’t do that’ in a litany to hopelessness. The alter ego that Spiteri has created for his work is a blend of a character who is not very good at life but is extremely good at the tasks carried out in the films. 
Spiteri has also made small detailed narrative paintings that seem to be illustrations for a 1950s children’s story. These are painted in enamel paint in bright colours. Spiteri has made a video of each painting that adds a narrative, consisting of an overheard conversation. Since 2000 Spiteri has been making oil paintings.
!Sprinkle Annie.
American ex prostitute, artist and performer, best known and in fact notorious for her work Pro Porn and Anti Porn in which she reclines on an armchair and invites the audience to examine her vagina through a speculum, to masturbate her and to ask her any questions they want. 
!Stapleton Fergal.
British artist born around 1970.
!Stark Frances.
Americna aritst bron in the 1960s who uses a variety of media. Stark also writes as part of her practise. Her book The Architect and the Housewife is about her practise as an artist and the difficulty of making art at home. 
Stark ahs made a series of videos of her cats at home in her apartment. 
She also makes sculptures, including one of a small Perspex grandfather clock. 
Stark makes quantities of drawings, fro example her text drawings made using carbon paper which feature a word or a number of words written out in rows each row being added to. The low-tech transfer quality gives this work an ephemeral quality.
!Starling Simon.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture by turning one thing into another. For example a bible into a chair – Starling will dismantle the bicycle, strip it down, grind, melt and otherwise reduce it to its constituents which he then recasts as another object entirely. Pursuing his interest into how one thing can be made into another, Starling has also made a group of works, which are based on United Nations blueprints for agricultural implements. These are instructions in how to make essential implements from freely available materials, such as oil cans, bicycle parts, and pipes. Starling constructs these fully functioning implements such as ploughs, harvesters and irrigators, often painting them, and displays them as sculpture. 
!Starr, Georgina.
Britsih artist born in the 1960s who makes video and installation art. Starr creates a variety of personas and worlds and weave a fiction around herself that is sometimes quite like a children’s story. One installation in the early 1990s created a fairy tale or cartoon like environment in wihc the viewer could wander. In 1999 she made the Bunny Lakes trilogy, a multi-part work about teenage girls from a place called Bunny Lakes, based on a true story.
Starr’s earliest works were very simple. Crying, a video, shows Starr bursting into tears and crying sitting in front fo the camera. Antoher is a record on which a whislted tune is recorded. The tune was made by chance composition – Starr threw torn pieces of paper in the air, noted where they fell and translated this spacing into muscial notation.

!Station House Opera.
Britsih performance art group led by Julian Maynard Smith who came to prominence in the 1980s with highly formalised repetition-based works. Performers dressed in ordinary clothes and not speaking, would carry out everyday actions repetitively, without making allowance for each other’s movements but avoiding collision. The performers obeyed a set of rules and together a counterpoint effect was achieved. Table Move, an early work, consisted of performers moving furniture obsessively around the space, including wardrobes. 
Later works built on the idea of physical risk, involving the performers in a variety of increasingly dangerous endurance based works. One involved the performers sitting on and using furniture including tables, chairs and cupboards, while suspended high above a pedestrian area from the outside of a building. Another, Bastille Dances, consisted of a day long construction and dismantling of a model of the Bastille form polystyrene blocks.
!Stehli Jemima.
Britsih artist born in 1962, Stehli makes photographic work centring on her own body. Stehli began as a sculptor. Early works included scarves several metres long knitted on very large diameter needles, which were presented folded on the floor, and wool embroidered padded panels, around 60 cm long featuring words.
In the early 1990s Stehli began to make resin casts of isolated parts of her own body, such as a hand complete with watch or a breast, moving on to making casts of vessels and bottles which she presented in groups, and objects such as a purse or a tray. These small domestic scaled sculptures are lacquered and highly polished, often in deep reds, blues and black. 
Stehli made a performance work for which she lay naked except for a pair of high-heeled shoes motionless on the floor of a gallery, titled Shoes Chosen by the Curator in the mid 1990s. She presented her own body as a sculpture and documented this by photographing it. This led her to make a series of photographs of herself naked.
Stele’s photographic work since this point has concentrated on the relation of the viewer to the female body. She made two groups of images that alluded to bondage, in which parts of her body appear naked, the cable release of the camera highly visible. One group of photographs is black, in deep shadow; the other is white and bright.
She then made a series based on the work of the 60s artist Allen Jones. Stehli recreated Jones’ famous fibreglass sculptures of women in high heels and underwear acting as the support for glass table tops or stools, photographing herself recreating the poses, without the underwear but with the shoes. The photographs are printed so that she appears life size, in black and white.
Stehli work has sometimes involved other people. She invited women to be photographed by her naked, with herself also in the photograph. She then made what has become her best-known work - a group of photographs taken by men. She invited a number of curators and critics to come to her studio and be photographed sitting or standing as she faced them and removed her clothes except for a pair of high heels. The image is composed to show her back view in the foreground and the man looking outwards. Some of the men are looking at Stehli, some at the camera, some elsewhere. The men hold the cable release.
A series of works based on a particular photograph of Helmut Newton’s followed. Stehli recreated Newton’s black and white image of four fashion models standing naked staring at the camera in her studio, placing herself to the far side of the image, leaving space where the others are in the Newton original, on a white photographic backdrop. She stands in the exact pose of the original, naked in one photograph and clothed in jeans and a t-shirt in the other, in both cases holding the cable release, the cable trailing in from the camera at the bottom of the image.
A number of works made on this basis included a series based on the paintings of Francis Bacon. For these colour photographs Stehli moves in front of the camera set on a long exposure. The result is a blurred image of intense colour, distorting features and body parts in the same way that Bacon does. In a similar series she uses a red silk scarf moving it around her body so that the red blurs and makes a virtually abstract image.
Since 2000 Stehli has made images in which she is increasingly absent. A series of self-portraits in her studio show her often lying on the ground at odd angles to the camera, her gaze not directed at the viewer. 
!Steinbach Haim.
American artist born in the 1950s who became well known in the 1980s. His work included small sculptures on shelves, using cast metal, plastic and laminate.
!Stelarc.
Australian artist who made performance works involving physical endurance and technology. For one performance he inserted several hooks into his flesh and suspended himself from these, his body hanging horizontally from a bridge over Sydney Harbour. Stelarc is interested in the body as a machine and for another work he had a robotic arm constructed, which was connected to his brain via electrodes so that he could control it by thought. 
!Stevens Gary.
British artist born in the 1950s who made a number of performance works in the 1980s which took place in galleries. In his work Stevens adopted a stylised performing persona and often used soft toys in his work.
!Stezaker John.
Britsih artist born in the 1950s who makes photographic collage and printed work. Stezaker has made photographs of landscapes reflected in water, which he presents upside down so that they appear to be the right way up at first. He has made a series of large black and white prints based on an old photograph of a nude, which he has distorted.
!Stimson Myra.
British artist born in the 1960s who make paintings. Stimson's early work was figurative. In 2000 she made a series of paintings in which she reproduced shopping lists she had found in a supermarket.
!Stitt Andre.
British performance artist working in the 1980s.
!Stockholder Jessica.
American sculptor born in the 1960s who makes very large installations. Stockholders early work consisted of small assemblages of timber, carpet, household objects, wool and fabric. She began to make larger works – a transitional work consisted of painting areas of a garden shed, including painting the side it blue, extending the colour over the grass in front of the shed. A similar work involved a painted mattress.
Stockholder’s mature work consists of much larger scale site-specific installations. These are described by her as paintings and are often based on landscapes or still lives, for example by Cézanne. The installations are large sprawling constructions, often emerging from or including in their structure a partial demolition of the gallery wall. They appear like unfinished construction work which has a life of its own. Stockholder uses lengths of timber and board, or spreads items and material over the floor to build an environment, which is transparent, can be walked through and around, and which presents multiple views according to the angle from which they are observed. She adds flat planes in the form of sheet material, transparent lighting gels, fabric and carpet, and then paints certain areas. She uses light in the form of light bulbs in holders, and has also added miniature fans to create movement. In one installation a row of oranges sits on a shelf. In another a large carpet twists as if it is flying and has collided with the installation. Paint, timber and rope spill onto and extend over the floor.
One work is made from a bank of baths, arranged in rows then cut into with a blowtorch and painted, the orange paint extending right across the floor. Another work uses a number of refrigerators arranged in a wall like structure. A work made from a large pink sheet of plastic pulled up at various points by wires attached to the ceiling ahs a long title including the words ‘big rock candy mountain’. 
Most of stockholders works have very long impenetrable titles that seem as though they may explain the work but in fact do not.
!Stoner Tim.
British artist born in the 1970s who makes figurative paintings of people and groups of people. Stoner paints figures in silhouette with a halo of light behind them and adds a light coloured layer of paint, which serves to blur the image so that it appears as if it is viewed against the sun.
!Struth Thomas.
German artist who makes photographic portraits, landscapes and interiors characterised by their apparent stillness as if time has stood still.
!Stubbs Michael.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings and sculpture about painting, for example his squashed canvases. These are wall-mounted works consisting of several small square canvases about 50cm square sandwiched together with very thick layers of oil paint in a variety of colours. These are bolted together through the stretchers so that the paint is squeezed out all around the edges, giving the impression that there are several hidden paintings. The whole object is mounted on the wall, the top canvas blank.
!Sturgis Daniel.
Britsih painter born in the 1960s who makes brightly coloured hard edged pattern paintings based on flowers.
!Sugimoto Hiroshi.
Japanese photographer who makes photographs of a variety of subjects, often focussing on representing light, as for example in an number of works showing the light from candles.
!Swallow Ricky.
Australian artist born around 1970 who makes sculpture, for example a group of works made using record turntables as their base, on which Swallow placed model making figures and constructed space ships and other futuristic vehicles, habituations and buildings, incorporating the parts of the record player in to the whole. Some of the sculptures make use of the spinning of the turntable or the movement of the needle arm. The whole sculpture is sprayed with grey paint and presents a very convincing and ultimately unnerving scene from a future envisaged by swallow.
!Sze Sarah.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes site specific installations in which she arranges a vast amount of objects and materials over an entire room and its walls and ceiling. Sze chooses discarded materials, plastic bottles, cotton buds, cable, wire, paper, mass produced plastic vessels, toys, beads, sugar, glitter and hundreds of other items and materials and deploys them in a formal abstract composition also choosing to paint the walls, taking into account their form and colour. The vast scale of the undertaking is part of the effect of the work producing an effect of visual satiety in the viewer. 

!Taaffe Philippe.
American painter who first became successful in the 1980s who makes large 3 metre high paintings using often repeating patterns that seem to be based on printed and woven fabric designs and geometric abstract drawings. Taaffe’s work exhibits a combination of precision, in the drawing of the pattern, and painterlyness, in the application of paint over the whole. He uses strong colours, fro example yellow and black.
!Tait Neal.
British painter born in the 1960s who makes relatively small paintings sometimes semi-abstract, sometimes depicting objects. He paints in a loose gestural style.
!Takahashi Tomoko.
British artist who makes large scale installations from discarded furniture and junk. An early work in a gallery basement seemed at first sight not to be art but to be simply an excessively untidy storeroom that had perhaps been burgled. Takahashi has made numerous gallery installations; one consists of office furniture and equipment, which is strewn over the space in apparent chaos. In fact the arrangement is deliberate and painstaking. Filing cabinets, cupboards, desks, chairs, computers, pens, telephones, faxes, lamps, kettles, lengths of electric cable and just about anything that might be found in a modern office are arranged to look as if a cataclysmic event has taken place, perhaps an earthquake, a search by government agents, creating a sense of unease. The destruction of an everyday environment on such a grand scale underlines the temporality and built-in in obsolescence of our surroundings. Takahashi has also make a website called Word Perhect which is a hand drawn and written interface in which the viewer can select from a variety of notes, post-its, scraps of paper and jottings on the back of envelopes and paper bags found in the artists studio and add read her comments about the contents. The reader can type on the interface and on the notes in a font based on Takahashi’s handwriting.
!Taylor-Wood Sam.
Britsih artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic and video work. Taylor Wood is one of a number of artists who emerged in the early 1990s who are known as young British artists or YBAs.
One of Taylor Wood’s early works, Brontosaurus, is a video of a young man dancing to disco music, naked, swinging and flapping his penis energetically in the process. Another early work is a four screen video projection showing four opera singers performing La Boheme. The screens on which they appear are placed in a box shape, creating the sense that they are in a room which the viewer has entered, and that they are singing together. It becomes apparent that the films were shot separately and the singers are singing together but not together. A similar work, showing a couple having a row in a kitchen, is projected onto the walls in a corner, one showing the man and one the woman. The frame is composed so that it appears that one half of the kitchen, in which the man stands, is on one wall and the other half, in which the woman stands, is on the other. This gives the illusion that the space in which the filmed event is taking place has been partly reconstructed. It becomes clear that the row is in fact performed by actors who are not in the room at the same time. 
In the late 1990s Taylor Wood began to take panoramic photographs using a camera, which is set on a slowly revolving tripod. This enabled her to take a 360-degree view of a room and its occupants. She choreographed a series of group portraits featuring people she knew, taking place in borrowed apartments and other places. The long duration of the exposure made it possible for some of the subjects appear twice in the photograph. These colour photographs are about 3 metres long.
Taylor wood has also made a large number of conventional photographs including landscapes portraits and self-portraits. One shows her sitting on the steps of a building holding a young man, who is limp in her arms, a reference to paintings of the Virgin Mary holding Jesus. 
!Taylor Woodrow Steven.
 Performance Artist who made a number of solo and group performances in the 1980s. His solo work consisted mainly of theatrical poetry readings, in which Taylor Woodrow appeared as a kind of Victorian gothic character behind a lectern. His Dada like poetry was accompanied with pyrotechnics and fire breathing.
Taylor Woodrow was however best known for The Triptych Man and The Living Paintings, made between 1985 and 88. The Triptych man was a performance for three people who were inserted into a costume made from three men’s suits sewn together so that it had only three arms and legs wide each enough for two people’s limbs. The wearing of this forced the performers into a triangular formation, facing outwards, bound to and supporting each other but unable to see each other, and they were only able to move by bouncing. The Triptych man performed for several hours at a time, never speaking but often approaching people and sometimes remaining still for long periods while the performers rested.  
The Living Paintings was static work fro three performers, who were hung from the wall by a concealed harness wearing suits, painted a solid colour with matching makeup on any exposed skin and hair. They also wore contact lenses that featured an enlarged cornea with a white iris. These performers remained silent but did move (in as much as they could).
These Living Sculptures predated the now familiar street entertainers who rely for effect on inspiring the same feelings of embarrassment, uncertainty, and a desire to communicate in the viewer. 
!Teller Juergen.
British artist, formerly a fashion photographer, who has made work about the fashion world, including photographing hopeful models at the moment the door of the agency is opened, a group of images relating to Bertolt Brecht’s life in Berlin and the city after the wall and autobiographical work. He photographed a frozen dead dog sticking out of a refuse bin.
!Temin Kathy.
Australian artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic, video and installation work, centring on the creation of domestic interior spaces in the gallery, fashion and fetishism. Temin made an installation of Polystyrene room dividers crudely cut out in a 1960’s lattice pattern. She has made a variety of deliberately clumsily fashioned models of dwellings and spaces, and well made domestic items such as lamps and furniture. Temin also made a video documentation of two people dressed in koala suits, and has done work about shoe fetishism and celebrity.
!Thomson and Craighead.
British artists born in the 1960s who work together.
!Thorpe David.
British artist born in the late 1960s who makes complex collages from coloured paper, as well as specialist paper such as bark paper. Thorpe’s early works are based on photographs of 1960s and 70s modernist buildings, seen by day and by night. The image is built up from layers of paper cut to match the graduations of colour on the original. A night view of a house high on a hill is made from a great range of dark blue, grey and black paper, with bright orange for the illuminated windows, in which tiny black-silhouetted figures are seen. 
Thorpe’s views of modernist architectural ideals led him to begin to create images and to illustrate a Utopian vision. The collages began to depict invented buildings with symbolic overall structural design. He also designed an indoor planter which consisted several levels of mirrored glass, a small water feature and meadow plants.
!Throp Mo.
British painter born in around 1950.
!Tickle Matthew.
British artist born in the 1960s.
!Tillmans Wolfgang.
German artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic images of everyday objects and interiors, people, still lives, and abstract images made in the darkroom. Tillmans, formerly a fashion photographer, pioneered the influential everyday life, snapshot style of fashion photography that are so characteristic of the 1990s in Britain.
Tillmans always exhibits his work in groups, unframed, pinned to the wall,printed in various sizes from postcard size to three metres high. The images are produced as inkjet prints. 
The groups are not usually obviously related in terms of subject matter but are formally related (there are exceptions such as a collection of re-photographed images of young soldiers cut from newspapers). Thus an image of Kate Moss, a fashion model, might be placed next to one of some socks drying on a radiator, a naked man, some rotting fruit and an ashtray, a landscape. The images might be intimate or observational, and Tillmans’ work covers the range of categories including still life, landscape, cityscape, skyscraper, group and single portraiture. He photographs what is close to him and does not set out to find locations or subjects. A particularly well-known image from the 1990s is of two friends sitting on a branch of a tree, naked except for raincoats and boots. 
Sometimes Tillmans returns to a subject repeatedly, most famously in his work Concorde, a collection of photographs, also published as a book, of the aircraft in flight made over a period of weeks mostly at Heathrow airport where the plane spotters gather.
In 2000 Tillmans began to make more abstract images, using darkroom techniques and dyes to make colour images for example a pool of blurred red colour on a white ground that seems like a rose so that the connection with painting in his work, which has always been present, has become more obvious.
!Timoney, Padraig.
British artist born in the 1960s.
!Titchener Mark.
British artist born in the 1960s.
!Tiravanija Rirkrit.
Vietnamese/Cambodian/Korean artist known for his installations that are constructed to be social environments. Tivrana became known in the 1990s for his noodle bar, which he designed and built from ply and timber as counter with seating, where he cooked and served bowls of noodles to viewers. This is an idea which he has developed and which has taken place in a variety of locations and forms. 
!Toderi, Grazia.
Italian artist who made a digital video collage in which a model of a satellite is seen apparently floating in zero gravity inside a space consisting of a curved ceiling decorated with plaster relief which is digitally doubled to create a vault like space.
!Tomaselli Fred.
American artist who has made a number of works mixing painting and drawing with collage, which characteristically feature patterns made by placing dried leaves and flowers under a thick layer of poured resin.
!Toren Amikam.
British artist born around 1950 who makes works from found paintings onto which he stencils words, cutting out the letters to leave the wall behind the canvas visible.
!Tosic Chris.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings, drawings and sculpture. In 2003 Tosic made a work consisting of a group of heads carved on the end of pencils.
!Trangmar Susan.
British artist born in the late 1950s who makes photographic work.
!Trockel Rosemarie.
Artist born in the 1950s who makes knitted paintings, panels made on a knitting machine using jacquard techniques placed on stretches, as well as balaclavas and gloves. Trockel uses various motifs such as the hounds tooth from the check pattern, and the playboy bunny, both of which appear incongruous when translated into knitting.
!Turk Gavin.
British artist born around 1960 who is known for his work in mixed media centring on his signature and the idea of authorship. An early work of Turks is a men’s urinal signed by him and placed on a plinth. This work is a copy of a work by Marcel Duchamp. Turk has made a variety of painting of his own signature, using as his subject matter something which is visually a tiny detail in the history of painting (rarely appearing on a twentieth century work) yet very important in terms of authentication and therefore of value. Turk has painted his signature in oil paint, screen printed it and made a relief from polystyrene balls. Perhaps Turks best known work is a life size wax work of Turk dressed as Johnny Rotten in the pose of Elvis Presley. He later made a waxwork of himself as a person living on the streets, with filthy skin dressed in ragged clothes and shoes. A companion work is an empty sleeping bag.
!Turrell James.
American artist who makes large scale installations, either in museum, on selected outdoor sites, or to commission. Turrell combines architectural structures with natural or artificial light to create visual tricks that give the illusion of a depth or height that isn’t there, of to focus the viewers vision on the sky. An example is a gallery installation, which consists of a dark room in which there seems to be a red glowing wall panel, which seems to have some kind of activity on its surface. It is only when the viewer steps right up the apparent panel that it can be discerned that there is a rectangle panel cut out of the wall, behind which is a void which is lit with red light, and in fact there is nothing there. Another gallery installation is made by making window where no window is expected so that the viewer is fooled into thinking that there is a sky coloured panel on the ceiling when in fact it is the sky.
Turrell's outdoor installations are sometimes very large scale. His observatory is a sunken earth work at the top of a specially constructed mound that is designed to harness the changing light for that geographic location at all times of year to create effects fro the viewer inside the work. The viewer’s view of the sky is controlled by the structure of the space that Turrell has designed for the observatory.
!Tuymans Luc.
German painter born in the 1950s who makes a large number of paintings nearly always the same size about 80 by 50 cm. Tuymans’ uses a sparse palette of subdued colours, rarely mixing colours, but using a range of tones of one or two colours in each work. His paintings are very simple representations of everyday subjects such as the pattern on wallpaper, flowers, furniture and objects. He has been very influential for a generation of artists.
!Twombly Cy.
American artist born in the 1930s who makes semi abstract paintings which have a words added. Twombly’s work often looks as if he has been repeatedly scratching out errors, it is composed largely of gestural and apparently scrappy marks, nearly always on an off white ground. He uses pencil and black paint to make his very fine markings, adding tiny almost incidental blobs of colour. The overall impression is of a whitish painting covered with obsessive scribbling in parts. The paintings often have titles that refer to Greek myths or are in Latin. 
!Tyson Keith.
Britsih artist who makes installations and drawings. Tyson become known in the 1990s for his Artmachine, a sculpture that produced random artworks the description of which would be ejected from the machine on a card. Tyson would then construct the artwork. One installation consisted of a whole gallery filled by these art works linked by cables. The artworks usually juxtapose household, found objects and building materials in unlikely combinations and sometimes have a kinetic or sound feature. His interest in multiplication of ideas is always documented in his prolific drawing practise. Tyson produces drawings often with text which address any number of scientific or eccentric inventions, everyday objects, astronomy, geography, physics and chemistry. Typically these drawings are always the same size, 120cm by 80 cm, but Tyson also produces much larger paintings.
Tyson also produced a black monolithic sculpture which emits a low hum as if it is a powerful minimal futuristic machine of some sort.
!Udomski. 
Artist who makes large paintings up to 3 metres high by cutting out single letters from newspapers and magazines, sticking them onto the canvas and painting around them. The resulting dense pattern, perhaps of the letter ‘O’ in upper and lower case hundreds of times painted around with very dark brown paint, gives the impression of a pattern o dots.
!Uklansky Piotr.
Polish artist born in the 1960s living in America who works in a variety of media. 
One of Uklansky best known works is a nightclub dance floor made from squares of coloured plastic illuminated from below, which he has installed in a number of galleries and which is permanently installed in a bar in New York. Uklansky has made other floor works, including a puddle, for which an amount of water is poured onto the floor with a wall label notifying that this is the artist's work, and his skating drawing, black rubber marks made by the artists shoes when he ice skated on a gallery floor. Uklansky has an interest in the ephemeral and the unnoticed; an early work consisted of a number of printed colour advertising flyers, which in fact advertised nothing sensible, pushed through the door of the gallery and the surrounding area as if they were junk mail.
Uklanksy also makes photographic work. Nazis is about 40 head and shoulders photographs of actors playing Nazi soldiers in cinema films.
!Vallance, Jeffrey.
American artist born in the 1950s who makes installation work and writes extensivlye about art both as part of his practise and as a critic.
Vallance lives in Las Vegas where he has established The Liberace Museum to house a collection of memorabilia and artefacts related to the pianist. This is in part ironic and in part a genuine tribute.
Vallance travels widely and part of his practise is an ethnogrpahci investigation into the sacred objects of other cultures. Vallance has made a bofy of work that investigates the religious relic, making relics of his own and investigating existing ones. He made a Shroud by covering his face with coffee and imprinting it on a cloth. He shows that the images on the Turin Shround are in fact images of nuclear mushroom clouds and explains why he thinks this is the case. He adopts a frozen chicken, calls it Binky and shows that it can be resurrected after death inn the manner of Jesus. He researched in detail the wherabouts fo the variuos relics of nails and wooden shards of the True Cross and wrote about them.
Vallance travelled to Tonga. Before he left he corresponded with the King and asked him what gift he would like. The king requeseted a pair of size 13 snorkelling fins which Vallance provided. The correspondance is published.

!Van der Stokker Lily.
Dutch artist born in the 1950s who makes wall paintings, screen prints, and self-adhesive stickers. Van der Stokker’s paintings are usually made with accompanying especially made or upholstered furniture. The paintings consist of flowers and other decorative elements linked by curling lines, with a few words of text, for example ‘Lily is 43 years old and Jack is 62 years old’, which surrounds a two sweater sofa with a pale beige covering. Self-adhesive stickers might be designed to be applied to a wall where it meets the floor. A screen print has an orange green and white plaid pattern on which is a bunch of flowers with the words Thank You. Van der Stokker’s painting is usually in pastel and pretty colours, painted precisely by hand with hard edges. Any text is usually in a hand writing style of lettering.
!Verhoeven Julie.
Artist who makes pencil drawings of women with text that take the style fashion drawings. Verhoeven is also a fashion designer.
!Villiger Hannah.
artist who made a series of Polaroid photographs of her own naked body holding the camera at arms length. The resulting series of images (it took several to document the whole of her body in this way) are printed enlarged so that the parts of Villiger are life sized, and exhibited in a row. 
!Viola Bill.
America artist working in Video born in the 1950s who came to prominence in the 1990s. Viola marries an interest in the representation of the human form in sculpture with a fascination for and skill in video technology. early works include an arrangement of different sized monitors from the tiniest one inch square to the more usual domestic size, removed from their casings, strewn on floor or in on work on a shelf. On the monitors films of different parts of the artists body his ear, crook of arm, side of neck etc. as if the monitors represented a fragmented body, making a comment on the possibility for classical figurative sculpture in the age of video. Also a work installed in a corridor shaped room. When the viewer enters the room and walks down it they trigger back projected videos installed behind the false walls. Each one projects a person who appears to walk towards the viewer from the far distances, getting gradually larger until they are life size and stand, facing the viewer confronting them. Viola used non artist as models and asked them merely to confront the camera. The work has a spooky quality and leaves the viewer with an erroneous sense of control. 
Violas best known work is a triptych large screen video projection showing his partner giving birth to their child, his mother dying and himself apparently drowning underwater. Simultaneously projected with a breathing/water soundtrack, the work draws obvious parallels between birth and death, birth and being underwater, death and being underwater. This work has a powerful impact not least because it features an actual birth and death. The work hailed as a religious masterpiece fro the 20th century.
Continuing his investigation of the sculptural representation of the human from Viola has since gone on to make a series of works on smaller wall mounted plasma screens. These works take famous paintings often old masters or renaissance works and recreate them in modern settings using actors and highly controlled lighting. Although they are video and therefore exist in time they appear static and little happens, therefore they can be read as paintings. In 2003 Viola made a work titled The Passion.
Von Hellerman Sophie
British painter who makes loosely painted images of scenes or events that imply narrative, or appear to have a literary reference. Hellerman paints with great economy, using the minimum brushwork required to produce often oneric, hazy images. The figures in her paintings often appear as if they are on a stage beucase most of the canvas is left bare, the thin paint soaked into the canvas.
!van Lamsweerde Inez.
Belgian/Dutch artist makes digitally manipulated images, her works in the late 1990s a series of portraits. Drawing on her experience in fashion photography these are highly glossy stylised portraits which engender a sense of unease in the viewer. For example a beautiful blonde reclining in a swimsuit will have male arms, or a smiling male model will have female hands. The digital alteration is so skilful as to be initially unnoticed and imperceptible technically.
!Voorsanger Jessica.
British artist born in the late 1960s whose work is concerned with the mentality of the fan. Her video and photographic work often has a participatory or collaborative element – she may invite answers to questionnaires, or include peoples responses in her own work. Voorsanger has made a number of works about being a David Cassidy fan and about being a football fan. Voorsanger has a web site that is integral to her practise.
!Vostell Wolf.
German actionist/performance artist working in the 1980s, Vostell organised large public performances in German cities, often using pyrotechnics and explosives. His interventions into public life were deliberaltely terrorist in their execution, and were intended to be disruptive. The formal and sculptural qualities of his actions were as important as their political aspect. He released a large amount of smoke at a specific site and time. He led a group of people about a city ending in a derelict excavated site. His ambitious works were extensively documented and Vostell wrote in detail about each one. 
!Wall Jeff.
Makes very large scale photographic works, usually presented on light boxes. Wall remakes works from art history including Hokusai's ‘Wave’, interpreting the works into a modern setting. The wave is a group of people on a dyke casing paper blown into the air by the wind. He made a work showing dying and wounded men in mud of a battlefield. The works are recreated in the most painstaking manner to ensure absoluter apparent veracity And accuracy, and photographed in larger format to ensure the sharpest highest quality image. It is difficult to fault the quality of the production. 
!Wallinger Mark.
British artist born around 1960 who has made work in a wide range of media. Wallinger’s practise includes video, sculpture, photography and painting. There is a strong socio-historical aspect to his work and he is interested in football, horse racing and  religion.
Wallinger’s early works are mostly sculpture. He placed a table football game on dark green marbled plinth, made a tapestry of Munch’s patingn The Scream and made a work with a bird in a cage. Another football work is a photo of Wallinger in the crowd leaving Highbury, a London football ground, caryying a banner bearing some significant words. He made a series of full length portraits of his friends presented as if they were homeless people. 
One of Wallinger’s early videos was a comparison between the television coverage of the opening of racing at Ascot by the Queen on three successive years. This is played simulataneously on three separate monitors and shows that the camera angles, shots, and commentary varies very little. Wallinger has made many works about horse racing. He made a number of almost lifesize paintings of horses in the style of Stubbs in which each horse is seen in profile, standing with no saddle or bridle, on a dark green background, painted very realistically and composed from the front half of one parent and the rear half of the other, joined in the middle across the body. In the late 1990s Wallinger and a syndicate purchased and began to race a horse named A Real Work of Art. 
Wallinger has also made a number of works about religion. One featuers himself carrying a blind person’s white stick walking down an upwards escalator chanting the lords prayer which is played backwards. Wallinger contributed a proposal for the empty sculpture plinth in Trafalgar Square in London titled Ecce Homo, a one metre high statue of a naked man (who could be a chirist figure) made in white marble that stands on one corner of the plinth close to the edge looking outwards.
!Warren Rebecca.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculptures from clay, ceramic and bronze. Warren models human figures very roughly, using lumps of clay barely fashioned, the surface not smoothed at all. Figures are sometimes engaged in sexual acts. Female forms are large breasted and monumental and male forms have oversized penises. The clay is usually left unglazed, sometimes and the sculpture is presented on a plinth. They range in size from life-size down to table top size. Warren has made a series of large sculptures which are homages to both the cartoonist Robert Crumb and the painter Philip Guston (who was himself influenced by Crumb). Each one is a Crumb-style figure (with an enormously exaggerated bottom) combined with elements of the work of a famous artist from history.
Warren has also made works in bronze, sometimes consisting of no more than a lump or a phallic object.
!Wearing Gillian.
British artist born in the 1960s whose work in a variety of media deals with identity. Wearing’s earliest work to gain popularity is called Dancing In Peckham, a video of herself with a personal stereo dancing in the middle of a busy shopping centre. She also walked around her local streets with her entire head bandaged, noting the comments and attitudes that she attracted. 
Wearing’s work has been very influential, notably Signs That Say What You Want Them to Say, a video and an series of photographs for which Wearing asked people on the street to write down on a piece of paper that she gave them something that they would like to say but never had. She then photographed them holding the sign in front of them. The resulting contrast between the expressed desire or emotion and the authors face is subject of the work. Continuing her exploration of how people see themselves, Wearing made a video of herself in bed with a number of transsexuals and transvestites, and a single shot video of a local police force, standing in rows. The officers remain motionless and so does the camera, so that what is a moving image becomes almost a still image. Individual movements, blinking, and any tics assume the same importance as gesture and facial expression in their absence.
In 2003 Wearing made an number of photographs of herself dressed as members of her own family, using masks, prosthetics, wigs and make-up.
!Webb Gary.
British artist born in the 1970s who makes abstract sculptures from a variety of building and fabrication materials, plastic, acrylic, blown glass, laminate, rubber, metal and fibreglass. Webb’s sculptures sometimes have an audio addition, piece of music, sound or a song emanating from part of the sculpture. Webb draws on the legacy of 1960s and 70s sculptors like Antony Caro and David Smith, but his choice of materials is fundamentally different since he chooses more fragile or delicate materials. An early work consists of a small wood veneered unit on legs with music coming from it and a plume of wood veneer. Another consist of an amorphous giant pebble form lacquered in bright yellow high gloss paint with various other elements. Webb used representative objects in some of his early work, such as a black skinned female doll attached to a sphere and decorated. There are often references to furniture, for example the coffee table is a favourite form. Sometimes a specially made blown glass element is added. Webb has made moulded rubber floor tiles as part of another sculpture. Webb’s sculptures have the look of giant three dimensional collages of abstract elements that are brought together formally in the manner of traditional sculpture but do not share this appearance.
!Wentworth Richard.
Britsih artist born in the 1950s who makes sculpture and photographic work. Wentworth observes the everyday life of and use objects and makes sculptures that observe the way in which one thing can be used for another. He uses aluminium buckets, tools, ladders and rope a lot. One work made in the 1980s is called Sinking of the Belgrano and consists of a model battleship in an aluminium bucket on a false surface. Antoher is a book with a large number of sweet wrappers inserted between the pages in an exaggeration fo the potential use of one as a bookmark.
For twenty years Wentworth has been photographing everyday the area around Caledonian Road in London where he lives. He has a particular interest in the interaction of handcraft, artisanship and make do and mend with the built environment. This collection includes documentation fo changing shop fronts and names, appropriate, unusual or inappropriate uses for street furniture, railings and doorways, graffiti, litter and makeshift solutions to broken objects and parts of buildings. Wentworth has also made a large collection fo photographs of the way one object is used for a new purpose, for example a saucer wedged in to keep a window open.
!Wermers Nicole.
German artist born around 1970 who makes collages.
!West Franz.
German artist who makes sculpture and installation and works on paper. West’s work generally features furniture, papier maché timber and plaster or bronze ‘lumps’ and painted collage. A sausage, turd or penis form appears frequently in his work whether made in papier maché, cast in bronze or painted into collages. 
West has a democratic approach to art, believing that an art objects value is created as much by the viewing subject as by the artist. To this end most of his sculpture is made to be touched or sat on. 
West is probably best known for his interactive sculptures. These are ungainly white plaster objects, organic amorphous white shapes that sit awkwardly on a plinth and look like joke sculptures. The viewer is instructed on the label accompanying the work to pick up the object and interact with it. There may be a video monitor showing a number of people doing this. A curtained off area with floor to ceiling mirror may be provided, and the viewer invited to enter with the sculpture and interact with it. Some of the sculptures have wire extrusions enabling them to be ‘worn’, or areas which allow then to be balanced on a shoulder. 
The interactive sculptures each have a specific plinth, which may incorporate a mirror, or a seat and in on case a bed on which the viewer can lie with the sculpture. 
West has made a large number of items of furniture, on which the viewer is free to sit or lounge. In particular he makes chairs and sofas, simple square shapes with unconventionally deep and wide seats, from unfinished steel concrete reinforcing rods with thin cushions upholstered in utilitarian materials such as canvas, sacking and silver insulation fabric. The furniture is sometimes placed in direct relation to a sculpture, for example a chair with a Hessian cushion is placed near to a pink papier maché lump which is hung from the ceiling. Or a pair of chairs with silver cushions are placed with a one and a half meter high sphere covered with brown parcel tape between them.
The furniture is designed to involve the viewer physically in West’s work, to provoke thoughts about the idea of contemplation fo art works.
Through his large number of lump sculptures West investigates the idea of the autonomous and intrinsically valuable art objects. He create essentially lumpen abstract objects that could be said to be ugly and presents then in the same way that any sculpture would be shown. The lumps range in size from around 50cm high to 2 metres high and more. They are made in papier maché on a plaster and timber frame work, sometimes with a gap left through which the inside can be viewed, and painted. They are also made in bronze, cast from papier maché originals and painted often in pink or pale blue, sometimes multicoloured. The forms of the lumps seem arbitrary although West has made a series fo lump portrait heads.
The phallic object which West deploys in his work as sausage, penis or turd appears as sculpture made from papier maché, bronze or resin cast, or fabricated in steel (sheet steel cut in pieces and welded together in a patchwork) and painted again in colours that seem inappropriate to the form and material such as pale pink and blue, ochre or gold. West has made sculpture in this form in sizes ranging from 30 cm long to 20 metres long outdoor sculpture. 
The phallic shape appears in West’s collages frequently. These consist of magazine cut-outs usually of men and women, which are painted over in poster paint to alter their body shape. They often feature half obscured sexual acts. West adds sausages cut from magazine advertisements emerging from a man or woman’s mouth, or from a man’s trousers. 
!Westwood, Martin.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes mixed media wall work using collage, drawing, painting and papier maché. Westwood often makes silhouette cut outs and uses spray paint over these or through doilies to create shadow effects. In general his work is wall based, but he has also made installations, for example one in which he hung a number of papier maché forms made over balloons, which he had painted, collaged and drawn on. Westwood often chooses unconventional paper for his drawings, such as newsprint, or printed material.  
!Whiteread Rachel.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes large scale sculpture. Whiteread casts the interior and the underneath of buildings, rooms and furniture in concrete, plaster, rubber and resin, making a solid form from negative space. 
She began by casting mattresses in rubber and translucent silicon around 1990. these solid forms were placed on the floor half bent to lean against the wall. She also cast a bath, producing a solid block with a negative bath shape set into it which looks like a sarcophagus, a basin done in the same way, and some tables and desks which are simply the solid blocks produced from the space under a table.
Whiteread’s first major work is Ghost, 1993, a cast fo a room in a house. This work appears as a life sized plaster block (made in sections) which has the impressions fo the details of the room on its surface, such as a fireplace which projects fro the surface instead of being cut into the wall, door and window frames which are negatives set into the surface, door handles, skirting board and light switches.
Whiteread continued to make smaller objects, and in 1997 she made House, her best known work and one which became notorious.
House was the cast of the interior of a two storey Victorian terraced house in the east end of London. The cast was made by spraying the interior with concrete and reinforcing it, then demolishing the exterior carefully. The row of houses was condemned and was to be demolished to make way for a new park so Whiteread’s work was to be temporary. However it was so popular that there were calls for it to remain as a permanent public sculpture in the park. There ensued a long public battle between arts bodies and the local authority with press and local residents both for and against the sculpture. The sculpture was finally demolished.
Whiteread was commissioned to make a holocaust memorial for Vienna. Her cast of the interior of a library sited in a pedestrian square was finally unveiled in 2001 after several years of wrangling between commissioning bodies, public bodies and the authorities. The library is a cement block with the spines of books covering it. These ‘books’ are removable, each representing a person from that city who died in the holocaust. 
Whiteread has continued to cast furniture throughout her career. She began to use acrylic resin at the end of the 1990s, casting the spaces underneath a classroom full of chairs, for example.  
!Wiener, Lawrence.
American artist bron in in the 1940s who is an influential conceptual artist working exclusivbley with text. Wiener made a replica of the Rosetta stone that is ten metres across. He made a great number of gallery installations that consist of transfer lettering applied to the wall to his specification, presentign a text that he has written, much or all of which is often crossed through. He also made typed and printed work on paper and paintings of text. Wiener’s work was an investigaton into the nature of language and specifically into the written word, taking the post-structuralist debate about language as a system of signs and signifiers and made it the subject of his work. In Wiener’s work the meaning of the words, the action fo reading by the viewer and the visual presecne of the words combine to make a work of art.
!Wilding Alison.
Britsih artist who makes sculptures from transparent acrylic sheet, slotted together to form complex three dimensional grid structures that take advantage fo the way that the edge of the acrylic can be seen as lines in space. Wilding’s sculptures are solid forms that give the impression of being light and delicate. She also makes working drawings which are in effect technical perspective drawings for the sculptures. 
!Wilkes, Cathy.
Scottish artist born in the 1960s who uses found objects which she justaposes to make allusive sculputral works. One work uses a campbeds and a man’s tie, another a small ornament on a shelf. There is an solitary and pensive qulatity to Wilkes’ work.
!Willats Stephen.
Britsih artist born in the 1950s who makes installation, text based and graphic work. Willats has also self-published a number of books and pamphlets. Willats investigates how people live from a social, political and geographical perspective, employing survey questionnaires, plans and charts, statistics and photography. He has produced a magazine for a number years. An exhibition of his work would typically feature photographs and newspaper clippings with accompanying text and detailed captions, posters printed by him, often with painted or drawn additions, video and a publication.
!Williams Kyffin.
British artist born in the 1930s who spent his life painting the welsh landscape. Williams’ impressionistic work is made with a very thick impasto, mostly applying paint with a palette knife to create a loose gestural interpretation fo what he sees. He uses bright colours as well as a natural earth palette. The paintings are rarely more than one metre high.
!Williams Sue.
American painter born around 1960 who makes figurative painting. Her best known work is from the 1980s when she was known as one fo a group of women artists described as Bad Girls for their strongly feminist, aggressive and angry work. 
Pro Porn and Anti Porn, a painting from this period, shows a little girls head prone under a kneeling man’s backside, his face invisible as he is seen from the rear with his erect penis and scrotum in full view. There is other similarly explicit drawing open the canvas together with text which sets out the tone fo William’s work. In this case the work considers child abuse and male sexuality. The outline drawings in the paining is done in black and grey on a yellow ground. 
!Williamson Erland.
Born in 1965 Scottish artist Williamson made socially engaged work, before he died in 1995. Williamson customised a van that he used to take the road show that he created to many different places. The van contained a collapsible kiosk which the artist would set up and open as a kind of drop in centre, he might for example set it up on a housing estate and ask residents if they wished to fill in questionnaires about where they lived, have a cup of tea, make flyers and posters themselves. He acted as a grass roots activist with the intention fo encouraging people to mobilise for change through his work. His work, the van and the kiosk has a strongly homemade aesthetic, hand painted signage on the van and kiosk done in an amateur style in gloss paint on hardboard, hand printed posters and flyers, a heavy dependence on the photocopier, and even t-shirts for sale printed with his own slogans. 
!Wilshire Hermione.
British artist born in the 1950s who makes installations and sculpture. Wilshire has often worked with glass. She blows large globules and droplets of glass and places them over photographs of parts fo the human body which are then viewed through the glass, magnified and glowing. One work consisted of glass droplets extruded into stalks which were fixed projecting form the wall. On the bottom of these were fixed photographs of finger prints, as if these had been left in the air. Another consisted of a large number of variable sized glass pebbles on the underneath of which were attached photographs fo penises. These were arranged on the floor as if they had been scattered in a allusion to ejaculation.
!Wilson Jane and Louise.
British artist sisters born in the late 1960s who make large scale projected video installations. 
An early work from the beginning of the 1990s involved them filming themselves under hypnosis to see if they were telepathic because they are twins. At this time they also made a series fo photographs showing parts fo their bodies in empty offices, which were printed in a square format and hung at odd levels in the gallery relating to the architecture of the space. These photographs were an investigation into the nature of deserted space and the human traces left in them by default.
The Wilson’s then began to make the work for which they are known, which involves the seledtion of a location, which is invariably an empty building with a specific purpose or history, for exampel Greenham Common nuclear base, a law court, and a casino. They film these empty spaces and add a soundtrack. Sometimes the film features the artists themselves, more recently there are no people in the work.
The films are multi-screen projections, often shown on adjacent walls so that they meet in a corner. The consequent interaction of the moving images is controlled and deliberate, creating a multiple view of the same space and refusing the viewer a fixed viewpoint. In some works the screens are placed at angles, freestanding, with projections on front and back. The Wilsons make free use of mirroring and doubling in their work, often seen as an obvious consequence of the fact that they are twins. They make run the same film both backwards and forwards so the that the images create a symmetrical pattern. The  way the films are edited often leads to an abstract patterning effect.
!Wilson Richard.
British artist who is best known for a work that was permanently installed in the Saatchi gallery in London. This consists of a steel tank which is waist height. The tank fills the room but there is a narrow projection into the tank allowing viewers to walk into the middle. The tank is apparently filled with black oil. Although in fact the oil is no more that 10 cm deep, its opacity, viscosity, slight movement and reflective ness create an unnerving feeling of vulnerability and disorientation in the viewer, paradoxically coupled with an atmosphere of contemplation.
!Wilson TJ.
American artist who makes video using found footage, his own footage and often uses subtitles. He has made a film about a roman villa and another about a baby elephant and its mother, the subtitles for these are not intrinsic to the images
!Winstanley Paul.
British artist who makes paintings fo shadows cast by window frames, the structural elements fo roofs, usually in black and white, the lines softened, the image becoming abstract.
!Wood Richard.
Britsih artist born in the 1960s who made a series of sculptures by arranged coloured plastic storage baskets, trays and other items and then melting them with a blow torch so that they meld together, colours mingling and are transformed into spiked and dripped structures. Woods also made a cast of a rolled carpet rug and painted it to resemble the real carpet. 
!Woodrow Bill. 
Britsih artist born in the 1950s who made installations from collections of waste plastic bottles and containers, arranged by colour and shape on the wall or floor to create an image. Woodrow also made a series of works using discarded refrigerators, washing machines. He cut the form of an object for example a guitar from the metal walls of a washing machine and by bending and folding the metal coaxed it into a convincing representation which remains attached to the washing machine. He also fashioned a broken umbrella into a crow. 
!Wood and Harrison. 
Britsih artist duo bron in the 1960s who make performance videos in which they use their own bodies to create physical sculptures. The artist rely on the weight and motion of their bodies and use each other as counterbalances in a series of interactions with simple props such as planks, boxes and sheets of ply. They create ramps, fulcrums, towers and execute sharply performed and choreographed manoeuvres that exhaustively explore interaction of human body with flat plane and with three dimensional object. The videos are shown on monitors, often each monitor shows a single work looped. The ambient sound of Wood and Harrison’s activities is often loud as sheets of timber are walked on, dropped for example. Wood and Harrison aim to apply the principles of minimal sculpture to live art, reminiscent of Robert Morris’s activity in Olympia with Carolee Schneeman and the early video work fo Richard Serra and Vito Acconci.
!Woods Richard.
British artist bron in the 1960s who makes wall paintings consisting of abstract patterned elements positioned in unconventional places, perhaps high up, across a corner, along the floor, or in some place that relates to the architecture of the building. The works sometimes cover a wall, but are more usually quite small, around one metre high or wide. The designs have many references: historical architectural decoration, tattoos, textile designs, architectural drawing and typography for example but Woods refines them until the references all but disappear into his sharp edged, precise, often monotone works. The works are hand painted in black, or one or two of the primary colours. 
!Wool Christopher.
American artist who makes large abstract paintings on a heavy gauge woven paper which are mainly printed, often using fragments of found patterns, with areas of paint added, predominantly in black and white.
!Wright Elizabeth.
British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture and installation that is concerned with the relative size fo objects. In the mid 1990s Wright made an installation which consisted of a floor strewn with over-sized cigarette butts about 40 cm long, and Marlboro packets about 30 cm long. Both objects larger that life size but the wrong size in relation to each other. The objects are made from cardboard and paper which is painted to look realistic. 
Wright also made scaled down telephone directories which are actually printed and bound, supermarket carrier bags and crisp packets also printed, as well as a bicycle one third larger than life size. 
!Wyn Evans Cerith. 
Welsh artist born in the 1950s who uses a variety fo media. Wyn Evans’ work often includes or is based on literary or philosophical text. Many of his works are subtle alterations of a pre-existing object in order to transform the everyday into the sublime. For example his neon exit sign in which the word is written backwards, his metal crowd barrier which is covered in gold leaf. 
Some works of Wyn Evans works are very simple in execution, for example a large circular convex mirror and a transparent chandelier. A luminous painting to read by is a 50 cm high canvas coated with luminous paint, to be hung at eh head of a bed, which is charged with enough light during the day to provide reading light at night. Sometimes his work takes the form of an unobtrusive site specific intervention, for example altering the pitch of some bells strung across a stairway to bar public access in the John Soane museum in London. 
Much of Wyn Evans’ early work is 16mm film. His film about the death of the director Pasolini shows a group of small boys playing on a beach, as behind them a text, a quotation from Pasolini, outlined in fireworks on a specially constructed frame ignites, blazes and burns away. He ahs also use neon text running round a light fitting, a phrase that reads continuously with no end. Wyn Evans has made a number of neon works using text that reads in a loop, or can be read forwards and backwards, as well as small neon arabesque shapes. He has also made text etched onto Perspex panels that hang from the ceiling. This use of quotation is given its most complex rendering in a work from 2001. Wyn Evans hung a mirror ball in a gallery and projected a bright light at it. The light flashes on and off in a Morse code pattern, spelling out a quotation from Oscar Wilde. 
In 2003 Wyn Evans made a work consisting of several crystal chandeliers the bulbs of each flashing the Morse code for a particular extract from a philosophical or literary text.
!Yong Ping Huang.
Chinese artist working from the 1980s onwards who made a sculpture by putting books in a washing machine and moulding the resulting pulp into a sphere. He also created a number of outdoor pyrotechnic events.
!Ziranek Sylvia.
Britsih performance artist who made a number of works in the 1980s and has also published written work. Ziranek's invented persona is central to her work and this is carried through into her appearance – she always dresses in bright pink and often has pink hair.
!Zittel Andrea.
American artist born in the 1960s who makes functional sculptures. Zittel's series of portable living units includes a kitchen, a bedroom and a lounging area. The units are made so that they can be folded away and easily transported. They are made to a high specification with polished wood exteriors and upholstered interior with special fittings (lights, drop down tables, cupboards, handles, mirrors etc.) The lounging area is a purple upholstered sunken seating area, the walls of the case functioning as the walls that enclose it. The kitchen is fully functional if minimal.
Zittel has also designed a number of items of clothing and has made a site specific work in the desert near to where she lives
